One of the resonators is strongly coupled, with coupling strength g, to the spin ensemble with resonance frequency ∆. **Summary.** The paper investigates strong light-matter interactions in a complex hybrid system combining three superconducting microwave cavities and an electron spin ensemble. By modeling the system using input-output theory, the authors demonstrate that the multi-mode nature allows for the formation of dark states. These engineered dark states are crucial because they can protect quantum information stored in the spin ensemble by suppressing unwanted coupling to the surrounding environment.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes the boundaries of cavity QED by integrating multiple coupled resonators with solid-state spins. The ability to engineer protected quantum states (dark states) via environmental coupling control is a key theme in modern open quantum systems research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research explores how controlled coupling of multiple superconducting microwave cavities to an electron spin ensemble can generate signatures of strong coupling and engineer dark states.

**Main result.** The study confirms the existence of a multi-mode strong-coupling regime and observes spectral dips indicative of dark state formation, which suppresses coupling to the measurement environment.

**Method.** The system is modeled using an input-output formalism derived from a complex Hamiltonian, with experimental verification through continuous wave electron spin resonance (cwESR) measurements.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a hybrid microwave circuit comprising three superconducting resonators coupled together and interacting with a paramagnetic electron spin ensemble. The model incorporates terms for cavity-cavity, spin dynamics, spin-cavity coupling (Tavis-Cummings type), and coupling to the external transmission line.

**Key observables.** Complex microwave transmission $S_{21}$ as a function of probe frequency ($\omega_{	ext{probe}}$) and static magnetic field ($B_0$); Rabi splittings; suppressed absorption dips.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Resonator frequencies (e.g., 5.753 GHz, 5.864 GHz, 5.873 GHz); inter-cavity coupling ($\zeta_{23}/(2\pi) = 6.0 	ext{ MHz}$); Cooperativity parameters ($C_\pm$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes the single-excitation limit and neglects sub-ensemble distribution of spin resonance frequencies; some theoretical analyses assume point-like coupling to the transmission line.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 conceptually shows the setup measuring $S_{21}$. Figure 2 compares measured vs. simulated microwave absorption amplitudes $|S_{21}|$ across varying magnetic fields, while Figure 8 and 9 analyze the theoretical conditions for dark state formation based on coupling parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the multi-cavity hybrid system Hamiltonian to applying the input-output formalism for spectral analysis. It details observations of field-independent modes, strong coupling splittings, and finally analyzes the specific conditions under which a stationary dark state can form by suppressing radiation into the measurement channel.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spin ensembles are considered as potential candidates for quantum memory and quantum enhanced sensing applications. Here, we explore the controlled coupling of multiple superconducting microwave cavities to a spin ensemble, which shows signatures of strong coupling and, due to the multi-mode character, the formation of dark states. In particular, the latter are of interest, as they provide a potential pathway to enhance memory times and enable protected storage of non-classical states in spin ensembles due to the suppressed coupling to the circuit environment. We model the spin multi-cavity hybrid to reproduce the spectra and extract characteristic coupling strengths using the input-output formalism.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper demonstrates how to use genuine qutrit entanglement in an optical atomic clock platform to simultaneously estimate multiple physical parameters. By leveraging the higher dimensionality of the internal atomic states, they surpass the precision limits achievable by treating each parameter estimation independently. This work establishes key building blocks for quantum information science using neutral atoms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly pushes the boundaries of quantum sensing by moving beyond qubit limitations into high-dimensional qudit systems using neutral atoms, which is a major focus area in AMO physics and quantum metrology.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research addresses extending quantum metrology from single-parameter estimation (using qubits) to joint multi-parameter estimation using entangled multi-level qudits in an atomic clock platform.

**Main result.** They experimentally demonstrated genuine qutrit entanglement and achieved a joint estimation variance lower than the ideal individual two-level sensing threshold, showing this advantage persists under noise.

**Method.** The authors used controlled operations (like CZ gates) to generate high-dimensional entangled states in $^{88}	ext{Sr}$ atoms and then employed optimal metrological probes and readout circuits for joint phase estimation.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an optical clock platform using $^{88}	ext{Sr}$ atoms trapped in triple-magic optical tweezers. The internal energy levels form a qutrit ($\{|0angle, |1angle, |2angle\}$), allowing for the encoding of higher-dimensional quantum information.

**Key observables.** Joint estimation variance ($Y_{	ext{joint}}$), entanglement fidelity ($F$), and comparison against individual two-level sensing thresholds.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of atoms ($N$), the injected phases ($	heta_1, 	heta_2$), and noise parameters (e.g., coherence factors $\eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The fidelity calculation is not fully corrected for all state preparation and measurement errors; theoretical analysis often assumes specific noise models (depolarizing/dephasing).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup, demonstrate entanglement via population measurements ($P_{ii}$) and parity oscillations ($	ext{C}_{ij}$), and compare joint vs. individual estimation variances under ideal and noisy conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical platform and generating qutrit entanglement to theoretically constructing optimal multi-parameter probes, experimentally realizing the joint estimation, and finally analyzing noise robustness across different theoretical limits.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum metrology harnesses entanglement to improve measurement precision beyond classical limits. While standard protocols rely on two-level qubits to estimate a single parameter, extending them to entangled multi-level qudits enables the optimal simultaneous estimation of multiple parameters within a single probe. However, generating such multi-level entanglement and harnessing it for joint multi-parameter estimation in atomic clocks has remained an outstanding challenge. Here, we experimentally demonstrate genuine qutrit entanglement and joint multi-parameter estimation in an optical clock platform. Leveraging control over the ground state and two fine-structure clock states of $^{88}\text{Sr}$ atoms trapped in triple-magic optical tweezers, we generate a maximally entangled two-qutrit state with a loss-postselected fidelity of F = 0.85(1), certifying genuine multi-level entanglement. Taking advantage of this high-dimensional entanglement, we theoretically construct and experimentally realize an optimal two-qutrit metrological probe state and noise-robust readout circuit to simultaneously estimate injected phases on two optical clock transitions. We observe a joint estimation variance below the ideal individual two-level sensing threshold, and show theoretically that this advantage persists at state-of-the-art atom numbers under circuit-level noise. These results demonstrate the key building blocks towards quantum information science with high-dimensional states encoded in the internal energy levels of neutral atoms.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper analyzes dynamical phase transitions in a model of strongly interacting Rydberg ions coupled to phonons. Using mean-field theory and advanced quantum diagnostics like the Loschmidt echo, the authors map out complex phase diagrams influenced by ion interactions. The findings demonstrate that these interactions drastically alter both the equilibrium phases and the non-equilibrium relaxation dynamics of the system.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it combines trapped ion platforms (a key AMO system) with complex open quantum dynamics and many-body physics concepts like DPTs, making it a rich area for theoretical investigation in quantum optics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates dynamical phase transitions (DPTs) occurring in a generalized dissipative Dicke model.

**Main result.** The analysis reveals that Rydberg ion interactions significantly modify the mean-field phase diagram, leading to distinct phases and the emergence of tricritical points. Furthermore, non-equilibrium dynamics exhibit signatures like slow relaxation near these transitions.

**Method.** The study employs a combination of Mean-Field (MF) analysis for steady states and quantum diagnostics like the Loschmidt echo and entropy growth for finite-size, non-equilibrium dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system is trapped Rydberg ions arranged in a linear array, coupled to external vibrational modes (phonons). The dynamics are governed by a Master equation incorporating Rabi coupling, spin-phonon interaction, laser driving, and dissipation.

**Key observables.** Spin average ($\langle \sigma_j angle$), phonon averages ($X, P$), Von Neumann entropy, Loschmidt echo, and steady-state polarization ($S_z$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rydberg interaction strength ($V_{jl}$), spin-phonon coupling ($g$), Rabi frequency ($\Omega$), dissipation rate ($\gamma$), and detuning ($\Delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on Mean-Field approximations, including product approximations for coupled spin and bosonic operators.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows the ion chain setup. Figure 2 presents MF phase diagrams for different Dicke model regimes (closed, dissipative, generalized). Figure 3 illustrates system dynamics following parameter quenches.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the Hamiltonian and Master equation governing the system. It then analyzes steady states using Mean-Field theory to map out the phase diagram, identifying critical points. Finally, it investigates non-equilibrium quantum dynamics using diagnostics like the Loschmidt echo to characterize relaxation pathways.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study dynamical phase transitions in the generalized dissipative Dicke model in an array of trapped Rydberg ions, where their density-density interactions compete with the collective spin-phonon coupling, laser driving and dissipation. This setting offers a versatile approach to study equilibrium as well as non-equilibrium many-body phenomena, as parameters, such as the Ising interaction, laser-ion and spin-phonon coupling can be tuned. Through analyzing the mean-field phase diagram, we find a variety of distinct phases and the emergence of a tricritical point that are sensitively dependent of the interaction between Rydberg ions. We then study the quantum dynamics for a finite system size and characterize parameter dependent dynamics using the spin average, entropy, and Loschmidt echo. Distinctive signatures of the dynamical phases, such as slow relaxation and metastability, arise near the phase transition. This analysis predicts rich quantum dynamics of the finite system that link to the non-equilibrium mean-field phases. Our study widens the exploration of collective and non-equilibrium phases in Dicke models, and reveals that the Rydberg ion interaction drastically affects the phase diagram and dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theory detailing how local measurements perturb the critical behavior of gapless quantum matter like Tomonaga-Luttinger liquids. Using replica calculations, the authors show that this measurement-induced randomness causes correlation functions to decay with specific power laws featuring logarithmic corrections. This establishes a new regime of criticality beyond standard theoretical models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the non-trivial interplay between quantum measurements and fundamental critical phenomena, providing a quantitative framework for how measurement backaction modifies universal properties in 1D systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The theory investigates how local measurements perturb and alter the universal long-range correlations of gapless quantum matter, specifically Tomonaga-Luttinger liquids.

**Main result.** Average correlators decay with universal power laws accompanied by logarithmic corrections when measurements are relevant, a signature distinct from both forced measurements and conventional critical scaling.

**Method.** The authors employ replica instanton calculations to handle the intrinsic randomness of measurement records, complementing this with matrix-product-state calculations for verification.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the Tomonaga-Luttinger liquid (TLL), a 1D quantum critical state described by a continuum field theory derived from lattice Hamiltonians. The analysis focuses on weakly monitoring these systems.

**Key observables.** Connected density and phase fluctuation correlators ($\langle\hat{n}(x)\hat{n}(0)angle$, $\langle e^{i[	heta(x)-	heta(0)]} angle$), and the full multifractal spectrum of correlation moments.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Luttinger parameter ($K$) dictates the algebraic decay, while the measurement strength ($\gamma$) controls the perturbation regime; the focus is on $K < 1/2$ where perturbations are relevant.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the low-energy physics can be described by 1+1d Conformal Field Theories (CFTs) and utilizes the replica trick to average over measurement outcomes.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare theoretical predictions for density and phase correlators under weak measurements against those from forced measurements, illustrating the unique decay behavior induced by randomness.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a general theory of measurement-altered criticality, applying it first to TLLs using replica methods to calculate Born-averaged observables. It contrasts these results with standard critical scaling and provides numerical support via MPS calculations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Local measurements can alter long-range correlations in gapless quantum matter. We propose a theory of weakly-monitored Tomonaga-Luttinger liquids, a broad class of quantum critical states in one dimension. In order to address the intrinsic randomness of the measurement record, we develop a replica instanton calculation to study Born-averaged observables. We find that when measurements are relevant, average correlators of density and phase fluctuations decay at long distances as universal power laws with logarithmic corrections, a feature we argue is peculiar to measurement-induced randomness. We characterize the full multifractal spectrum of moments of correlations functions, revealing broad, strongly non-gaussian fluctuations across the ensemble of post-measurement states. We support these analytic results with matrix-product-state calculations, and provide a general picture of measurement-altered criticality for ground states described by 1+1d conformal field theories. Our results establish that physical measurements alter critical quantum states in a manner that lies beyond both forced measurements and conventional critical scaling.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes how interference patterns in the readout statistics of a meter used in a weak measurement can reveal information about the system's underlying quantum properties. By relating interference terms in the post-selection probability to fluctuations in the meter reading, the authors demonstrate that these statistical details allow for the objective determination of weak values. This provides a rigorous framework for interpreting measurements where standard assumptions about classical statistics break down.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep theoretical understanding of measurement contextuality and weak measurements, which is highly relevant for quantum optics and open quantum systems where continuous monitoring or weak coupling is central to the physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how quantum interference effects, specifically those arising from meter readout statistics, can reveal fundamental details about weak measurements.

**Main result.** It establishes a universal relationship between quantum interference in the post-selection probability and the interference observed in the meter readout statistics. This analysis shows that these interference terms correct for measurement fluctuations, allowing objective values to be determined by weak values.

**Method.** The authors perform a careful theoretical analysis of the quantum dynamics of an interacting system (the observable) and a continuous variable meter during post-selection. They utilize quasi-probability distributions to track interference effects.

**Model / system.** The model involves a general weak measurement setup where a system observable $\hat{A}$ interacts unitarily with a meter whose position is continuously measured. The analysis focuses on the resulting joint probability distribution $P(f, x)$ after post-selection ($f$).

**Key observables.** Quantum interference in the post-selection probability; quantum interference in the meter readout statistics; weak values ($\langle A \rangle_w$); conditional fluctuations (e.g., Ozawa-Hall uncertainty).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Measurement strength ($s$), which dictates whether the measurement is weak or projective.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that quantum interference effects are measurable in the meter readout statistics and that these statistics provide more information than just conditional averages.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general framework by analyzing the joint probability of post-selection and meter readout. It then details how interference terms modify both the average value and, crucially, the conditional fluctuations (variance) observed in the meter.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

It is widely assumed that the quantum fluctuations of the meter readout in a weak measurement make it impossible to identify the contributions originating from the individual values of the physical property observed in the measurement. Here, we show that a careful analysis of the quantum dynamics of the meter system allows us to identify a universal relation between quantum interference in the post-selection probability, and quantum interference in the statistics of the meter readout. The analysis reveals that quantum interference modifies the readout distribution of the meter in two ways, a diffusion term that identifies the appropriate operator ordering in the post-selected variance of the observed system property, and a wavefunction-dependent update of the initial meter statistics based on the effect of back action on the post-selection probability. Our results show that the statistical patterns described by meter interference provide important details about the physics of post-selection in weak measurements, allowing us to exclude the possibility of statistical artefacts in the experimental observation of weak values.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a novel 'unidirectional rescue' mechanism for quantum energy transport in coupled emitter arrays trapped in dark states. This process utilizes non-Condon exciton-phonon coupling to create an irreversible escape route into the radiative channel. The key finding is that this rescue rate maintains near-unity efficiency independent of system size, significantly outperforming standard dephasing mechanisms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses open quantum systems dynamics in cavity QED, providing a concrete, engineered pathway to enhance energy transfer that circumvents fundamental limitations imposed by system geometry (size scaling).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is overcoming energy trapping within optically dark states that form due to strong light-matter coupling in cavity arrays.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate a unidirectional 'rescue' mechanism, mediated by non-Condon exciton-phonon coupling, which efficiently channels population out of the dark manifold into the radiative channel regardless of system size.

**Method.** The dynamics are modeled using a Lindblad master equation approach, comparing the rescue rate against standard dephasing mechanisms through detailed numerical simulations and theoretical analysis.

**Model / system.** The system is an array of coupled two-level emitters interacting with a single optical cavity (polaritonic system) and coupled to an exciton-phonon bath. The dynamics are governed by the Lindblad equation incorporating multiple dissipation channels.

**Key observables.** Transport efficiency ($\eta$), dark state decay rate, transient bright peak signature in photon emission, and size scaling of efficiency ($\eta$ vs $N$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size ($N$), temperature ($k_B T/\Delta$), coupling strengths (e.g., cavity-drain rates), and the ratio of rescue to dephasing rates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on linear order system-bath coupling and the rotating-wave approximation, though unidirectionality is robustly shown even beyond these limits at T=0.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare dynamics under pure rescue versus pure dephasing, showing size-independent high efficiency for rescue while dephasing shows a size-dependent deficit. They also map the competition between rates as a function of temperature and rate ratios.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem (dark state trapping), introduces the unidirectional rescue mechanism via non-Condon coupling, provides quantitative evidence through size-scaling tests comparing rescue vs. dephasing efficiency, and concludes by discussing implications for quantum control.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Strong light-matter coupling in optical microcavities can transport energy ballistically across an emitter array, but the same coupling buries most of the excitation in a manifold of dark states that grows with system size and traps energy outside the transport channel. We show that the off-diagonal (non-Condon) part of the exciton-phonon coupling opens a one-way escape route from this trap, driving population irreversibly from dark states into the radiative channel. This rate is fixed by a photonic-weight conservation law rather than by dark-bright overlap which evacuates the dark manifold at a rate independent of system size. The mechanism contributes to transport with near-complete efficiency with four signatures being single exponential dark state decay, a size scaling efficiency gap, distinct temperature behavior, and a resonance in the escape rate at vibrational bath modes. Beyond polariton transport, it recasts dark states from a parasitic loss channel into an engineered dissipative resource, with implications for light harvesting and dissipation based quantum control.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper demonstrates a method to generate highly entangled two-mode Schrödinger cat states in cavity optomechanics by carefully controlling the system's dissipation. By tuning the phase of an interaction term ($	heta$), researchers can maximize bipartite entanglement between mechanical modes, proving that engineered environmental coupling is a powerful tool for quantum state preparation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work exemplifies advanced reservoir engineering by showing how controlled dissipation can stabilize non-Gaussian entangled states (cat states) in mechanical oscillators, a key area for quantum sensing and computation using macroscopic quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates generating two-mechanical mode entangled Schrödinger cat states (SCS) and characterizing steady-state entanglement in a cavity optomechanical system subject to environmental dissipation.

**Main result.** The phase of the phonon-hopping interaction ($	heta$) can be tuned to control bipartite entanglement, achieving maximum entanglement at intermediate phases like $	heta=\pi/2$, even when considering thermal excitations.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving an effective master equation for the mechanical modes via adiabatic elimination of the cavity field and quantifying entanglement using logarithmic negativity ($\mathcal{E}_N$).

**Model / system.** The system is a hybrid cavity optomechanical platform with one cavity mode coupled to two spectrally identical, interacting mechanical oscillators. The dynamics are governed by a master equation incorporating damping from both the cavity and mechanical baths.

**Key observables.** Bipartite entanglement (quantified by logarithmic negativity $\mathcal{E}_N$), Schrödinger cat state formation, and steady-state coherence ($c(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The phase of the phonon-hopping interaction ($	heta$), mechanical damping rates ($\Gamma$), cavity decay rate ($\gamma_a$), and coupling strengths ($g_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Adiabatic elimination of the fast-decaying cavity mode is used, and some analytical approximations rely on weak phonon exchange interactions ($\chi \ll \Gamma_2$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the system components (Fig. 1), show bipartite entanglement vs. $	heta$ (maximized at $\pi/2$), and demonstrate temporal evolution of fidelity $F(t)$ confirming analytical accuracy limits.

**Paper structure.** The paper models the full Hamiltonian, derives a reduced master equation for mechanical modes using adiabatic elimination, analyzes the system in collective eigenmode bases ($	ext{D}\pm$), and finally calculates entanglement measures to show phase-dependent control over quantum states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate a dissipation-engineering approach to produce a phase-dependent collective-mode Schrödinger cat state involving two modes. Our model features a single cavity mode that interacts with two spectrally identical mechanical oscillators. Both oscillators are coupled through a phase-dependent hopping interaction. We demonstrate that by adjusting the phase of the phonon-hopping interaction, one can control the bipartite entanglement of the phase-dependent two-mode cat state during its generation. Additionally, our study reveals that phonon interactions act as a tunable parameter, enabling the manipulation of steady-state entanglement between the bare mechanical modes, even in the presence of environmental effects and thermal excitations. Our scheme provides a feasible approach for the phase-dependent multi mode non-Gaussian states preparation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically examines how interactions and nonlinearities impact fundamental uncertainty relations that bound the precision of measurements in bosonic quantum transport. By analyzing model systems like the spin-boson model, the authors show that while local bounds are tight for linear systems, nonlinearity can significantly alter or invalidate these established predictive limits.

**Why it may be interesting.** This is highly relevant as it bridges non-equilibrium thermodynamics (entropy production) with quantum transport theory, providing concrete predictions about measurement limitations in interacting nanoscale devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how nonlinearities (interactions) affect the validity and predictive power of local kinetic and thermokinetic uncertainty relations governing bosonic transport.

**Main result.** The paper demonstrates that while linear systems allow for tight, locally defined precision bounds, these bounds are impacted or potentially broken when strong nonlinear interactions are present in the steady-state regime.

**Method.** The analysis employs theoretical tools including the Gorini–Kossakowski–Sudarshan–Lindblad (GKSL) master equation, Full Counting Statistics (FCS), and derivations of various activity measures to establish bounds.

**Model / system.** The work analyzes bosonic transport in two specific models: a harmonic oscillator network and the intrinsically nonlinear spin-boson model (NESB). The general framework involves an open quantum system coupled to Markovian baths.

**Key observables.** Precision $P(X) := (I(X))^2 / S(X)$, entropy production ($\sigma$), local/global activity measures, and current noise ($S(N)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis focuses on the transition from linear to nonlinear regimes, and the dependence of bounds on the specific definition of 'activity'.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Approximations used include Born, Markov, and secular approximations when deriving correlator-based activity definitions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 sketches illustrate the physical setups: a harmonic oscillator network coupled to two reservoirs, the single oscillator limit, and the NESB model under strong nonlinearities.

**Paper structure.** The paper first reviews the theoretical framework of uncertainty relations in bosonic transport. It then develops multiple definitions for dynamical activity using Green's functions and correlators, showing their mathematical equivalence under approximations. Finally, it applies these tools to analyze how nonlinearity affects the derived local precision bounds in specific model systems.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The precision of transport observables can be bounded by the entropy production and the activity of the transport process via kinetic and thermokinetic uncertainty relations. In linear bosonic systems, such uncertainty relations can provide tight bounds when the activity is replaced by a local activity of the measurement contact of interest---even in the strong-coupling regime. How much nonlinearities (or interactions) impact the validity and predictiveness of these local bounds and how much this impact depends on the concrete definition of activity are open questions that we address for two experimentally relevant model systems, a harmonic oscillator network and the intrinsically nonlinear spin-boson model. We thereby provide experimentally testable predictions on how nonlinearities impact or even break local kinetic and thermokinetic precision bounds.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors demonstrate the control of quantum transport by engineering dissipation in a synthetic lattice built from $^{87}	ext{Rb}$ BEC atoms. By tuning the loss rate relative to tunneling, they achieve near-perfect absorption at an optimum balance point, contrasting this with ballistic or Zeno reflection regimes. This establishes controlled dissipation as a powerful resource for tailoring quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly controllable platform (BECs) to engineer open quantum systems, making it directly relevant to research in open quantum dynamics, engineered dissipation, and non-Hermitian physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study experimentally explores wavepacket absorption when impinging on a dissipative region within an engineered quantum system, aiming to control quantum transport by tuning dissipation.

**Main result.** Optimal energy absorption is achieved by carefully matching the tunneling strength and the dissipation rate, demonstrating a controllable transition between ballistic propagation, near-perfect absorption, and Zeno reflection.

**Method.** The research combines experimental measurements on an atomic BEC with numerical simulations using non-Hermitian tight-binding Hamiltonians to model wavepacket dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system is a 1D non-Hermitian synthetic lattice realized using the hyperfine manifold of a trapped $^{87}	ext{Rb}$ Bose-Einstein condensate. The lattice features an abrupt interface between dissipative and non-dissipative subchains.

**Key observables.** Absorption efficiency ($A$), occupation probabilities $P_j(t)$, and total probability $P_T(t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dissipation rate ($ar{\gamma}$), inter-chain tunneling strength ($J_0$), and the ratio $ar{\gamma}/ar{J}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on modeling the system with a non-Hermitian Hamiltonian, comparing measurements against numerical simulations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate wavepacket occupation probabilities in three regimes (lossless, optimal absorption, Zeno reflection) and show parameter scans confirming that maximum absorption requires matching tunneling and dissipation.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical system and Hamiltonian to presenting experimental observations across different dissipation regimes, followed by theoretical analysis using idealized semi-infinite models for confirmation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We experimentally explore the absorption of a propagating wavepacket impinging upon a dissipative region in an engineered quantum system. We employ a 1D non-Hermitian synthetic lattice with an abrupt interface between dissipative and non-dissipative subchains, using the states of the electronic ground-state hyperfine manifold in a $^{87}$Rb Bose-Einstein condensate as sites. By tuning the dissipation rate, we observe a progression from ballistic propagation, to near-perfect absorption, to quantum Zeno reflection. Guided by numerical simulations, we identify that optimal absorption occurs when tunneling and dissipation are properly matched, and find qualitative agreement with an idealized semi-infinite model across all dissipation regimes. Our results establish synthetic lattices as a versatile Quantum simulation platform for dissipation-engineered quantum transport and highlight controlled dissipation as a resource for tailoring quantum dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops the theory of large deviations for quantum systems, which is crucial for understanding rare events in physics. It demonstrates that these fluctuations can be characterized by minimizing measurement-induced relative entropy across various models. The work applies this framework successfully to both condensed matter analogs (anharmonic oscillators) and fundamental cosmology problems like stochastic inflation.

**Why it may be interesting.** It bridges foundational statistical mechanics (large deviations) with modern open quantum systems dynamics and high-energy astrophysics (cosmology), providing tools applicable to non-equilibrium quantum processes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a practical theory of large deviations for quantum systems, addressing a major gap in understanding rare fluctuations relevant to statistical physics and cosmology.

**Main result.** The paper shows that dominant rare quantum fluctuations minimize the measurement-induced relative entropy, providing a thermodynamic description applicable from quantum walks to cosmological field evolution.

**Method.** The authors analyze open quantum systems using techniques involving quantum fidelity maximization and minimizing measurement-induced relative entropy, extending classical large deviation principles.

**Model / system.** Models include the anharmonic oscillator coupled to a bath, general quantum random walks, and long wavelength scalar fields evolving on a fixed de Sitter background in cosmology.

**Key observables.** Measurement-induced relative entropy, Quantum Fidelity ($\mathcal{F}$), rate function $I(\alpha)$, and the breakdown of KMS symmetry.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (thermal vs. quantum regimes), coupling constants ($\lambda$), and cosmological scale factors defining de Sitter space.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes Gaussian noise for deriving diffusion equations, and it builds upon established large deviation theory from classical statistical mechanics.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the crossover between classical random (thermal), quantum mechanical (WKB), and numerical instanton contributions to the probability distribution $P(x)$ at large distances.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first analyzing the anharmonic oscillator's transition from thermal to quantum behavior, then generalizing this using relative entropy minimization for abstract quantum walks, and finally applying these concepts to random walks arising in stochastic inflation/de Sitter cosmology.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Rare fluctuations in physical systems depend on the detailed microphysics responsible for the fluctuations. In classical statistical systems, the large deviation principle has elucidated the role of semi-classics in describing this regime, and has simultaneously provided a the mathematical foundation of statistical mechanics. Large deviation theory for quantum system is considerably less developed. As all physical systems are fundamentally quantum mechanical, this leaves a major gap in our understanding of rare fluctuations relevant to statistical physics, cosmology, and more. In this paper, we develop the practical aspects of the theory of large deviations relevant for calculating rare events in physical systems from quantum walks to cosmology. We first analyze the case of the anharmonic oscillator coupled to a bath, showing explicitly how the system evolves from dominantly statistical (e.g. thermal) to quantum fluctuations. We then generalize these results, showing that the dominant rare fluctuations minimize the measurement-induced relative entropy. This perspective provides a thermodynamic description of a wide range of open quantum systems. We apply these results to random walks that arise in cosmology through stochastic inflation. We show that the evolution of the density matrix of long wavelength fields on a fixed de Sitter background breaks the KMS symmetry, giving rise to a stationary density matrix that does not respect detailed balance.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes a novel quantum framework to analyze event graphs, treating them as physical systems governed by an XY-type spin Hamiltonian derived from Lattice Gauge Theory. It bridges abstract graph learning concepts with rigorous quantum mechanics by mapping latent participant states onto observable event isospins. The resulting model can be implemented using quantum-inspired graph transformer architectures for anomaly detection.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit connection between lattice gauge theory (a tool from condensed matter/high energy physics) and the structure of modern graph neural networks is highly relevant for theoretical physicists exploring quantum information applications in complex systems modeling.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a quantum framework to analyze event graphs derived from classical datasets, specifically for anomaly detection.

**Main result.** It establishes a mathematical bridge between graph learning (event graphs) and quantum mechanics by formulating the dynamics using an XY-type spin model governed by a Lattice Gauge Theory Hamiltonian.

**Method.** The approach involves mapping participant graphs to event graphs via a line-graph transformation, assigning Quantum Harmonic Oscillators (QHOs), and then deriving an effective Hamiltonian structure suitable for graph neural network architectures.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by a directed participant graph $G(V, E)$ whose edges generate Schwinger isospins. This leads to an event graph $	ilde{G}$ where dynamics are governed by a Kogut-Susskind Hamiltonian (KSH) acting on observable anomalous spins.

**Key observables.** The expectation value of the edge isospin $\langle S_{j	o k} angle$ reveals partial information about hidden participant states; the final classical score $s_\ell$ is read out from the reduced density operator $ho^{(	ext{out})}_{\ell}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The truncation dimension $d=2$ (leading to Ising-like dynamics) and the coupling strengths $J_{l,l'}$ and local fields $h_\ell$ in the Hamiltonian are key parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** A primary assumption is the lowest nontrivial truncation of the local Hilbert space ($d=2$). Furthermore, practical implementations rely on approximating the global reduced state by preserving local coherence ($ho^{(	ext{out})}_{\ell} \approx ho^{(L,M)}_{\ell}$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the mapping from participant graphs to event graphs using QHOs and isospins. Figure 2 shows reference graph examples of binary event archetypes corresponding to specific isospin states.

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines the quantum structure on the participant graph (QHOs, Schwinger isospins). It then applies a line-graph transformation to define observable nodes/edges in the event graph. The dynamics are formulated via a U(1) LGT leading to an XY-type Hamiltonian. Finally, it details how this physical model can be realized and trained using quantum-inspired graph transformer architectures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Graph representations of discrete events provide a natural foundation for machine-learning models of anomaly detection, yet they also suggest a deeper quantum description in which graph structure gives rise to interacting quantum degrees of freedom. We develop a quantum framework based on a directed participant graph whose edges represent events connecting pairs of source and destination vertices. A line-graph transformation maps each event to a node of a bidirectional event graph, whose edges inherit relational information from the participant graph. Since event datasets are naturally organized as collections of event records, their raw attributes align directly with the nodes of the event graph. A quantum harmonic oscillator (QHO) is assigned to every node of the participant graph, with the collective Hilbert space of these QHOs providing a complete basis for representing quantum states. Every directed edge of the participant graph thereby acquires a Schwinger isospin arising from the two endpoint oscillators. Under the line-graph transformation, event-graph nodes correspond to observable isospins whose interactions through bidirectional edges provide a natural substrate for learning from event datasets, while the quantum states associated with the underlying participant nodes remain latent and inaccessible to direct observation. Within this framework we formulate a compact U(1) lattice gauge theory (LGT) on the event graph that leads to a Kogut-Susskind Hamiltonian (KSH) in the form of an XY-type spin model governing the dynamics of sparse anomalous-event isospins immersed in a bath of many nominal events. The proposed framework establishes a mathematical foundation for quantum-inspired graph-based anomaly detection and provides a principled bridge between graph learning, LGT, and quantum information.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops an advanced string theory toolbox to handle the complex physics of mobile Quantum Hall (QH) interfaces that terminate at edges. It constructs a junction framework that rigorously enforces charge and anomaly conservation across these boundaries. The result provides a systematic, operational definition for QH branes and endpoint theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, field-theoretic approach to open quantum systems boundaries, which is highly relevant for understanding dissipation, coupling to leads, or interfaces in condensed matter systems like superconducting qubits or topological insulators.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a consistent theoretical framework for determining the endpoint data required for a freely moving Quantum Hall (QH) interface that terminates on a physical or topological boundary.

**Main result.** The authors establish an open-worldsheet junction framework, showing that charge conservation and anomaly flow dictate that specific localized excitations, like a lone chiral Majorana, cannot terminate without satisfying complex continuity conditions.

**Method.** The work employs variational principles applied to the worldsheet action, developing specialized boundary functionals (like the capped relative-area functional) to enforce consistency across geometric, material, and topological sectors.

**Model / system.** The model is a mobile QH interface treated as an open worldsheet embedded in a background manifold. It incorporates concepts from string theory, chiral edge states, and topological order relevant to fractional quantum Hall physics.

**Key observables.** Endpoint force (F), bending moment (M), material charge ($Q_{mat}$), total charged-boson zero mode ($Q_{seg}$), and the scattering matrix $S_\gamma(\omega)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The filling factors ($
u$), topological sectors, geometric support curves, and coupling constants governing anomaly flow.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to fixed open segments; processes involving worldsheet splitting or joining are considered beyond the scope of this specific construction.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the geometry of an open mobile QH interface terminating at endpoints L and R on a support curve, defining the capped relative area $A_{op}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing mechanical boundary terms via variational calculus to formulating the junction framework. It then details charge conservation laws, analyzes endpoint completion using topological sectors (open Wilson lines), and concludes with specific scattering analyses for Majorana junctions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A freely moving quantum Hall (QH) interface may end on a physical edge or topological boundary, but fixed-edge theory cannot determine what endpoint data make such a termination consistent. Here we formulate an open-worldsheet junction framework in which the embedding, material charge, anomaly flow, and topological boundary condition are organized together. The endpoint is specified by a geometric support and variational boundary data, together with condensable topological sectors and any outgoing channels required to absorb or continue the worldsheet flux. This construction extends the charge--shape relation to an interval and shows why a lone chiral Majorana cannot terminate on a finite-dimensional endpoint degree of freedom. It gives an operational definition of a QH brane and a systematic basis for endpoint and network theories of dynamical QH interfaces.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores 'quantum Mpemba effects' to show that entanglement generation and decay can be exponentially accelerated or slowed down. By analyzing the spectral properties of open quantum systems governed by Lindblad dynamics, the authors derive conditions for these speedups using specific entanglement measures. This work is significant for understanding non-equilibrium resource management in quantum technologies.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful theoretical framework for understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in open quantum systems, showing how engineered dissipation can dramatically alter relaxation rates of key resources like entanglement.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how quantum Mpemba effects can exponentially accelerate entanglement generation or slow down its decay in open quantum systems.

**Main result.** Exponential speedups are demonstrated by engineering the system's dynamics to suppress slow spectral modes, showing that these effects can arise independently of conventional mechanisms.

**Method.** The analysis uses spectral decomposition of the Lindblad master equation and compares entanglement evolution using different measures (e.g., Concurrence vs. Logarithmic Negativity).

**Model / system.** The theory is applied to open quantum systems evolving under a Lindblad master equation, with specific examples including the dissipative two-site Kitaev model and a long-range Ising chain.

**Key observables.** Entanglement measures (Concurrence, Logarithmic Negativity), preparation/relaxation times ($	au_{	ext{prep}}, 	au_{	ext{rel}}$), and spectral coefficients ($Z^{(N)}_k$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dissipation rates ($\gamma, \kappa$), coupling strengths ($\Delta$), and the choice of entanglement monotone.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The long-time dynamics are analyzed via spectral decomposition, assuming Fréchet differentiability for certain observables.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate mode visibility diagrams ($Z^{(N)}_k$) showing conditions for speedup, and time evolution plots comparing different entanglement measures under varying parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces two types of entanglement Mpemba effects (PEME and REME), derives the necessary spectral conditions using linear approximations, applies these concepts to specific models like the I2KM, and compares results across various entanglement monotones.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entanglement is a key resource for quantum technologies. We show that protocols employing quantum Mpemba effects allow one to exponentially accelerate the generation of entanglement, or to slow down the decay thereof. Two entanglement Mpemba effects with different operational meaning are introduced, focusing either on the task of rapidly generating a certain threshold value for entanglement or on achieving the asymptotic steady-state value. We show that entanglement Mpemba effects depend on the chosen entanglement measure. Using cluster elimination methods, many-body quantum systems also benefit from the exponential speedup of entanglement generation, as we demonstrate for a dissipative long-range Ising chain.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the entanglement Hamiltonian generated in a fermionic chain after partial measurements. By mapping the problem onto a thermal field theory using conformal mappings, the authors show that the EH encodes detailed information about the measurement outcome, which is richer than what can be obtained solely from the entropy. This establishes a strong link between quantum measurement processes and fundamental concepts of statistical mechanics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep insights into how measurement backaction modifies entanglement structure, linking it rigorously to thermodynamic concepts like chemical potentials within the framework of CFT for free fermions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the entanglement Hamiltonian (EH) of an infinite hopping chain's ground state following partial projective measurements in the occupation basis.

**Main result.** The EH for post-selected states contains more outcome-specific information than the corresponding entropy, and its local description relates to a grand-canonical thermal system via conformal field theory techniques.

**Method.** The analysis employs Conformal Field Theory (CFT) methods, mapping the measurement process onto a path integral on a cylinder geometry derived from complex conformal mappings.

**Model / system.** The model is an infinite chain of non-interacting fermions governed by a simple hopping Hamiltonian. The system undergoes partial projective measurements in two disconnected segments.

**Key observables.** Entanglement Hamiltonian (EH), Entanglement Entropy ($S$), Induced Charge Density ($ho(x)$), and Local Chemical Potential ($\mu_m(x)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The local inverse temperature $eta(x)$, the total induced charge $Q$, and parameters related to boundary phases ($\delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on taking a continuum limit from lattice results and utilizing CFT descriptions for the reduced density matrix.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the measurement setup and the conformal map onto a cylinder. Figure 2 plots entanglement entropy versus induced charge, comparing numerical data against the theoretical CFT prediction $S(Q)$.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining the physical system and measurements, deriving the reduced density matrix using CFT techniques (conformal mapping), establishing the relationship between the EH and a thermal grand-canonical state, and finally comparing the resulting entropy/chemical potential structure to lattice calculations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the entanglement Hamiltonian of an infinite hopping chain in its ground state, after partial projective measurements in the occupation basis. For a segment separated by two measurement regions from the rest of the chain, we show that the reduced density matrix can be related to a grand-canonical state via a conformal mapping and a gauge transformation in the underlying field-theory description. The entanglement Hamiltonian is then described by a local inverse temperature that vanishes as a square root around the endpoints and is independent of the particular measurement outcome. In sharp contrast, the local chemical potential is shown to be related to the induced charge density in the segment. Hence the entanglement Hamiltonian of a post-selected state contains much more information on the measurement outcome than the respective entropy.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes how making a local consensus rule irreversible changes pattern formation in a binary spin system. The key finding is that irreversibility arrests the system into stable, mixed labyrinth patterns characterized by 'frustrated' sites. This work provides a detailed comparison between reversible and irreversible relaxation dynamics on a lattice.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the concept of irreversible dynamics leading to metastable, patterned states is highly relevant to understanding non-equilibrium phase transitions in complex systems, which has parallels in quantum quench dynamics or driven many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the fundamental differences between reversible and irreversible dynamics when a local imitation rule drives a binary field toward consensus or striped patterns.

**Main result.** Irreversibility causes the system to freeze into stable, balanced non-consensus labyrinth structures that would otherwise be eliminated by reversible relaxation. The resulting pattern is determined deterministically by initial conditions, with frustration marking the seams.

**Method.** The authors compare two dynamics: a standard reversible threshold process (related to mean curvature flow) against an irreversible 'one-flip' rule on a lattice.

**Model / system.** The model uses spins $s_i \in \{\pm 1\}$ on $\mathbb{Z}^2$ interacting via a Gaussian kernel. The dynamics involve local updates based on the weighted majority of neighbors, with the key distinction being that irreversible dynamics allow sites to flip at most once.

**Key observables.** Frustrated spins (sites unable to flip against their local field), pattern morphology (bicontinuous labyrinths), and agreement metrics comparing different update orders.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction range ($\sigma$), initial spin composition ($p_0 \approx 1/2$), and the degree of irreversibility (the one-flip constraint).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The comparison hinges on the single difference: the irreversible 'one-flip' rule versus the reversible relaxation process.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the contrast between consensus states (reversible) and frozen labyrinths (irreversible), showing frustrated sites marking collision seams, and demonstrating that healing these sites does not alter the coarse domain layout.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the dynamics and compares reversible/irreversible outcomes. It then analyzes specific geometries like flat stripes and droplets using continuum approximations derived from the lattice model. Finally, it rigorously separates the deterministic placement of domain walls from the secondary role of update-order effects via frustration.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Majority threshold dynamics, in which each agent adopts the dominant state in a weighted neighborhood, relaxes a binary field toward consensus or stripes. We study what happens when this rule is made irreversible: each agent, interacting through a Gaussian kernel on a lattice, may flip out of its local weighted minority at most once. The reversible form is threshold dynamics of Merriman-Bence-Osher type, an exactly solvable calibration in which interfaces move by mean curvature with closed-form lattice pinning and mobility. Irreversibility changes the outcome. From random initial conditions, the one-flip rule freezes balanced non-consensus labyrinths that reversible relaxation drives away. The patterned regime is a window of initial spin compositions around equal balance, narrowing as the interaction range grows, controlled by a standardized bias whose onset is independent of scale over a fourfold range. The frozen morphology is arrested coarsening: bicontinuous at balance, with a feature width that grows sublinearly and falls below the interaction range at large scales. We determine the mechanism by intervention. Fixing the initial condition while varying the update order shows that the coarse domain layout is deterministic, while the update order enters only in a secondary first-passage race that fine-tunes the wall positions the deterministic dynamics has already set. Those walls are marked by frustration: agents frozen against their own local field. This signature is identically absent from any reversible relaxation, is overwhelmingly interfacial, and carries almost none of the pattern's large-scale shape.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates how coupling a Haldane spin chain to an ohmic bath induces quantum phase transitions, focusing particularly on the resulting boundary physics. Using QMC simulations and scaling analysis, the authors show that the topological nature of the SPT ground state leads to unique boundary criticality distinct from non-topological transitions. The work provides a framework for controlling edge properties in dissipative many-body systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This is highly relevant as it tackles non-equilibrium quantum dynamics in strongly correlated systems. The ability to tune boundary criticality via dissipation provides a powerful theoretical tool for understanding open quantum systems beyond simple equilibrium critical points.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how dissipation influences both bulk and boundary critical behavior in quantum spin chains, specifically starting from a Symmetry-Protected Topological (SPT) phase.

**Main result.** The paper demonstrates that the topological edge modes exhibit a distinct boundary criticality compared to transitions out of trivial states. The analysis successfully extracts scaling dimensions for these boundary effects using QMC simulations and comparison with field theory expansions.

**Method.** The research employs large-scale Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) simulations, finite-size scaling analysis, and comparisons with $\epsilon$ expansion results derived from dissipative quantum field theories.

**Model / system.** The primary system is the Haldane chain ($S=1$ spin chain), which is coupled to a dissipative ohmic bath. The study also analyzes related models like dimerized chains and the O(3)-symmetric nonlinear sigma model.

**Key observables.** Generalized string order parameter ($\Delta_{	ext{string}}$), bulk correlation ratios ($R_{	ext{spin}}(L)$), edge local moment susceptibility ($\chi_{	ext{edge}}/eta$), and various scaling dimensions ($\Delta_e$, $\eta_{\parallel}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dissipation strength ($\alpha$), system size ($L$), and critical couplings ($\alpha_c$). The analysis compares results for the Haldane chain ($\delta=0$) versus trivial/dimerized states ($\delta=0.4$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping quantum dynamics to classical path integrals (for some models) and extrapolating finite-size data to infinite size using power laws.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate bulk criticality measurements via spin/string order parameters vs. dissipation strength ($\alpha$), and boundary criticality via edge local moment susceptibility ($\chi_{	ext{edge}}/eta$) vs. $\alpha$.

**Paper structure.** The work systematically compares the critical behavior of the Haldane chain (SPT) against a trivial state, analyzing both bulk exponents using correlation ratios and distinct boundary exponents derived from edge susceptibilities.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the Haldane chain coupled to a dissipative ohmic bath. Using large-scale quantum Monte Carlo simulations, we identify a second-order quantum phase transition into an antiferromagnetic state with spontaneously broken SO(3) symmetry that is governed by an interacting fixed point with dynamical exponent $z\approx 2$. We derive a generalized string order parameter which indicates that the symmetry-protected topological ground state of the Haldane chain is stable for weak dissipation and develops a nontrivial scaling dimension at criticality. In particular, its topological edge modes display nontrivial boundary criticality that is distinct from an equivalent transition out of a trivial state; for the latter, we perform an accurate $ε$ expansion of a dissipative $φ^4$ theory with ordinary boundary conditions. Our setup realizes a spin impurity in a critical yet nonconformal antiferromagnet and paves the way for continuously tunable boundary criticality controlled by dissipation.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes how a periodically oscillating magnetic field drives the 2D Ising model across its continuous phase transition, revealing universal dynamic scaling laws. The key finding is that the system exhibits characteristic oscillatory behavior in its magnetization and energy density as time progresses relative to the driving period. This provides a theoretical framework for understanding non-equilibrium critical dynamics under external periodic forcing.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the methodology of analyzing non-equilibrium dynamics driven by time-periodic fields is highly relevant to open quantum systems where external periodic drives (e.g., in cavity QED or cold atoms) are used to probe quantum critical points or drive coherent evolution.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates dynamic scaling behavior in classical ferromagnetic systems undergoing continuous phase transitions when subjected to a periodic external driving field.

**Main result.** Periodic driving induces a peculiar dynamic scaling characterized by oscillatory behavior around a smooth curve that approaches a large-time stationary state, confirming theoretical predictions for the universality class.

**Method.** The analysis employs dynamic finite-size scaling theory, generalizing it to incorporate time periodicity and analyzing the collapse of observables onto universal scaling functions.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the two-dimensional (2D) Ising model on a square lattice. The system dynamics are governed by purely relaxational dynamics (Model A) at the critical temperature, driven by $h(t)=-A \cos(2\pi t/P)$.

**Key observables.** Time-dependent magnetization ($M(t)$), subtracted bond-energy density ($E_s(t)$), and period-averaged rescaled quantities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The scaling variables are $	au = t/P$ (normalized time) and $\sigma = A P^\kappa$, where $\kappa = y_h/z$. The analysis examines limits such as large-$	au$ and small-$\sigma$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory assumes purely relaxational dynamics (Model A) and relies on the ability to reach the thermodynamic limit ($L 	o \infty$) for scaling predictions.

**Figures summary.** Figures demonstrate dynamic scaling collapse for both magnetization and energy density under periodic driving, showing distinct regimes from initial transients to large-time stationary oscillations.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework by generalizing finite-size scaling to include periodic driving. It then applies this theory to the 2D Ising model using numerical simulations to confirm the predicted dynamic scaling laws for magnetization and energy density, concluding with extensions to temperature driving protocols.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the critical dynamics arising from a time-dependent periodic homogenous source coupled to the order-parameter field, which drives a classical ferromagnetic system across a continuous transition. For this purpose, we consider the paradigmatic two-dimensional (2D) Ising model in the presence of a periodic magnetic field $h(t)=-A\, \cos (2πt/P)$, evolving under a purely relaxational dynamics at the critical temperature. We show that the periodic driving gives rise to a peculiar dynamic scaling behavior in the thermodynamic limit, arising from a nontrivial interplay among the time $t$, the amplitude $A$ and period $P$ of $h(t)$. The relevant scaling variables are $τ=t/P$ and $σ=A P^κ$, with $κ= y_h/z$, where $y_h=(d+2-η)/2$ is the critical dimension of the magnetic field, and $z$ is dynamic exponent for the critical relaxational dynamics ($κ\approx 0.865$ for the 2D Ising model). The dynamic scaling behaviors of the magnetization and bond-energy density show an oscillatory behavior around a smooth curve which approaches a large-$τ$ stationary behavior. We also briefly discuss the dynamic behavior of an Ising system driven across the critical point by a periodic time-varying temperature at zero magnetic field.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops rigorous mathematical criteria to determine when stochastic Monte Carlo simulations can accurately model bosonic open quantum dynamics governed by the GKSL equation. By analyzing the positive semidefiniteness of the diffusion matrix derived from path integrals, the authors clarify the necessary conditions for these powerful simulation techniques to be both feasible and indispensable over mean-field approximations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides crucial theoretical guidelines for computational physicists attempting to simulate complex open quantum systems, clarifying when simple mean-field or truncated Wigner approaches are insufficient.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is establishing rigorous criteria for when Monte Carlo stochastic simulations are mathematically feasible and physically necessary for accurately modeling bosonic open quantum dynamics.

**Main result.** The authors derive sufficient conditions, starting from the path integral formulation, that guarantee the positive semidefiniteness of the diffusion matrix, thus validating the use of stochastic methods over mean-field approximations.

**Method.** The work combines path integral formulations with phase-space mapping using quasiprobability distributions (P, Wigner, Q functions) to derive and analyze corresponding Stochastic Differential Equations (SDEs).

**Model / system.** The system involves bosonic open quantum many-body dynamics governed by the Gorini–Kossakowski–Sudarshan–Lindblad (GKSL) equation. The analysis is performed in phase space, considering interactions and dissipation from an environment.

**Key observables.** Positive semidefiniteness of the diffusion matrix ($\mathbf{A} \succeq 0$), time evolution of quasiprobability functions ($W_s$), and quantum fluctuation terms.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis focuses on the thermodynamic limit, high-occupancy limits, and the nature of the jump operators (e.g., quadratic vs. general).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on mapping the GKSL equation to a Fokker–Planck type description, which is only valid under specific conditions regarding higher-order quantum fluctuations.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically moves from defining the problem (GKSL dynamics) to establishing the mathematical framework (phase-space mapping and path integrals), deriving necessary feasibility criteria for stochastic simulation, and finally analyzing specific limits where approximations break down.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Monte Carlo sampling of the stochastic differential equations (SDEs) based on the quasiprobability distribution function, such as the Glauber--Sudarshan P, Wigner, and Husimi Q functions provides a powerful framework for investigating bosonic open quantum many-body dynamics described by the Gorini--Kossakowski--Sudarshan--Lindblad (GKSL) equation, while considering the effects of quantum fluctuations beyond the mean-field approximation. However, the stochastic Monte Carlo simulation is possible only when the corresponding Fokker--Planck equation has a positive-semidefinite diffusion matrix, and the general conditions for the diffusion matrix to be positive semidefinite have remained unclear. In this work, starting from the path integral formulation, we first derive the sufficient conditions under which the diffusion matrix is positive semidefinite for an arbitrary Hamiltonian, jump operators, and choice of quasiprobability distribution functions. We also analytically derive the corresponding SDEs to be solved. We then investigate the dynamics of the GKSL equation in the thermodynamic limit and show that, depending on the form of the jump operators, the mean-field approximation may fail to describe the dynamics accurately, making stochastic Monte Carlo simulations indispensable. Furthermore, we derive the sufficient conditions under which the higher-order quantum fluctuation terms beyond the Fokker--Planck description vanish identically, even when the jump operators contain quadratic terms. Under these conditions, whenever the corresponding SDEs can be derived, the stochastic Monte Carlo simulation reproduces the exact dynamics. These results clarify the conditions under which the stochastic Monte Carlo simulations are both feasible and necessary for accurately describing the dynamics governed by the GKSL equation in phase space.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the field-space entanglement dynamics between two coupled Luttinger liquids subjected to a time-varying tunneling interaction. Using Gaussian approximations and solving the Ermakov equation for each momentum mode, the authors derive analytical expressions for key entanglement measures like logarithmic negativity. The results characterize how correlations build up over time, providing insights into non-equilibrium quantum information transfer.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous analytical tools for quantifying non-equilibrium quantum correlations in 1D systems, directly applicable to understanding entanglement generation in cold atom or superconducting circuit quenches.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on how entanglement dynamics evolve between two extended quantum fields when a time-dependent tunneling interaction is activated across their entire length, contrasting with standard spatial bipartitions.

**Main result.** Analytical expressions for logarithmic negativity and mutual information are derived in the Gaussian approximation, showing that early-time entanglement growth exponents depend only on the first non-null derivative of the coupling protocol.

**Method.** The analysis employs a Gaussian approximation after transforming the Hamiltonian into symmetric and antisymmetric field sectors. Key results rely on solving the mode-dependent Ermakov equation for each momentum mode.

**Model / system.** The system consists of two gapless one-dimensional quantum many-body systems modeled by Luttinger liquid theory, coupled via a time-dependent tunneling interaction $J(t)$.

**Key observables.** Logarithmic negativity (LN), mutual information (MI), Rényi entropies ($S_\alpha$), and entanglement entropy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($eta$), coupling protocol function $J(t)$, and the time evolution regime (early-time, long-time average, adiabatic limit).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses a Gaussian approximation for the interaction term and assumes initial states are thermal and factorized across the two fields.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates field-space entanglement generation via global coupling. Figure 2 summarizes the mathematical framework involving symplectic propagators and mode decomposition.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the model Hamiltonian, deriving the decoupled dynamics in terms of harmonic oscillators governed by the Ermakov equation, and then applying this formalism to calculate specific information-theoretic quantities (LN, MI) across various quench protocols and temperature regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entanglement dynamics depend not only on how a quantum system is partitioned, but critically on how interactions across that partition are structured. For a spatial bipartition of a locally interacting system, entanglement is generated near the boundary and then propagates into the bulk. By contrast, when two extended quantum fields are coupled locally along their entire length, the interaction crosses the field-space partition everywhere, and this generates correlations throughout the system. Here, we study the entanglement dynamics between two gapless one-dimensional quantum many-body systems described by Luttinger liquid theory. The systems are initially decoupled and prepared at zero or finite temperature, after which a time-dependent tunneling interaction is activated uniformly along their length. Within a Gaussian approximation, we derive general analytical expressions for the logarithmic negativity, mutual information, and Rényi entropies under arbitrary coupling protocols. At zero temperature, entanglement displays an early-time power-law growth whose exponent is fixed solely by the first non-null derivative of the tunneling protocol. Once the coupling saturates, we obtain exact long-time averages of the information-theoretic quantities and characterise how the correlations scale with temperature and the final coupling strength. We also analyse how mutual information and logarithmic negativity approach the adiabatic limit for a very slow protocol with respect to intrinsic system timescale. This work extends the study of entanglement dynamics in nonequilibrium field theory to field-space partitions and mixed initial states.

</details>

**Authors:** Emir Sezik, Gunnar Pruessner  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.05805v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `non-equilibrium universality` **4/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **3/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **3/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **2/5** · `Frenkel-Kontorova` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `exotic spin models in light-matter` **1/5**

These phases are separated by a half-stable Gaussian fixed point, that is accessible only from δR &gt; 0 along the separatrix √α|λR|/2 = |δR|.
