**Summary.** This paper investigates the complex phase diagram of Malthusian flocks using advanced field theory techniques. By analyzing spin-wave fluctuations via Renormalization Group flow, the authors identify a previously unknown XY-like phase transition driven by activity. The work provides quantitative scaling predictions for this active critical point.

**Why it may be interesting.** Although focused on active matter, the use of advanced techniques like BKT-like RG flows, symmetry analysis of Goldstone modes, and calculating universal scaling behavior makes it highly relevant to theoretical physicists studying non-equilibrium phase transitions in many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To characterize the full phase structure of Malthusian flocks, identifying novel phases beyond those associated with long-range order.

**Main result.** The study identifies a novel phase where dynamics resemble the equilibrium XY Model, characterized by an interplay between activity and spin-wave fluctuations leading to RG flows similar to the BKT transition.

**Method.** The research employs field-theoretic Renormalization Group (RG) analysis on effective equations of motion derived from symmetry considerations for Goldstone modes.

**Model / system.** The system models two-dimensional Malthusian flocks, which are treated as a continuum limit spin system with vision-cone interactions. The dynamics are governed by an effective equation of motion for the angle field $\theta(x, t)$.

**Key observables.** Spin-correlation function $\langle \cos(	heta(r, t) - 	heta(0, 0))\rangle$, and renormalized couplings ($\lambda_R, \delta_R$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise strength ($\Gamma$), Activity coupling constant ($\lambda$), Diffusivity ($D$), and the RG scale variable ($\ell$ or $\mu$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on perturbative RG procedures and approximations for calculating loop corrections, particularly in the asymptotic limit where the system approaches a Gaussian fixed point.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the three physically distinct regions of the RG flow near the Gaussian critical point, separating the Malthusian and XY phases.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the effective field theory for the Goldstone modes, then applying dimensional analysis and naive power counting. It proceeds through a detailed perturbative RG calculation to derive flow equations, culminating in asymptotic solutions for correlation functions near critical points.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Novel phases, beyond long-range order in two dimensions, have continued to be discovered within flocking models, establishing flocking as one of the pivotal paradigms in active matter. However, much of the discussion around ``Malthusian'' (constant density) flocks, an analytically more tractable alternative to the Vicsek model, has centred around the scaling exponents governing the intermediate regime prior to the proliferation of asters, leaving open the question of what other phases the model might display. Here, we study the two-dimensional dynamics of Malthusian flocks and identify a previously unnoticed phase, where the dynamics is that of the equilibrium XY Model. By identifying the symmetries of the model, we derive the effective equations of motion for the Goldstone modes and analyse the spin-wave fluctuations. We identify a novel critical point separating two distinct phases and, using a perturbative RG procedure, determine the RG flows in its vicinity. This allows us to calculate the universal scaling behaviour at the critical point, along with its logarithmic corrections. The novel phase transition here is due to the interaction of activity and spin-waves, unlike the equilibrium counterpart, which undergoes a phase transition in effective degrees of freedom, namely vortices. Nevertheless, the RG flows are similar to those of the Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless transition, and we show that for sufficiently strong noise, the activity becomes irrelevant and the system crosses over to the equilibrium XY universality class.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces an AI-assisted framework to mitigate quantum noise caused by environmental coupling, modeling the process as a diffusion-like trajectory. It trains neural networks on synthetic data from weak measurements to learn an effective inverse map that reconstructs the original quantum state from noisy observations. This offers a powerful hybrid classical-quantum approach for approximating non-unitary dynamics in noisy quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, machine learning-driven methodology for tackling open quantum system dynamics in noisy environments, which is crucial for scaling up near-term quantum hardware by mitigating coherence loss.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses quantum error mitigation by developing an AI-assisted framework to estimate the initial state of a quantum system corrupted by decoherence.

**Main result.** They propose using machine learning models (like U-Nets and Transformers) trained on synthetic data from weak measurements to learn an effective, non-unitary denoising map that approximates the inverse noisy evolution channel.

**Method.** The core method involves training neural networks to map observed noisy density matrices ($ho_t$) back to their estimated pre-noise state ($ho_0$), mimicking classical diffusion model inversion techniques in a quantum context.

**Model / system.** The system is an arbitrary quantum state undergoing decoherence modeled by sequential local weak measurements, which generate a quantum diffusion process. The framework treats the noise as a sequence of non-unitary operations.

**Key observables.** Reconstructed initial density matrix ($ho_0$), noisy intermediate states ($\{ho_t\}$), and the fidelity of reconstruction across single and multi-qubit registers.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Measurement strength parameter ($\epsilon$) and the noise process parameters derived from Pauli basis measurements.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The inverse process is an approximation, not a strictly physical quantum channel; local measurement independence is assumed for global reconstruction; and post-processing filters are used to enforce density matrix constraints.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the forward noisy evolution via weak measurements and the AI model's role in approximating the reverse path from $\{ho_t\}$ back to $ho_0$, comparing global vs. local reconstruction capabilities.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the physical problem of decoherence via weak measurements, then draws an analogy to classical diffusion models, details the training process using various ML architectures (LSTM, ViT, U-Net), and concludes with rigorous mathematical filtering techniques for ensuring output validity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Coupling between a quantum system and its environment causes decoherence by transferring information from the system to environmental degrees of freedom. When discretized in time, such interactions can be interpreted as sequences of weak measurements that provide an effective model of noisy quantum dynamics. Motivated by this picture, we propose an AI-assisted error-mitigation framework for quantum diffusion processes generated by sequential local weak measurements. The forward process progressively erases information from the input state through weak measurements performed in randomly selected Pauli bases, producing basis-dependent local dephasing and locally depolarizing dynamics on average. Machine-learning models are trained on exact synthetic density matrices to learn a channel- and distribution-specific denoising map and estimate the corresponding pre-noise state. We benchmark the approach on single-qubit states and separable and entangled multi-qubit registers. We also study distribution-dependent local-to-global reconstruction, in which local reduced density matrices are used to reconstruct the global state. This experimentally motivated setting relies on locally accessible information and is therefore compatible with noisy and distributed quantum systems. More broadly, the framework provides a hybrid classical-quantum approach for approximating non-unitary dynamics and mitigating coherence loss.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper develops generalized fluctuation relations for calculating work extracted from quantum devices that operate based on a first-detection trigger mechanism. It shows that these relations correct the standard Jarzynski equality by incorporating an entropic term related to the average detection time. The results establish fundamental, measurable limits on energy conversion in such measurement-driven quantum engines.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work extends fundamental theorems like the Jarzynski equality into the regime of measurement-induced quantum engines, providing new bounds on energy extraction that depend on the measurement protocol itself. This is highly relevant for understanding non-equilibrium thermodynamics in open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper derives quantum fluctuation relations characterizing the work extractable from a quantum device undergoing repeated projective measurements triggered by a first-detection event.

**Main result.** Two new fluctuation relations are established, correcting the standard Jarzynski equality with an entropic term dependent on the mean first-detection time. These relations provide fundamental limits on both total and extracted work.

**Method.** The derivation utilizes principles of quantum thermodynamics, applying Jensen's inequality to relate expectation values involving exponential functions of work, and employing time-reversal symmetry arguments.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum device subjected to repeated projective measurements (a 'quantum first-detection information engine'). The analysis considers the general case where the system interacts with an external environment.

**Key observables.** Work extracted ($W_{	ext{ex}}$), total work exchanged ($W_{	ext{tot}}$), average first-detection time ($\langle n angle$), and survival probability ($S_\infty$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($eta$), measurement interval ($	au$), and the initial state $ho_{in}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is based on the Two-Projective-Measurement (TPM) scheme, and certain normalization procedures are required if the survival probability $S_\infty[ho_{in}] > 0$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates a 'First detection information-to-work converter' setup. Figure 2 compares extracted work ($\dot{w}_{	ext{ex}}$) against its upper bound derived from Jensen's inequality and first-detection mediated ergotropy.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon the standard Quantum Jarzynski Equality (QJE) by incorporating the non-Markovian effects of repeated measurements. It derives fluctuation relations for total work and extracted work, showing how these are modified by terms related to the mean first-detection time.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We derive two quantum fluctuation relations for systems undergoing repeated projective measurements. These fluctuation relations characterize the work that can be extracted from a quantum device when a first-detection event triggers a mechanical operation leading to positive work production. The correction to the standard quantum Jarzynski equality depends logarithmically on the mean first-detection time for the time-reversed dynamics.   Application of Jensen's inequality leads to fundamental limits on both the total work involved in the repeated measurements and final mechanical operation, and the extracted work alone.   The general case of a device connected to an external environment is also considered.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a geometric framework using state graphs and hitting times to detect slow relaxation in many-body quantum systems. By analyzing the spectral radius of the graph's hitting matrix, the authors show that this metric acts as a sensitive indicator, sharply changing at the transition into slow dynamics across multiple model types.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, unified graph-theoretic formalism to diagnose non-equilibrium dynamics in complex quantum systems, which is highly relevant for understanding thermalization and glassy behavior in AMO or condensed matter settings.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to identify and characterize slow relaxation dynamics in complex many-body quantum systems.

**Main result.** Slow dynamics are robustly identified by analyzing the geometry of the state graph, specifically through a sharp increase in the spectral radius of the hitting matrix at the transition point.

**Method.** The authors adapt tools from quantum random walks theory to construct and analyze the 'many-body state graph' using quantum hitting times derived from the unitary time-evolution operator.

**Model / system.** Three paradigmatic models are studied: the Rosenzweig-Porter model (RPM), the quantum East model (QEM), and the triangular lattice gas model (TLGM). The analysis is general for systems with discrete Hilbert spaces governed by a Hamiltonian H.

**Key observables.** Pairwise hitting times ($h_{ij}$), the kernel function $k_{ij}(T)$, and the spectral radius of the hitting matrix ($ho(\hat{h})$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Control parameters tuning the system across regimes, such as $\gamma$ in RPM or $s$ in QEM, which dictate the transition between fast and slow dynamics.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on constructing a state graph where nodes are basis states and using asymptotic limits of time-averaged quantities to characterize long-time behavior.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how $ho(\hat{h})$ changes across dynamical regimes, showing sharp increases at the onset of slow dynamics for QEM and RPM. Other figures show the evolution of $k_{ij}(T)$ over time T.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the state-graph formalism using quantum hitting times; it applies this framework to three distinct models (RPM, QEM, TLGM); it establishes that graph geometry metrics like $ho(\hat{h})$ serve as universal indicators for slow relaxation transitions; and finally, it discusses the connection between these geometric measures and autocorrelation function decay.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We adapt tools from the theory of quantum random walks to investigate slow relaxation dynamics through the many-body state graph. Specifically, we construct a probe of heterogeneity between basis states defined using hitting times derived from the unitary time-evolution operator. We find that the state-graph geometry, encoded by the pairwise hitting time of basis states, is a highly sensitive indicator of slow relaxation dynamics in a variety of systems. We study three paradigmatic models: the Rosenzweig-Porter model, the quantum East model, and the triangular lattice gas model, exhibiting a sudden onset of slow dynamics upon tuning of a control parameter. As a global characterization of the graph geometry, we analyze the spectral radius of the hitting matrix. We find that it increases sharply at the onset of slow dynamics, spanning many orders of magnitude, with a characteristic crossing point at the transition. Our work provides a geometric framework for describing and identifying phases with slow relaxation using a unified graph-theoretic formalism.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically investigates how mobile impurities bind to fractional quasihole excitations within Fractional Chern Insulators. By analyzing the binding energy of these anyon-impurity composites using advanced numerical techniques, the authors propose a robust method to experimentally measure the fractional charge inherent to the topological state. This establishes a pathway for controlled manipulation and braiding of exotic quasiparticles in quantum simulators.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges topological physics (FCIs) with impurity physics, providing concrete, measurable signatures (binding energy ratios) that can be used to verify exotic fractional charge excitations in controllable quantum simulators.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the formation, structure, and energetics of bound states between a mobile impurity and pinned quasihole excitations within Fractional Chern Insulators (FCIs). The ultimate goal is to establish controllable protocols for manipulating these anyonic composite objects.

**Main result.** The binding energy analysis provides a direct method to extract the fractional charge of the underlying quasihole. Furthermore, controlled transport of this composite object can be demonstrated by externally steering the pinning potential.

**Method.** The research combines analytical arguments with large-scale numerical simulations, utilizing techniques like Mean Field theory and Tensor Network methods (iMPS/DMRG) to analyze ground state energies in lattice models.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using the interacting Harper–Hofstadter model deep in the FCI regime, applicable to quantum-engineered platforms. The Hamiltonian includes terms for host interactions ($\hat{H}_{	ext{host}}$), mobile impurity hopping ($\hat{H}_{	ext{imp}}$), and their coupling ($\hat{H}_U$).

**Key observables.** Binding energy ($E_B$), fractional charge of the quasihole ($Q'$), integrated charge response to pinning potentials, and the ratio of binding energies $E_R$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength ($U/J$), flux ($\phi$), filling fraction ($
u=1/2, 1/3$), and the strength of external pinning potentials ($V_h$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often employs mean-field approximations for the impurity-host interaction term and relies on specific lattice models (Harper-Hofstadter) to represent the topological state.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate density distributions, binding energy dependence on trap strength/interaction, and how the ratio of binding energies approaches expected fractional charge values in both CI and FCI regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from analyzing single-particle bound states in traps to modeling interacting systems using the Harper-Hofstadter model. It systematically calculates binding energies for integer vs. fractional charges, demonstrating how external potentials control the excitation state and enabling proposals for braiding protocols.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Mobile impurities provide a powerful means of probing correlated and topological quantum matter, through their dressing by the surrounding medium and the practical probes granting access to the resulting composite object. Motivated by the recent observation of anyon-impurity composites in the solid state, as well as recent realizations of Laughlin-type states in engineered lattice systems, we investigate the formation of a bound state between a mobile impurity and a single pinned quasihole in the interacting Harper-Hofstadter model deep in the fractional Chern insulator regime. Combining analytical arguments with large-scale numerical simulations, we characterize the structure, energetics, and stability of hybrid anyon-impurity bound states, and show that their binding energy provides direct access to the fractional charge of the quasihole under conditions that we identify. We further demonstrate that the composite object can be coherently transported by externally steering the quasihole pinning potential. Our results establish a realistic pathway for controlled anyon-impurity manipulation in quantum-engineered platforms, enabling experimentally feasible protocols for braiding.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes an optimal strategy for scheduling syndrome measurements in quantum error-correcting memory, treating the measurement interval as an optimizable control parameter rather than a fixed clock cycle. By deriving that the optimal interval scales inversely with code distance ($\Delta t^* \propto 1/d$), the authors show this leads to an exponential suppression of logical errors. Furthermore, they introduce an adaptive timing protocol that significantly outperforms static scheduling when noise is bursty.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides crucial theoretical guidance for designing next-generation quantum memories by moving beyond fixed operational cycles. The focus on optimizing the measurement schedule itself is a key area in open quantum systems and fault tolerance theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is optimizing the syndrome-measurement timing ($\Delta t$) in quantum error-correcting codes to minimize logical errors by balancing accumulating idling noise against measurement-induced faults.

**Main result.** Optimal scheduling requires setting the interval inversely proportional to the code distance ($\Delta t^* \propto 1/d$), which yields an exponential reduction in logical error rates compared to fixed-interval protocols. Adaptive strategies further improve performance, especially during noise bursts.

**Method.** The authors developed a phenomenological logical-noise model to analytically derive the optimal timing scaling and analyzed time-dependent noise using adaptive control theory.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on fault-tolerant quantum memories implemented via rotated surface codes. The error sources modeled include idling errors (exponentially dependent on $\Delta t$) and measurement faults (linearly dependent on measurement frequency).

**Key observables.** Logical error rate ($p_L$), optimal syndrome interval ($\Delta t^*$), relative suppression factor ($\Gamma_{	ext{opt}}$), and the time-dependent noise activity $\lambda(t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code distance ($d$), idling noise rate ($\lambda$), measurement fault probabilities ($p_{	ext{stab}}, p_{	ext{read}}$), and experimental parameters from Google's Willow device.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a phenomenological logical-noise model, and the adaptive strategy assumes instantaneous knowledge of the time-dependent noise profile $\lambda(t)$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the trade-off between error sources vs. $\Delta t$, showing that $\Delta t \propto 1/d$ yields exponentially lower errors than constant intervals; other figures compare fixed vs. adaptive protocols for dynamic noise.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the fundamental trade-off, derives the optimal scaling $\Delta t^* \propto 1/d$, and then extends this analysis to time-dependent noise using an adaptive control strategy, concluding with quantitative predictions based on current experimental hardware parameters.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Syndrome-measurements timing is usually treated as a fixed clock cycle of a quantum error-correcting code. For quantum memories, however, the intra-measurement interval is itself an optimizable control parameter: measuring too rarely allows idling errors to accumulate, whereas measuring too often introduces measurement-induced faults. We propose a phenomenological logical-noise model for this trade-off and analytically show that the optimal syndrome-measurements interval scales inversely proportionally with the code distance and that this produces an exponential reduction of logical-error rates in the distance relative to constant-interval schedules. Furthermore, for time-dependent idling noise, we develop an adaptive timing strategy based on the measured syndrome activity that outperforms every fixed-interval protocol, with largest gains for short but strong noise bursts. Simulations of rotated surface-code memories with matching decoding validate the phenomenological model, the distance-dependent optimum, and the adaptive-strategy improvement. Moreover, with the experimental noise parameters reported by Google in Nature 638 (2025), our model predicts reductions in logical-error rates per unit time of up to $40\%$.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes spin-spin entanglement in Compton scattering, treating the outgoing photon and nucleon as a two-qubit system. It develops formalisms using helicity amplitudes to calculate the entanglement measure ($\lambda_{\min}$), demonstrating that polarization is crucial for generating entanglement. The findings suggest this quantum diagnostic tool can map out detailed electromagnetic structure of nucleons.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on particle physics (QCD/Electromagnetism), the core methodology—quantifying entanglement via density matrices and partial transposition criteria ($\lambda_{\min}$)—is directly transferable to analyzing quantum correlations in complex many-body or open quantum systems studied in AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates spin-spin entanglement generated in the final state photon-nucleon system during Compton scattering at both low and high energies.

**Main result.** The authors established a no-go theorem for unpolarized scattering with real amplitudes, but found that polarized scattering reveals rich patterns of maximally entangled Bell states, suggesting entanglement is a novel tool to probe nucleon electromagnetic properties.

**Method.** The analysis involves calculating the two-qubit spin density matrix ($ho$) using helicity amplitudes derived from scattering cross-sections in different kinematic regimes (low/high energy).

**Model / system.** The physical system is Compton scattering off a spin-1/2 nucleon (proton or neutron), treated as an outgoing photon and nucleon two-qubit quantum state.

**Key observables.** Spin density matrix ($ho$), minimum eigenvalue of the partially transposed density matrix ($\lambda_{\min}$), polarization vectors ($\vec{B}_N, \vec{B}_\gamma$), and correlation matrices ($C_{ab}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Photon energy ($\omega$) relative to pion mass ($m_\pi$); electric and magnetic polarizabilities ($\alpha_E, eta_M$); scattering angle ($	heta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on working in the Center-of-Mass (CM) frame; specific approximations are used for high energy via perturbative QCD/GPDs.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates Compton scattering kinematics. Figure 2 shows contour plots of $\lambda_{\min}$ mapping entanglement strength across varying photon energy and scattering angle for electron-target scattering.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the general formalism using helicity amplitudes to construct the density matrix, proving a no-go theorem in unpolarized cases, then detailing low-energy results sensitive to polarizabilities, and finally extending the framework to high-energy QCD calculations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study spin-spin entanglement in the final state photon-nucleon system in Compton scattering, both at low energy below the pion threshold and at high energy in perturbative QCD to next-to-leading order. We first establish a no-go theorem showing that, for any spin-$\frac{1}{2}$ target, entanglement cannot be generated in unpolarized Compton scattering if the scattering amplitudes are real. We then consider polarized Compton scattering off the electron, the proton and the neutron. At low energy, we uncover a rich variety of maximally entangled Bell states and their unitary equivalents realized across different regions of the kinematic plane. Interestingly, the proton and neutron targets exhibit distinct patterns of entanglement. In the neutron case, the electric and magnetic polarizabilities dramatically influence the pattern and even the existence of entanglement. This suggests that entanglement can serve as a novel tool for investigating the detailed electromagnetic properties of the nucleons.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work predicts that strange metal behavior, characterized by linear temperature dependence of resistivity ($ho \propto T$), persists at an unconventional quantum critical point in the 2D Hubbard model. Using advanced non-perturbative methods, the authors demonstrate this scaling occurs without requiring a simple linear-in-$T$ self-energy correction along the Fermi surface.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on strongly correlated electron systems, the failure of quasiparticle descriptions and the emergence of linear-T resistivity at a QCP are fundamental concepts relevant to non-Fermi liquid physics studied in AMO contexts (e.g., near Feshbach resonances or quantum critical points in ultracold atomic mixtures).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates strange metal behavior and its connection to unconventional quantum critical points (QCPs) in two dimensions where standard Fermi liquid theory breaks down.

**Main result.** The authors predict that resistivity scales linearly with temperature ($ho \propto T$) at the QCP, even when the single-particle self-energy does not exhibit a simple linear-in-$T$ dependence along the Fermi surface.

**Method.** Non-perturbative calculations using an improved two-particle self-consistent approach (TPSC+) are employed to analyze correlation functions and transport properties.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is the two-dimensional Hubbard model on a square lattice with nearest-neighbor hopping, focusing on fillings near but above half-filling ($n>1$).

**Key observables.** Resistivity ($ho$), single-particle self-energy ($\Sigma$), spin susceptibility ($\chi_{sp}$), and charge conductivity ($\sigma_{xx}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is performed near the QCP separating Fermi liquid and incommensurate SDW order, with key parameters including interaction strength $U$ relative to hopping $t$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumptions include using the Ornstein-Zernicke form for $\chi_{sp}$ and prioritizing gauge invariance when calculating conductivity via current-current correlation functions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate temperature dependencies of spin fluctuation quantities ($\chi_{sp}, \xi^2_{sp}$) showing power laws, confirmation of linear resistivity scaling at low T, and comparisons between theoretical expressions for the f-sum rule.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops by first establishing the physical context (strange metals near QCPs), then applying advanced many-body techniques (TPSC+) to calculate key susceptibilities and transport coefficients ($\sigma_{xx}$), ensuring gauge invariance throughout the analysis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Properties of the two-dimensional Hubbard model with nearest-neighbor hopping are under scrutiny in cold-atom experiments and diagrammatic quantum Monte Carlo. Given the controlled nature of these approaches, it is timely to make predictions about strange-metal behavior and its relation to quantum-critical properties. Even for interaction strengths below the Mott transition, several peculiarities occur at the quantum critical point separating Fermi liquid and incommensurate spin-density wave order. Here, we predict, using the non-perturbative improved two-particle self-consistent approach, that for correlation lengths ranging from about one to one hundred lattice spacings, Kohn anomalies on the underlying Fermi surface lead to unconventional critical exponents. The temperature dependence of the single-particle self-energy acquires strong momentum dependence along the Fermi surface with no clear evidence of Landau quasiparticles. Nevertheless, we observe strange-metal behavior, namely, resistivity that scales linearly with temperature as $T\rightarrow{0}$. Our work shows that a linear temperature dependence of resistivity arises without a linear-in-temperature self-energy along the Fermi surface, as is often assumed.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces the Hadamard Sensing Channel (HSC), a deterministic technique to suppress spurious signals that degrade quantum sensor readings due to imperfect microwave pulses. By leveraging the orthogonality of Hadamard matrices, HSC achieves signal cancellation superior to probabilistic methods like Phase Randomization. It offers a robust and hardware-efficient solution for high-fidelity nanoscale magnetometry.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a mathematically exact and hardware-friendly protocol for mitigating systematic errors in quantum sensing measurements, which is crucial for advancing high-resolution magnetometry experiments involving open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Spurious responses generated by finite duration microwave pulses during dynamical decoupling sequences compromise quantum sensing fidelity in nanoscale systems.

**Main result.** The proposed Hadamard Sensing Channel (HSC) deterministically cancels these spurious signals using a finite set of orthogonal phase patterns, matching ideal suppression while being more robust than probabilistic methods.

**Method.** HSC employs a deterministic phase-design framework based on Hadamard matrices to average out unwanted signal components over a finite number of runs.

**Model / system.** The study models quantum sensors, specifically using Nitrogen-Vacancy (NV) centers in diamond for nanoscale nuclear magnetic resonance. The system evolution is governed by a Hamiltonian involving spin operators and time-dependent fields.

**Key observables.** Transition probability related to the sensor's remaining coherence ($\langle \hat{S}_x angle$) and the Fourier amplitude of spurious responses ($f_{\perp}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of runs, $M$, which dictates the suppression factor; control error parameters like pulse angle errors and detuning.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that exploiting the orthogonality of Hadamard matrices provides a sufficient basis for deterministic cancellation, even if it deviates from strict abelian group constructions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically compares standard DD, phase-randomized DD, and the proposed HSC protocol, illustrating deterministic modulation across four runs dictated by $H_4$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies spurious responses in DD sequences. It then contrasts existing methods (PR) with its novel HSC approach, deriving the suppression mechanism using Hadamard matrix properties, and finally validating performance through simulations comparing HSC against PR under realistic control errors.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Dynamical decoupling sequences are essential for nanoscale quantum sensing, but the finite duration of microwave pulses generates spurious responses. While phase randomization (PR) protocol can suppress these artifacts, they rely on probabilistic averaging. This introduces an inherent statistical variance that demands excessive sequence lengths and stringent hardware capabilities for true random phase generation. Here, we propose Hadamard sensing channel (HSC), a deterministic phase-design framework based on Hadamard matrices. HSC completely eliminates the statistical variance of PR by exactly and deterministically canceling spurious signals using a finite set of orthogonal phase patterns. Simulations confirm that HSC matches the ideal suppression of PR but exhibits superior robustness against control errors. HSC offers a mathematically exact and hardware-friendly solution for reliable high-resolution nanoscale nuclear magnetic resonance.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the optimal timing ratio for performing X-type versus Z-type syndrome measurements in surface codes built on imperfect hardware. By tailoring this measurement schedule according to the underlying noise bias, the authors demonstrate substantial reductions in the logical error rate. This work provides a practical guide for quantum hardware manufacturers to maximize performance without exhaustive simulation.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on QEC theory, understanding how measurement scheduling mitigates correlated errors (like those induced by defects or crosstalk) provides insights into error management in complex quantum hardware architectures relevant to AMO platforms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates optimizing the scheduling ratio between X-type and Z-type syndrome measurements in surface codes that are adapted to function despite hardware defects.

**Main result.** Optimal scheduling ratios, which depend on the noise bias, can significantly reduce the logical error rate by factors up to 8.46x compared to uniform scheduling, even when considering crosstalk effects.

**Method.** The authors use extensive numerical simulations based on a circuit-level noise model to characterize how varying the X:Z measurement round ratio impacts the overall logical error rate (LER).

**Model / system.** The physical system is the Surface Code, a leading candidate for Fault-Tolerant Quantum Computing. It is modeled as being implemented on hardware containing stochastic defects and operating under biased noise conditions.

**Key observables.** Logical Error Rate (LER), $	ext{LER}_X$, and $	ext{LER}_Z$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise bias ($\eta = p_Z/p_X$), code distance ($d$), defect rate, and the X-to-Z scheduling ratio ($R_X:R_Z$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that defects necessitate alternating X/Z measurements (Snakes and Ladders framework) and focuses on the low-to-moderate bias regime.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate surface code patches with varying defect rates, show components like data qubits and defective couplers, and depict decoding graphs showing interleaved X and Z super-stabilizers based on the scheduling ratio.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically investigates the optimal $X$-to-$Z$ check round-scheduling ratio under biased noise for defect-adapted surface codes. It develops methods to determine this optimum from calibration data, quantifies the LER reduction achieved by optimizing the schedule, and extends these findings to crosstalk-dominated architectures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fault-tolerant Quantum Computing (FTQC) relies on Quantum Error Correction (QEC) codes that encode logical qubits across many physical qubits to detect and correct errors. The surface code is among the most widely studied codes due to its high error threshold, the existence of efficient decoders, and hardware-friendly properties: a planar, two-dimensional layout with nearest-neighbor connectivity. In practice, however, the fabrication of solid-state quantum processors introduces hardware defects, resulting in defective qubits and couplers that must be discarded. Adapting the surface code to these defects often requires measuring the $X$- and $Z$-type checks in separate rounds rather than simultaneously. In this work, we investigate the optimal $X$-to-$Z$ checks round-scheduling ratio under biased noise systems. Our results characterize how key architectural parameters, such as noise bias, code distance, and defect rate, impact the logical error rate. We provide insights into how to determine the optimal scheduling ratio directly from device calibration data, enabling manufacturers to maximize performance without extensive simulations. Our approach reduces the logical error rate by up to $4.25\times$ at a $1\%$ defect rate and up to $8.46\times$ at a $2\%$ defect rate for a distance-$13$ surface code under moderately biased noise. Furthermore, we demonstrate that the benefits of round-scheduling extend beyond the defective-hardware setting. In biased-noise architectures subject to CNOT crosstalk, separating $X$ and $Z$ measurement rounds yields up to $4.5\times$ reduction in logical error rate.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes residual quantum correlations in bosonic fields across spacetime horizons using reflected entropy. It demonstrates that while standard entanglement decays, a specific measure of correlation saturates to a non-zero floor, retaining classical structure. The findings are directly applicable to black hole physics, providing insights into information retention near event horizons.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a sophisticated tool (reflected entropy) to quantify correlations that persist beyond simple entanglement measures in highly curved or accelerating spacetimes, which is crucial for understanding Hawking radiation and quantum information near horizons.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how quantum correlations, measured by reflected entropy, survive across horizons or causal boundaries for bosonic fields in non-inertial frames and black hole spacetimes.

**Main result.** While standard entanglement vanishes asymptotically, the Alice–Bob reflected entropy saturates at a nonzero floor, indicating surviving classical correlation. Furthermore, the inter-wedge reflected entropy exhibits a unique linear divergence with the squeezing parameter for bosons, distinguishing it sharply from fermionic cases.

**Method.** The authors employ reflected entropy and the Markov gap as diagnostics, utilizing conserved charges to block-diagonalize reduced density matrices into solvable sectors.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on a free bosonic field quantized in Rindler coordinates (modeling acceleration) and extends these results to Schwarzschild black hole spacetimes. Key states analyzed include Bell, Werner (W), and GHZ tripartite states.

**Key observables.** Reflected Entropy ($S_R$), Markov Gap, saturation floors for correlation measures, and the divergence rate of inter-wedge entropy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Acceleration parameter ($a$) leading to Unruh temperature $T=a/2\pi$, and the Bogoliubov squeezing parameter ($r$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on identifying a conserved charge to reduce infinite-dimensional problems to finite sectors, and it uses single-mode approximations in some contexts.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Minkowski spacetime mapped to Rindler coordinates, defining causally disconnected wedges separated by the horizon H. Tables summarize $	ext{SR}(A:B)$ across the Hawking spectrum ($\omega/T_H$) for different quantum states.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem using reflected entropy in non-inertial frames (Rindler space), develops the mathematical tools via conserved charges, calculates results for specific tripartite states (Bell, W, GHZ), and finally maps these findings directly to the black hole context.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the reflected entropy and the Markov gap for modes of a free bosonic field shared by inertial observers (Alice, Charlie) and a uniformly accelerated one (Bob), for a bipartite Bell state and the tripartite Werner (W) and Greenberger--Horne--Zeilinger (GHZ) states. The bosonic Bogoliubov transformation spans an infinite-dimensional Fock space with an unbounded squeezing parameter unlike the fermionic case. By identifying a conserved charge, we block-diagonalize the reduced density matrices into exact two-dimensional sectors, yielding closed or semi-analytic forms for all three states. Although bosonic entanglement is known to vanish asymptotically, the Alice--Bob reflected entropy instead saturates at a nonzero saturation floor, retaining the surviving classical correlation, and converges to the value Alice shares with Bob's causally disconnected partner. Crucially, only the inter-wedge reflected entropy diverges, linearly in the squeezing parameter---the sharp distinction from the fermionic case, where it stays bounded---while the Markov gaps saturate. The construction transfers verbatim to a Schwarzschild black hole, where the saturation values become mass-independent constants.

</details>

### [The Locality Gap: A Thermodynamic Law for Objective Facts](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05753v1)

**Authors:** Maxim V. Churilov  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.05753v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `entanglement & information structure` **4/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5**

A partition-local controller resets each allowed block independently, whereas a global controller first exploits cross-block correlations. Their work difference is the locality gap G∅ P. Repeating both tasks with X supplied as catalytic classical side information gives GX P ; the double difference is the thermodynamic factuality charge equation (1).</sub>

**Summary.** This paper introduces the 'thermodynamic factuality charge' as a quantifiable resource associated with redundant environmental records in Quantum Darwinism. By analyzing the difference in minimum work required for local versus global erasure, it establishes that this charge measures how much information is redundantly encoded across accessible fragments. This provides a novel thermodynamic law connecting quantum correlations to physical constraints.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous thermodynamic measure for quantum redundancy that goes beyond simple entropy measures, linking fundamental concepts of quantum information (like correlations) directly to measurable work costs at finite temperature.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a universal thermodynamic signature that quantifies 'factuality' or objective information content arising from redundant records in an environment, extending concepts from Quantum Darwinism.

**Main result.** The paper derives the 'thermodynamic factuality charge,' $\mathcal{F}_T^P$, which is proportional to $k_B T$ times a specific combination of mutual information terms. This charge quantifies the thermodynamic resource generated by redundant records under restricted control, ranging between bounds set by secret sharing and perfect broadcasting.

**Method.** The analysis uses work-extraction principles, comparing minimum work costs for local versus global erasure/resetting tasks on a 'record cloud' $F$. The core diagnostic is derived from differences in free energy or work ($\Delta W$), leading to information-theoretic expressions involving mutual information and entropy.

**Model / system.** The system models objective facts as a 'record cloud' $F$ resulting from an event $X$, which is partitioned into blocks $\mathcal{P}$. The framework applies concepts of quantum information theory (mutual information, total correlation) within a thermodynamic context involving work costs at temperature $T$.

**Key observables.** Thermodynamic factuality charge ($\mathcal{F}_T^P$), Locality Gap ($G_X^P$), Normalized thermodynamic record number ($	ilde{N}_P^X$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** $k_B T$ (thermal energy scale), $H(X)$ (source entropy), $|\mathcal{P}|$ (number of blocks).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work identities are derived within a 'deliberately explicit reset model.' The theory does not modify quantum mechanics or posit objective collapse.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the operational setting by comparing work costs for partition-local vs. global resets ($G_\emptyset^P$) and source-assisted versions ($G_X^P$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the problem using work asymmetries (locality gap), deriving the central 'thermodynamic factuality charge' ($\mathcal{F}_T^P$), establishing its bounds based on information theory, and applying it to specific physical scenarios like perfect broadcasting.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Objective facts in quantum Darwinism are values recorded redundantly in many independently accessible fragments of the environment. Redundancy does not create additional logical information, so its thermodynamic meaning has remained unclear. We show that it creates an exact work asymmetry between controllers with different access architectures. For a record cloud $F$ and a partition $\mathcal{P}$ into blocks that cannot be jointly controlled, the reversible isothermal erasure penalty relative to a global controller is $k_{\mathrm{B}}T$ times the partition total correlation. If the source event $X$ is supplied as catalytic classical side information, subtracting the corresponding penalty gives the source-loss law $\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}}^{T}=k_{\mathrm{B}}T\left[\sum_{B\in\mathcal{P}}I(X{:}F_B)-I(X{:}F)\right]$. We call this the thermodynamic factuality charge. It ranges from $-k_{\mathrm{B}}T H(X)$ for perfect secret sharing to $(|\mathcal{P}|-1)k_{\mathrm{B}}T H(X)$ for perfect broadcast objectivity. Its normalized form defines a thermodynamic record number between $0$ and $|\mathcal{P}|$. We derive Landauer--Darwin bounds, endpoint-rigidity certificates, exact growth and partition-refinement laws, spectrum-broadcast saturation, and closed formulas for noisy classical and quantum collision models. The theory does not modify quantum mechanics or posit objective collapse; it identifies the thermodynamic resource generated by redundant records under restricted control.

</details>

**Authors:** Wayne J. Chetcuti, Nathan Goldman, Patrizia Vignolo, Anna Minguzzi  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum gases · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.05290v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper theoretically proves that one-dimensional, strongly interacting quantum mixtures on a ring can exhibit exact anyonic exchange statistics. By analyzing the momentum distribution and energy response to an applied magnetic flux, the authors establish a direct link between spin/orbital degrees of freedom and fractionalized statistics. This work suggests a pathway for realizing and manipulating anyon states using ultracold atomic gases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete theoretical predictions—measurable via ultracold atoms—for observing fractional exchange statistics (anyonization) in controllable quantum gas setups by manipulating artificial gauge fields.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study demonstrates the exact statistical transmutation of quantum mixtures on a ring, showing that their exchange statistics can be anyonic.

**Main result.** The wavefunction exhibits exact anyonic statistics upon particle exchange, and this signature is directly encoded in the momentum distribution tails and persistent current measurements.

**Method.** The analysis uses Bethe Ansatz techniques (necklace Ansatz) to construct wavefunctions for strongly repulsive mixtures and maps these results onto theoretical descriptions of hard-core anyons.

**Model / system.** The system models one-dimensional, strongly repulsive quantum mixtures confined to a ring threaded by an artificial gauge field. The Hamiltonian includes both orbital and spin degrees of freedom, allowing for the study of Fermi-Fermi and Bose-Bose analogues.

**Key observables.** Momentum distribution $n_\downarrow(k)$, energy spectrum $E_0(\phi)$ vs flux $\phi$, and persistent current $I(\phi)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Strongly repulsive limit, artificial gauge field flux ($\phi$), particle number parity ($N_p$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the strongly repulsive limit where spin and orbital degrees of freedom can be decoupled for exact solutions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate wavefunction construction via necklaces; momentum distributions compare impurity results to anyonic predictions, showing characteristic tails; energy spectra show flux periodicity changes; and time-evolved simulations demonstrate coherent dynamics during a quench protocol.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the general model Hamiltonian and using the necklace Ansatz for exact solutions in the single-impurity limit. It then compares these results to theoretical anyon models, analyzing momentum distributions and energy spectra across different flux values before concluding with a dynamical quench protocol.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

One-dimensional strongly repulsive quantum mixtures exhibit non-trivial exchange statistics arising from the interplay between orbital and spin degrees of freedom. Using an exact solution, we demonstrate that in the single-impurity limit the wavefunction of Fermi-Fermi and Bose-Bose mixtures on a ring threaded by an artificial gauge field display exact anyonic statistics under exchange of the impurity with the majority particles. The resulting fractional exchange phase is fixed by the angular momentum sector selected through the applied flux. We find that the impurity momentum distribution coincide exactly with the anyonic one, independently of the bosonic or fermionic nature of the mixture, with the large-momentum tails encoding a direct signature of the anyonic statistical angle. Finally, we devise a quench protocol for reversible dynamical anyonization. Our results provide a path for realizing and manipulating anyonized states with ultracold atoms.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces Ant-Q, a novel memory hierarchy designed for quantum control systems to overcome severe bottlenecks caused by limited on-chip memory and slow data transfer. By implementing a pipelined workflow that overlaps circuit loading with execution, Ant-Q significantly boosts system throughput and allows for the running of much deeper and more complex quantum experiments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the classical control layer required to run complex quantum algorithms, which is crucial context for any theoretical physicist designing or simulating quantum hardware experiments.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The development of precise quantum experiments and high-throughput quantum computation is limited by a memory bottleneck within the classical quantum control system layer.

**Main result.** The proposed Ant-Q memory hierarchy design successfully transforms the inefficient 'punched-card' workflow into a pipelined architecture, reducing I/O overhead to near zero and enabling deep circuit execution.

**Method.** Ant-Q integrates DRAM with BRAM on the control board to create a pipeline that overlaps data loading, quantum circuit execution, and result readout, bypassing sequential bottlenecks.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on optimizing FPGA-based quantum control systems (like QubiC 3.0) which interface classical processing units with Quantum Processing Units (QPUs). The system architecture involves managing data flow between DRAM (large capacity) and BRAM (fast cache) on the Programmable Logic.

**Key observables.** Overhead reduction percentage relative to execution time, support for deep Randomized Benchmarking circuits, and overall batch throughput improvement.

**Important parameters / regimes.** BRAM capacity limits ($\le 2048$ commands), DRAM non-deterministic latency (refresh cycles), and the comparison between old vs. new system overheads.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary bottleneck is memory structure; future work plans include bypassing the PS-to-host link and implementing predictive preloading for cross-buffer jumps.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the transformation from a sequential 'Punched-Card workflow' to an overlapped, pipelined workflow in Ant-Q, detailing data paths and buffer usage.

**Paper structure.** The paper identifies the memory bottleneck, proposes the Ant-Q memory hierarchy integrating DRAM/BRAM for pipelining, evaluates its performance using real-world circuits (RB), quantifies overhead reduction, and discusses future scaling limitations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

As quantum computing continues to demonstrate promise and attract growing attention, there is an increasing need for more precise experiments to advance the development of quantum devices, as well as higher circuit throughput to validate more domain applications. However, this need is hindered by a memory bottleneck at the quantum control system layer, arising from limited on-chip BRAM capacity and the non-deterministic latency of DRAM. To break this bottleneck, we present Ant-Q, a memory hierarchy design that integrates DRAM with BRAM to support pipelined quantum circuit execution while ensuring deterministic inter-circuit timing. We evaluated Ant-Q using 26 real-world experimental and computing circuits. The results show that Ant-Q supports deep circuits for 1Q and 2Q Randomized Benchmarking and reduces the overhead of circuit loading and readout uplink relative to execution time from 22.90%-1417.05% to near zero. Ant-Q is being integrated into QubiC 3.0, with part of its functionalities already available.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analytically computes the entanglement entropy and full counting statistics for non-interacting fermions in an excited state within a rotating 2D harmonic trap. The authors demonstrate that fundamental scaling laws, such as the area law, remain valid even for these highly excited states. Furthermore, they establish robust additive relations for both entropy and particle number cumulants when analyzing annular subsystems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous theoretical confirmation that fundamental scaling laws (like the area law) persist in complex excited states beyond the ground state, linking entanglement structure directly to particle number fluctuations in a controlled quantum setting.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates entanglement entropy (EE) and Full Counting Statistics (FCS) for excited states of fermions confined in a rotating trap, specifically testing the validity of area law scaling.

**Main result.** The area law scaling holds for EE even in these excited states, and both EE and particle number cumulants exhibit robust additive relations when calculated for an annular region.

**Method.** Analytical computation using the overlap matrix formalism, which relates EE and FCS to eigenvalues of correlation matrices derived from non-interacting fermion wavefunctions.

**Model / system.** Non-interacting spinless fermions confined in a two-dimensional harmonic trap rotating at an angular frequency $\Omega$. The system is analyzed for excited states constructed by filling consecutive single-particle energy levels (e.g., LLL sector).

**Key observables.** Rényi entanglement entropy ($S_q$), particle number cumulants ($\langle\hat{N}_D^pangle_c$), and the centered cumulant generating function.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The angular frequency $\Omega$, the radius $r$ (for discs), and the width of the annulus; analysis is performed in limits where the system size ($M, N$) is large.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The fermions are non-interacting. Additivity for annular regions requires the width to be sufficiently large. The calculations often rely on asymptotic approximations derived from integral representations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate density profiles and show scaling behavior of Rényi entropy ($S(2)/\sqrt{N}$) across different regimes (inner edge, bulk, outer edge) in rescaled coordinates $\xi$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the formalism for EE and FCS using correlation matrices. It then calculates these quantities for discs, confirming area law scaling. Subsequently, it extends the analysis to annular regions, demonstrating additive decomposition of both entropy and cumulants.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the entanglement entropy of a class of $N$-particle excited state of fermions confined in a two-dimensional harmonic trap rotating at an angular frequency $Ω$. The excited state is constructed by filling a particular set of $N$ single-particle energy levels. We analytically compute the Rényi entropies of order $q$ in a disc of radius $r$ around the centre of the trap, and the cumulants corresponding to number fluctuations of fermions within the disc. We found that the area law scaling of entanglement entropy holds even for a class of excited states. We also verified the well-known series expansion of entanglement entropy in terms of the particle number cumulants for non-interacting fermions. We further derive the centered cumulant generating function, demonstrating that the associated probability distribution function in the disc has identical scaling properties, up to a variable shift, to the known ground-state result. Finally, we extend our result to an annular region, showing that both the Rényi entropy and particle number cumulants of the annulus decompose into sums of the corresponding quantities of the two bounding discs. These additive relations hold as long as the width of the annulus is sufficiently large.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces and quantifies the 'entangling power' of neural networks by analyzing polynomial decoders. It shows that this power, $E_p(K)$, grows combinatorially with latent dimension $K$ and decoder degree $p$. The results provide a powerful tool to bound the complexity of quantum correlations achievable through machine learning models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous mathematical framework for understanding how much quantum entanglement (a key resource in quantum information) can be encoded or simulated using classical machine learning architectures, which is vital for designing efficient quantum simulation models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses quantifying the complexity of correlations between subsystems when using neural network representations, moving beyond the limitations of traditional measures like Schmidt rank.

**Main result.** It establishes that the entangling power ($E$) of a polynomial decoder is exactly equal to the number of monomials $N_p(K) = inom{K+p}{p}$, demonstrating exponential entanglement generation with modest resources.

**Method.** The authors use theoretical bounds derived from linear algebra and polynomial theory, relating the rank of a function to the dimension of the space spanned by its polynomial components.

**Model / system.** The model involves representing a correlation function $f(x, y)$ via an encoder-decoder structure: $f(x, y) = g(\phi_A(x), \phi_B(y))$, where $g$ is restricted to be a polynomial of degree $p$.

**Key observables.** Entangling Power ($E$), Schmidt Rank ($R$), Latent Space Dimension ($K$), and Decoder Degree ($p$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relationship between the required minimum latent width ($K_{\min}$), the target rank ($R$), and the decoder degree ($p$) is central, with specific scaling laws provided for different regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses specifically on polynomial decoders of a fixed maximum total degree $p$, contrasting this with general nonlinear decoders which require exponentially large complexity.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the general encoder-decoder template, comparing bilinear (Schmidt) vs. nonlinear decoding and plotting the entangling power $E_p(K)$. Figure 2 compares the required minimum latent width ($K_{\min}$) for a given Schmidt rank across different decoder degrees.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of correlation complexity using NNs, derives the definition of entangling power based on polynomial decoders, establishes necessary and sufficient conditions relating rank to $N_p(K)$, and concludes with specific examples like the Bell state.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Characterizing the complexity of correlations between subsystems is a fundamental task across information theory, machine learning, and science. In quantum physics, neural networks have found increasing application in learning wavefunctions. Here we introduce the entangling power of an encoder-decoder neural network, which quantifies its ability to generate entanglement between subsystems, dependent on a latent space dimension $K$ and the complexity class of the decoder. We exactly calculate this quantity for polynomial decoders of degree $p$ acting on a $K$-dimensional latent space. Our results establish the exponential entangling power of neural networks with modest resources. More broadly, our work provides a framework for analyzing correlations in machine learning that generalizes the notion of the Schmidt rank in entanglement theory.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously establishes a sharp universal bound ($\sqrt{2} \min\{n, m\}$) comparing the trace norm of an operator on a bipartite system to its injective tensor norm. This result has direct physical consequences, providing optimal bounds for quantifying the gap between global and local measures of quantum correlation and improving bounds for quantum data hiding protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental mathematical tools (sharp constants relating different norms) that govern how local measurements constrain global properties in multipartite quantum systems, which is crucial for understanding entanglement quantification and resource theories.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the sharp universal comparison between two norms ($\|z\|_1$ vs. $\|z\|_{\epsilon}$) used to probe bipartite quantum systems, quantifying the loss of information when restricting measurements from global observables to product (local) observables.

**Main result.** It proves that the ratio of these norms is bounded by $\sqrt{2} \min\{n, m\}$, establishing this constant as sharp for both general operators and correlation measures in quantum states.

**Method.** The proof relies on establishing an $L_1$ noncommutative Khintchine inequality using random coefficients derived from a Haar unitary matrix. Applications involve analyzing the gap between global and product correlation measures.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on operators $z \in M_n \otimes M_m$ acting on bipartite quantum systems, particularly in the context of characterizing correlations ($\Deltaho = ho_{AB} - ho_A \otimes ho_B$) within quantum states.

**Key observables.** Trace norm ($\|z\|_1$), Injective tensor norm ($\|z\|_{\epsilon}$), Global correlation measure ($C_{	ext{glob}}(ho)$), Product correlation measure ($C_{	ext{prod}}(ho)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensions $n$ and $m$, and the limiting constant $\sqrt{2}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes $n \le m$ without loss of generality for the main theorems. The proof involves advanced tools from operator theory, including Haar moment formulas.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general norm inequality using Khintchine's inequality; it then applies this result to quantify the gap between global and product correlation measures for quantum states; finally, it extends these bounds to analyze data-hiding ratios.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

To probe a bipartite quantum system, one may use arbitrary global operators or restrict to product operators acting separately on the two subsystems. We determine the sharp universal comparison between the resulting norms. For every $z\in M_n\otimes M_m$, we prove $\|z\|_1\leq\sqrt{2}\min\{n,m\}\|z\|_\varepsilon$, where $\|\cdot\|_1$ is the trace norm and $\|\cdot\|_\varepsilon$ is the injective tensor norm associated with the trace norms on $M_n$ and $M_m$. To prove the upper bound, we establish an $L_1$ noncommutative Khintchine inequality whose random coefficients are the entries of a Haar unitary. We also show that the coefficient $\sqrt{2}$ is sharp. As applications, we show that the same sharp constant governs the gap between bipartite correlation measured in trace norm and that measured by a correlation function, and obtain an improved universal upper bound for quantum data hiding. The upper bound has also been formalized and machine-checked in Lean.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents an experimental demonstration of the Quantum Fourier Transform (QFT) on up to 100 qubits using superconducting hardware. The key contribution is the 'Convolutional QFT' compilation strategy, which efficiently maps the circuit onto a linear nearest neighbor topology while minimizing gate count and depth. This work sets a new record for large-scale quantum algorithm execution fidelity.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses hardware constraints by providing a highly optimized, scalable quantum algorithm implementation for a fundamental subroutine like QFT, which is crucial for realizing complex quantum simulations or computations in AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing an efficient compilation strategy for the Quantum Fourier Transform (QFT) subroutine on quantum hardware with limited qubit connectivity.

**Main result.** The authors achieved the largest experimental QFT demonstrated to date, successfully running up to 100 qubits while maintaining a distinguishable output state above background noise.

**Method.** They introduced and experimentally validated the 'Convolutional QFT,' a novel compilation strategy that maps the QFT onto a linear nearest neighbor (LNN) topology using compact kernel gadgets.

**Model / system.** The experiments were performed on the IBM Quantum Platform, targeting superconducting qubits with an LNN connectivity constraint. The core computation is the QFT circuit itself.

**Key observables.** Process fidelity ($\hat{F}$), success probability of measuring the correct target bitstring, and resource counts (CX gates).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Qubit count ($n$) up to 100; process fidelities measured at $n=50$ (11.4%) and $n=80$ (1.8%).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The reported fidelity is for the unitary QFT alone, after readout error mitigation, and does not account for classical post-processing of near-target states.

**Figures summary.** Figures demonstrate the comparison between various QFT compilation strategies (e.g., Park/Ahn vs. Convolutional) regarding CX gate count and depth, alongside success probability plots showing scalability up to 100 qubits.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the need for efficient LNN compilation, details the derivation of the convolutional strategy using circuit identities, presents quantitative comparisons of resource requirements (Table I), and concludes with experimental validation on the IBM Quantum Platform showing high-qubit fidelity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present and experimentally validate the `Convolutional QFT': a constructive compilation strategy for the Quantum Fourier Transform (QFT) subroutine on a linear nearest neighbor (LNN) qubit topology. We first introduce a novel strategy that compiles the $n$-qubit QFT onto an LNN topology using only $n^2 - n$ $CX$ gates, matching requirements of a direct compilation on an all-to-all architecture. We then derive the convolutional variant used in our experiments, which requires an additional two $CX$ gates in total, and is realized via a compact, translation-invariant kernel circuit gadget that traverses a quantum register. We demonstrate the power of the convolutional compilation strategy on the IBM Quantum Platform by executing QFT benchmarking circuits. We measure a process fidelity of 11.4% at 50 qubits, and 1.8% at 80 qubits. The correct output state remains clearly distinguishable above background noise up to 100 qubits. These results constitute the largest experimental QFT demonstrated on any quantum computing hardware to date.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper calculates the relative entropy of entanglement for a bipartite state obtained by tracing out one subsystem from a tripartite Haar-random pure state. It proves that this entanglement measure scales logarithmically with the system dimensions, showing it closely matches other key entanglement measures like the mutual information. The methodology relies on sophisticated bounding techniques involving classicalization and product overlap estimates.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous quantitative estimates for entanglement measures in highly random quantum states, which is fundamental for understanding resource theories in quantum information processing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is determining the relative entropy of entanglement ($	ext{ER}$) for a bipartite mixed state $ho_{AB}$ derived from tracing out one subsystem (C) of a tripartite Haar-random pure state $|\psiangle_{ABC}$.

**Main result.** The relative entropy of entanglement scales as $\log 	ilde{d} + O(1)$, where $	ilde{d} = \max\{d_C, d_+\}$, indicating that it nearly saturates the minimum of the entanglement of formation and mutual information.

**Method.** The calculation uses bounding techniques involving an upper bound derived from one-sided classicalization (Schmidt dephasing) and a lower bound established via maximal product overlap ($\Lambda(ho_{AB})$).

**Model / system.** The model is a tripartite quantum system governed by a Haar random pure state $|\psiangle_{ABC}$, leading to the reduced mixed state $ho_{AB} = 	ext{Tr}_C |\psiangle\langle\psi|_{ABC}$.

**Key observables.** Relative entropy of entanglement ($	ext{ER}(ho_{AB})$), Entanglement of Formation ($	ext{EF}(ho_{AB})$), Mutual Information ($I(A:B)$), and the maximal product overlap $\Lambda(ho_{AB})$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensions of the subsystems $d_A, d_B, d_C$, particularly in regimes where $d_C \le D = \min\{d_A, d_B\}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are stated to hold with high probability over the Haar measure. The upper bound is achieved by an explicit separable state obtained via one-sided Schmidt dephasing.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing bounds on $	ext{ER}(ho_{AB})$ using both classicalization techniques (upper bound) and maximal product overlap arguments (lower bound), culminating in the main theorem relating these quantities to system dimensions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We determine the relative entropy of entanglement of a bipartite mixed state $ρ_{AB}$ obtained by tracing out one subsystem of a tripartite Haar-random pure state $|ψ\rangle_{ABC}$, finding $E_R(ρ_{AB})=\log\frac{d_Ad_B}{\max(d_A,d_B,d_C)}+O(1)$. Equivalently, the relative entropy of entanglement nearly saturates the smaller of the entanglement of formation $E_F(ρ_{AB})$ and the mutual information $I(A:B)$. The upper bound is achieved by an explicit separable state obtained through one-sided Schmidt dephasing, which is therefore approximately closest.

</details>
