**Summary.** This paper presents a unified theoretical framework to determine the sample complexity required for Quantum State Tomography (QST) when measurements are noisy. It analyzes structured state classes like MPSs and PEPSs, deriving explicit error bounds that quantify the performance gap between estimators that account for noise versus those that ignore it. This work is vital for understanding the fundamental limits of quantum characterization in realistic experimental settings.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous mathematical tools to quantify how much experimental data is needed to reconstruct quantum states accurately when real-world imperfections (noise) are present, which is crucial for designing practical quantum hardware characterization protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a unified theoretical framework to analyze the sample complexity of structured Quantum State Tomography (QST) when measurements are corrupted by various noise sources.

**Main result.** It derives non-asymptotic trace-norm recovery guarantees for two estimators: one that explicitly accounts for calibrated noise, and another 'noise-unaware' estimator which suffers from a persistent deterministic bias term.

**Method.** The analysis uses covering-number techniques to characterize the intrinsic complexity of various structured quantum state classes (like MPSs or low-rank states) under noisy observation models.

**Model / system.** The system involves characterizing unknown quantum states $ho$ for $n$-qudit systems, which are assumed to belong to specific structured classes such as Matrix Product States (MPSs), Projected Entangled-Pair States (PEPSs), or low-rank approximations thereof.

**Key observables.** Trace-norm recovery error ($\|b_ho - ho^\star\|_1$), sample complexity, and the dependence of these metrics on noise levels.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise strengths ($\lambda$ for state preparation, $a$ for measurement), system size ($n$), and the structural complexity parameters (e.g., rank $r$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes physical consistency by applying to structured models like LR-MPOs/PEPOs, and it explicitly models noise using depolarizing channels.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the general problem of QST complexity under noise, develops a unified theoretical framework based on covering numbers for various state structures, and derives explicit non-asymptotic bounds by comparing noise-aware versus noise-unaware estimation strategies.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum state tomography (QST) has attracted considerable attention due to its fundamental role in quantum information processing. In this paper, we develop a unified theoretical framework for analyzing the sample complexity of structured QST under noisy observations arising from state preparation noise, measurement noise, and finite-shot statistical noise. The proposed framework applies to a broad family of structured quantum-state classes, including general mixed states, sparse states, low-rank states, matrix product states (MPSs), matrix product operators (MPOs), projected entangled-pair states (PEPSs), and projected entangled-pair operators (PEPOs), while further introducing physically consistent structured models---including low-rank and sparse states, low-rank MPOs (LR-MPOs), and low-rank PEPOs (LR-PEPOs)---that simultaneously exploit low-dimensional structures and preserve the physical constraint. Within this framework, we derive unified non-asymptotic sample complexity guarantees for two constrained least-squares estimators under noisy observations: a noise-aware estimator that incorporates the calibrated noise model and a noise-unaware estimator based on the ideal Born measurement model. For the noise-aware estimator, we derive unified trace-norm recovery guarantees that explicitly characterize the dependence of the sample complexity on three fundamental quantities: the complexity of the underlying structured state class, the complexity of the measurement ensemble, and the state preparation and measurement noise levels. For the noise-unaware estimator, we establish a unified non-asymptotic recovery guarantee consisting of a statistical error term and an additional deterministic bias term arising from the mismatch between the assumed reconstruction model and the noisy observation process.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel quantum protocol for shadow tomography that estimates expectation values of many observables from limited measurements. It achieves a sample complexity bound that scales only polylogarithmically with the number of observables and crucially ignores the dimension of the state. This resolves an open question in quantum information theory by providing a highly efficient, dimension-free estimation technique.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a significant theoretical advance in quantum estimation theory by achieving dimension-independent bounds for shadow tomography, which is fundamental for characterizing unknown quantum resources.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to estimate all expectation values of a set of observables for an unknown quantum state using measurements on multiple copies, known as shadow tomography.

**Main result.** The authors developed a protocol achieving sample complexity that is polylogarithmic in the number of observables and crucially independent of the dimension of the quantum state.

**Method.** The approach involves reducing the problem to a finite-ensemble estimation via minimax arguments, followed by developing an iterative measurement strategy using sequential pretty-good measurements (PGMs) with posterior updates.

**Model / system.** The system is an unknown $d$-dimensional quantum state $ho$ on a Hilbert space. The process involves performing multiple copies of measurements corresponding to known observables $\{E_i\}$.

**Key observables.** Expectation values $\{	ext{Tr}(ho E_i)\}_{i=1}^M$, the measurement outcomes from sequential PGMs, and the estimated state $\hat{ho}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Accuracy ($\epsilon$), failure probability ($\delta$), number of observables ($M$), and the dimension of the state ($d$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on extending results from finite-state estimation to the full continuous state space using a trace-distance net.

**Paper structure.** The paper first tackles the problem for a finite ensemble, developing an r-round sequential PGM with posterior updates. It then uses minimax arguments and trace-distance nets to lift this result from a finite set of states to the entire quantum state space.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

\textit{Shadow tomography} is a fundamental problem in quantum information theory. Given multiple copies of an unknown $d$-dimensional quantum state $ρ$ and a known collection of observables ${E_1,\ldots,E_m}$, the goal is to estimate all expectation values $\{\Tr(ρE_i)\}_{i=1}^m$ to additive accuracy $\varepsilon$ with probability at least $1-δ$.   An elusive open question from the seminal shadow tomography work of Aaronson (STOC'18) is whether this task admits a dimension-independent sample complexity with only polylogarithmic dependence on $m$, as suggested by the best-known lower bounds. In this work, we give a quantum protocol for shadow tomography with sample complexity \[ O\left( \frac{1}{\varepsilon^2} \frac{(\log (m/δ))^4} {(\log\log (m/δ))^3} \right), \] which is polylogarithmic in the number of observables and independent of the dimension of the unknown state thereby answering Aaronson's original question while also providing an exponential improvement in the prior best dimension independent sample complexity of shadow tomography from Sinha (STOC'25).   Our approach first reduces the general shadow-tomography problem to a finite-ensemble estimation problem via a minimax argument. We then develop an observable-independent protocol that repeatedly applies the pretty-good measurement and updates the priori distribution over the finite ensemble according to the measurement outcomes. A refined tail analysis of the resulting estimation error yields simultaneous accuracy guarantees for all observables.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the scalability bottleneck in running large Quantum Neural Networks by proposing 'late fusion' as a replacement for computationally expensive full state reconstruction. By training subcircuits independently and combining outputs classically, they achieve comparable accuracy at exponentially lower cost. This provides an efficient, noise-robust alternative for implementing complex quantum algorithms on near-term devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on QML, the methodology of characterizing resource requirements using entanglement diagnostics ($S_A$) and establishing theoretical bounds for information recovery has implications for understanding correlations in complex quantum many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is mitigating the exponential computational overhead associated with 'reconstruction' when running large Quantum Neural Networks (QNNs) via circuit cutting on limited quantum hardware.

**Main result.** Late fusion, which uses a small classical head to combine independently trained subcircuit outputs, matches full reconstruction accuracy at exponentially lower cost and shows improved noise robustness.

**Method.** The authors propose replacing complex quasiprobability reconstruction with late fusion—a linear-cost combination inspired by multimodal learning—and introduce a 'quantumness dial' $Q$ to characterize the trade-off.

**Model / system.** The work analyzes variational quantum circuits (QNNs) partitioned into independent subcircuits ('circuit cutting') for machine learning tasks. The comparison is between full reconstruction of expectation values and classical combination of local marginal outputs.

**Key observables.** Accuracy gap between late fusion and full reconstruction, computational cost scaling ($\gamma^{2k}$ vs. constant), and the cut-entanglement diagnostic $S_A$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The quantumness dial $Q \in [0, 1]$ controls the interpolation between pure fusion ($Q=0$) and full reconstruction ($Q=1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The authors do not claim a universal quantum advantage over classical ML; the value proposition is conditional on task structure. They rely on simulating scales where exact reference data is available.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare cost scaling (exponential growth for reconstruction vs. flat late fusion) and show performance tracking across various datasets using different readout strategies.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares multiple readout strategies (reconstruction, partial reconstruction via $Q$, late fusion) by analyzing their computational costs and empirical accuracy on standard benchmarks, culminating in diagnostic tools to pinpoint when fusion is sufficient.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Circuit cutting lets a large quantum neural network (QNN) run as independent subcircuits on small devices, but rebuilding its outputs by reconstruction carries a classical sampling overhead exponential in the number of cuts - the dominant runtime cost in prior work. We ask whether, for machine-learning tasks, this step is necessary, and replace it with late fusion: each subcircuit is trained and measured independently, and a small classical head combines their outputs - a linear-cost, decision-level combination borrowed from multimodal learning. To characterize the trade-off we introduce a quantumness dial $Q$, a tunable reconstruction budget interpolating from pure fusion to full reconstruction, and a cut-entanglement diagnostic that indicates how much reconstruction a task needs (Spearman $ρ=0.59$ over $104$ runs). Across synthetic and standard datasets, independently trained late fusion matches full reconstruction accuracy within $0.04$ at every point of the controlled sweep and on every classical benchmark, at exponentially lower cost; it is also markedly more robust to shot and device noise. Controlled entangled-data experiments locate the boundary where fusion must fail. We do not claim advantage over classical machine learning - consistent with recent benchmarking, quantum offers no accuracy edge on these datasets. Late fusion is thus an efficient, noise-robust, self-characterizing alternative to reconstruction for circuit-cutting QML.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the challenge of online quantum state tomography by analyzing how structural assumptions on measurements can drastically improve learning guarantees. Using advanced online optimization techniques like OGD, the authors derive regret bounds that depend on intrinsic properties (like rank) rather than the exponential Hilbert space dimension. The results show that under favorable conditions, the learnability of quantum states is significantly enhanced.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the practical limitations of quantum state characterization by providing theoretically rigorous methods to achieve high accuracy with limited measurement data, which is crucial for building scalable quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is improving the regret bounds for online quantum state tomography, which is necessary because full state reconstruction is infeasible at scale.

**Main result.** By exploiting structural assumptions on measurements (like bounded Frobenius norm or low rank), the achievable regret bounds are significantly improved compared to general settings. Furthermore, using squared L2 loss in multi-outcome measurements yields a logarithmic regret bound independent of system size.

**Method.** The authors employ online learning frameworks, primarily Projected Online Gradient Descent (OGD) and Regularized Follow-the-Leader (RFTL), adapted for the constraints of quantum state space projection.

**Model / system.** The model involves estimating an unknown quantum state $ho$ over time $T$ using sequential measurements. The process is framed as an online learning task where the learner minimizes regret against an adversarial measurement sequence.

**Key observables.** Regret ($R_T$), which measures performance relative to the best fixed quantum state in hindsight, and the predicted measurement outcomes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structural properties of the measurements, such as bounded Frobenius norm ($\|E_t\|_F \le B$), low rank ($	ext{rank}(E_t) \le r$), or $\kappa$-sparsity.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on assuming specific structural constraints (low rank/sparsity) for the adversarial measurement operators, and assumes convexity and Lipschitz continuity of the loss function.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the general online learning framework using OGD, derives bounds based on structural assumptions (Frobenius norm, rank), and presents a complementary result showing logarithmic regret for multi-outcome measurements under squared L2 loss.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum state tomography is fundamental to quantum information processing but becomes infeasible at scale due to the exponential growth of the state space. Shadow tomography alleviates this challenge by focusing on predicting measurement outcomes rather than reconstructing the full state. Its online variant models adaptive and potentially adversarial measurement scenarios, where a learner sequentially predicts outcomes while competing with the best fixed quantum state in hindsight.   We show that exploiting additional structure in the measurements leads to significantly stronger regret guarantees. In particular, under the assumption that the adversarial measurements have bounded Frobenius norm, we analyze Projected Online Gradient Descent and derive regret bounds that depend on intrinsic structural properties, such as rank or sparsity, rather than the ambient Hilbert space dimension. As a complementary result, we show that one can achieve logarithmic regret, independent of both the number of qubits and measurement outcomes, for multi-outcome measurements under squared $L_2$ loss. These results demonstrate that incorporating realistic structural assumptions can substantially enhance the learnability of quantum states in online environments.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors theoretically investigate NV centers in hexagonal diamond (lonsdaleite) to find better spin qubit candidates than those in cubic diamond. They found that a specific, symmetry-reduced configuration (AB) significantly enhances the coherence time ($T_2$) by leveraging its unique zero-field splitting properties. This establishes lonsdaleite as a promising material for quantum information science applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses hardware platforms for solid-state qubits by predicting enhanced coherence times based on crystal symmetry, which is crucial knowledge for designing robust quantum memory elements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate Nitrogen Vacancy (NV) centers in hexagonal diamond (lonsdaleite) as potential spin qubits with enhanced coherence times compared to cubic diamond counterparts.

**Main result.** The AB configuration of the NV center in lonsdaleite exhibits an approximate fourfold enhancement of the Hahn-echo coherence time ($T_2$) at zero magnetic field due to a finite transverse zero-field splitting.

**Method.** First-principles calculations (DFT) combined with advanced quantum defect theory methods (QDET, gCCE) were used to model electronic structure and spin coherence times.

**Model / system.** The study models negatively charged NV centers ($	ext{NV}^-$) in two configurations (AA and AB) within the hexagonal diamond polymorph lonsdaleite, comparing them against the established cubic-diamond NV center.

**Key observables.** Hahn-echo coherence time ($T_2$), Zero-Field Splitting (ZFS) tensor components ($D$ and $E$), Photoluminescence spectra, and Debye-Waller factors.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Zero magnetic field ($	ext{B}=0$), specific lattice configurations (AA vs. AB), and the magnitude of the enhancement factor (approximate fourfold).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study assumes that symmetry-broken centers in lonsdaleite are viable candidates, though calculating intersystem crossing rates for full optical cycle efficiency was beyond scope.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare crystal structures and defect configurations; one figure shows energy splittings due to symmetry reduction (AB case); another compares PL/Abs spectra for AA and AB configurations.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the electronic structure, coherence properties, and optical signatures of NV centers in lonsdaleite. It contrasts two specific defect symmetries (AA vs. AB) against the cubic standard to pinpoint the optimal configuration for quantum applications.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that negatively charged nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centers in the hexagonal diamond polymorph lonsdaleite offer a route to spin qubits with enhanced coherence relative to their cubic-diamond counterparts. Using first-principles calculations, we examine two distinct defect configurations, AA, with the same symmetry as in cubic diamond and AB, with reduced symmetry. We find that the AB configuration of the NV center exhibits a finite transverse zero-field splitting, giving rise to an approximate fourfold enhancement of the Hahn-echo coherence time $T_2$ at zero magnetic field. The AA configuration, by contrast, closely reproduces the electronic structure and coherence properties of the cubic NV center. We further characterize the many-body electronic structure, vertical excitation energies, and photoluminescence spectra of both configurations, providing spectral fingerprints for their experimental identification. Our results establish symmetry-broken NV centers in lonsdaleite as promising candidates for quantum sensing and information science applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses an open problem in quantum nonlocality concerning whether maximally entangled states are sufficient for all pseudo-telepathic games. The authors construct a specific counterexample game, $G_{4,3,6,6}$, and rigorously prove that this game requires entanglement beyond what is provided by any maximally entangled state. This settles the long-standing question negatively.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work pushes the boundaries of understanding quantum correlations by providing a concrete counterexample in nonlocal games, which has deep implications for characterizing genuine multipartite entanglement beyond simple state characterizations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central question is whether maximally entangled quantum states are sufficient (complete) to guarantee a perfect strategy for every bipartite pseudo-telepathic nonlocal game.

**Main result.** The authors provide a counterexample, $G_{4,3,6,6}$, demonstrating that maximal entanglement is not always sufficient for such games, settling the open question negatively.

**Method.** The proof involves constructing an inner product game and using advanced mathematical techniques, including the NPA hierarchy relaxation solved via SDP, to show the non-existence of a perfect maximally entangled strategy.

**Model / system.** The study uses bipartite nonlocal games, specifically a newly defined class called 'inner product games,' involving input/output sets of sizes 4, 3, and 6 respectively. The analysis is based on quantum correlation measures derived from unitary operators.

**Key observables.** The existence or non-existence of a perfect strategy (probability one) using either an entangled state or a maximally entangled state for the game $G_{4,3,6,6}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Input set sizes (4 and 3), output set size (6), and the specific unitaries defining the inner product games.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof relies on solving a complex SDP derived from assuming the existence of such a state, which is computationally intensive and formalized in Lean.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem context regarding completeness for pseudo-telepathy, constructs the specific counterexample game $G_{4,3,6,6}$ (an inner product game), and then proceeds through mathematical machinery (NPA hierarchy/SDP) to prove that no perfect maximally entangled strategy exists.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

One of the longstanding open problems in quantum nonlocality is to determine if maximally entangled states are complete for bipartite pseudo-telepathic games: namely, if every nonlocal game which admits a perfect entangled strategy admits such a strategy which uses a maximally entangled state. We exhibit a counterexample to this in the form of a bipartite nonlocal game with input sets of size 4 and 3 and output sets both of size 6. This game is part of a new class of nonlocal games, which we call inner product games, which could be of independent interest.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops an effective field theory toolbox for understanding dynamically moving quantum Hall interfaces. It rigorously links the interface's geometric motion to its electrical charge transport using a worldsheet formalism derived from Chern-Simons theory. The resulting framework cleanly separates universal topological kinematics from material-dependent physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on QH physics, the development of a general worldsheet formalism for constrained boundary dynamics is highly relevant to modeling interfaces in other strongly correlated systems or topological materials where geometry dictates charge transport.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of formulating an effective, geometrically consistent theory for quantum Hall (QH) interfaces where interface motion is coupled to charge dynamics.

**Main result.** A systematic framework is derived that separates the universal Hall kinematics of the moving interface from its microscopic geometric and neutral energy contributions.

**Method.** The authors employ a worldsheet description, starting from the two-sided Chern-Simons response and introducing a relative-area construction to derive an equal-time constraint linking charge transport to interface shape.

**Model / system.** The system models Abelian quantum Hall interfaces separating incompressible phases. The analysis uses concepts from topological field theory (Chern-Simons action) applied to the geometry of the moving boundary.

**Key observables.** Normal charge current ($\mathbf{J} \cdot \mathbf{n}$), interface velocity ($v_n$), relative magnetic-area density ($a_\sigma$), and geometric energy terms.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling fraction difference ($\Delta
u = 
u_1 - 
u_2$) and the background magnetic field strength (implied by Chern-Simons coupling).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the electromagnetic Chern–Simons response in a flat sample, excluding terms like Wen–Zee or gravitational corrections.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the Frenet frame and level-set description used for analyzing the QH interface geometry.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the kinematic constraints from the Chern-Simons response, deriving an equal-time constraint via relative area construction, and finally separating the resulting dynamics into universal Hall kinematics and microscopic geometric terms.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A freely moving quantum Hall interface is fundamentally different from an ordinary edge fixed by an external confining potential. Since a normal displacement changes the areas occupied by the adjacent incompressible phases, the interface geometry and charge dynamics cannot be treated as independent degrees of freedom. We formulate this problem for interfaces between Abelian quantum Hall phases using a spatially reparametrization-invariant worldsheet description, in which tangential motion is a relabeling of the interface while normal motion is physical. Starting from the two-sided Chern--Simons response, we derive the relation between normal charge transport and interface motion. We then introduce a relative-area construction, defined with respect to a material reference curve, that converts this velocity relation into an equal-time constraint linking the charged boundary sector to the interface shape. Combined with the folded $K$-matrix current algebra, this identifies the universal Hall kinematics of the moving interface while leaving its geometric energy and neutral dynamics dependent on microscopic interface physics. The resulting framework provides a systematic basis for effective theories of dynamical quantum Hall interfaces.

</details>

### [Finite Quantum Histories: Holonomy Spectra, Minimal Clocks, and Exact Clock-Change Covariance](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05748v1)

**Authors:** Maxim V. Churilov  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.05748v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `non-equilibrium dynamics` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2608.05748_figures/2608.05748_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Lowest spectral branches for a cyclic history of length L = 12. A monodromy eigenphase shifts the clock momentum. Zero energy occurs only at a fixed monodromy vector; a small nonzero eigenphase produces a precisely controlled frustration energy.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper develops an exact formalism for analyzing finite-time quantum histories governed by cyclical unitary evolution. It shows that key physical observables like the spectral gap depend only on the global monodromy operator, not the individual steps. Furthermore, it rigorously classifies the symmetries (clock changes) that preserve the underlying information structure of the history.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly rigorous mathematical framework for understanding how global constraints (like cyclic boundary conditions or total evolution) dictate local observable properties in quantum systems, which is relevant to open system dynamics and measurement theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper solves the problem of characterizing cyclic quantum histories defined by arbitrary time-dependent unitary steps, removing restrictive assumptions about the order of individual steps.

**Main result.** It establishes that spectral properties (gap, trace) are determined solely by the monodromy operator's eigenphases, and it rigorously classifies exact symmetries (clock changes) preserving the history code structure.

**Method.** The analysis employs spectral theory applied to a cyclic history Hamiltonian, relating observables like the spectrum and heat trace to the gauge-invariant properties of the total monodromy operator $M$.

**Model / system.** The system models quantum evolution over a finite cycle time $L$, governed by a propagation Hamiltonian derived from sequential unitary steps $U_t$ acting on a finite-dimensional Hilbert space.

**Key observables.** Monodromy operator ($M$), spectral gap ($\Delta(U)$), history code ($	ext{K}_0$), and the predictive quotient of event alphabets.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cycle length $L$, monodromy eigenphases $	heta_a$, estimation error bounds ($\epsilon, \delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes finite-dimensional unitary steps and focuses on 'sharp' (unitary) clock changes; it distinguishes these from general basis rotations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the lowest spectral branches for $L=12$, demonstrating how a nonzero monodromy phase shifts the clock momentum and controls the frustration energy relative to zero energy.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first analyzing the Hamiltonian structure via the monodromy operator, then deriving closed forms for spectral quantities (gap, trace), followed by characterizing operational equivalence classes of clocks, and finally classifying the symmetries that preserve the history code.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We solve cyclic finite-dimensional quantum histories for arbitrary time-dependent unitary steps, without assuming that one step has finite order. The propagation Hamiltonian is a unitary connection Laplacian on a cycle; its complete gauge invariant is the monodromy $M=U_{L-1}\cdots U_0$. Its spectrum is $λ_{a,k}=1-\cos((2πk-θ_a)/L)$, where $e^{iθ_a}\in\mathrm{spec}(M)$. Thus the exact history sector is isomorphic to $\mathrm{Fix}(M)$, while frustration, the gap above a nonempty zero-energy sector, the determinant, and the finite-temperature trace are obtained in closed form. Ordinary spectral data recover the multiset of monodromy phase cosines but not phase orientation; low energy certifies proximity to an exact relational history. We then define the predictive quotient of a sharp finite clock relative to an accessible operator system as the unique coarsest event alphabet preserving all conditional statistics on a history sector. A finite-error theorem shows that threshold clustering recovers this quotient when the minimum diamond separation of inequivalent event channels exceeds four times the estimation error, and proves an optimal record-count bound. With full matrix access and homogeneous step $U$, the minimal number of clock events is the projective order of $U$. We distinguish the normalizer of the clock algebra from transformations preserving the coherent history code and classify oriented exact sharp clock changes by $U(r)\times\mathbb{Z}_L$ on a rank-$r$ history sector; without orientation the cyclic factor becomes dihedral. Reversible changes of full-information clock fibers are necessarily unitary, so irreversible coarse-graining is not exact clock covariance. Minimal realizations of a complete history Gram kernel are uniquely unitarily equivalent, with a finite-data Procrustes bound. Independent finite-matrix code verifies the main results.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes a multi-state geometric framework using the cQAC tensor to analyze quantum state manifolds. It derives a general sum rule connecting the second-order DC rectification response of insulators to fundamental quantities like the ground state third cumulant and a complex distortion tensor. This provides a powerful, geometry-based tool for understanding nonlinear transport in interacting many-body systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful geometric tool (cQAC tensor) to constrain measurable transport properties (rectification/currents) in complex many-body systems, linking geometry directly to non-equilibrium response.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a theory of multi-state geometry for density matrices and use it to derive general sum rules for second-order rectification response in quantum materials.

**Main result.** A zero-temperature sum rule is derived relating the frequency-integrated DC rectification response to the ground state third cumulant minus a complex distortion tensor, generalizing known single-particle results to many-body systems.

**Method.** The authors introduce the cQAC tensor, a quantum generalization of the Amari-Chentsov tensor, and use time-dependent perturbation theory on thermal density matrices to establish geometric connections.

**Model / system.** The work applies to general many-body systems, extending beyond clean, noninteracting zero-temperature limits. Specific applications include insulators modeled by a generalized Kane-Mele model.

**Key observables.** Second-order DC rectification response, ground state third cumulant, complex distortion tensor, shift current, and nonlinear Hall currents.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Zero temperature (T=0) is the primary focus for deriving exact sum rules; low-temperature measurements are discussed in relation to gap corrections.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory aims to generalize beyond assumptions of clean, noninteracting systems, but derivations rely on perturbation theory and specific geometric formalisms like the QAC tensor.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes; however, numerical verification is mentioned using a generalized Kane-Mele model.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the multi-state geometry framework via the cQAC tensor, derives the general zero-temperature sum rule for rectification response, specializes this rule to currents like the shift current, and finally discusses the temperature dependence of the result.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The geometry of quantum states has emerged as a key ingredient in understanding the linear and nonlinear responses of quantum materials. To date, however, the connection between geometry and nonlinear response is best understood for clean, noninteracting systems at zero temperature. In this work, we develop a theory of multi-state geometry for density matrices and use it to derive sum rules for second-order rectification, making no assumptions about the strength of disorder or interactions. We first show that perturbation theory for thermal density matrices gives rise to two dual information-theoretic connections and an almost complex structure. We introduce a complex, quantum generalization of the Amari-Chentsov tensor of classical information theory, the cQAC tensor, which captures the multi-state geometry of the perturbed density matrix. We derive a zero-temperature sum rule for the frequency-integrated DC rectification response of an insulator as a difference between a ground state third cumulant and the complex distortion tensor, a multi-state geometric quantity built from the cQAC tensor. This generalizes known single-particle sum rules for the shift and nonlinear Hall currents to many-body systems and general perturbations. Specializing to the shift current, we resolve the geometric contribution for multiband insulators into particle-like and hole-like terms. We verify the sum rule numerically in a generalized Kane-Mele model, finding that the geometric contribution can dominate the integrated response. Finally, we show that although the splitting of the sum rule into cumulant and geometric contributions does not survive at nonzero temperature, the measured sum rule for insulators differs from its zero-temperature form by corrections exponentially small in the gap, allowing low-temperature rectification measurements to probe the multi-state geometry of insulators.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops the theory required to achieve fault-tolerant quantum computing using high-dimensional nuclear spins encoded in a spin cat code. The core contribution is the construction of universal logical gates that are 'Error-Transparent' (ET) to phase errors, ensuring that operational noise remains correctable by subsequent Quantum Error Correction steps. This provides a concrete path toward scalable quantum computation in solid-state systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly detailed theoretical roadmap for realizing fault-tolerant quantum computation in solid-state spin qubits, directly addressing the critical issue of noise resilience in physical implementations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is constructing quantum gates for high-dimensional nuclear spin systems that are fault-tolerant against phase errors, which dominate noise in donor-in-silicon architectures.

**Main result.** The authors construct a universal set of Error-Transparent (ET) logical gates and demonstrate that ET operations are crucial for achieving optimal error correction capacity necessary for full fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Method.** The work involves theoretical construction of gate sets, formalizing the Error Transparency criterion ($\left[\hat{\mathcal{E}}_j, \hat{H}(t)ight]|\psi_Langle= 0$), and analyzing the fault tolerance of measurement and error correction gadgets.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes high-dimensional nuclear spins (e.g., in donor atoms in silicon) encoded into a spin cat code, which provides intrinsic robustness against phase errors. Control is achieved via ESR and NMR/NER driving fields.

**Key observables.** Error propagation paths, fault counts ($t(I), t(A), t(O)$), logical gate fidelity relative to ET vs non-ET gates, and measurement POVM operators (CAN, DUAL).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The code distance $p = 2r + 1$ and the error order $r$, which dictates the maximum correctable error weight.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Errors are primarily modeled as dephasing errors ($\hat{I}_z$), and the analysis relies on constructing gates that satisfy the ET criterion for fault tolerance across all components (gates, measurement, correction).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate energy level diagrams showing ESR/NMR control; one figure contrasts ET vs non-ET operations; another details the implementation of a logical CZ gate using multiple conditioned pulses.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops from establishing the physical platform and error model, to constructing the universal set of ET gates (including $Z(	heta)$ and $CZ$), and finally proving fault tolerance for measurement and error correction circuits based on these ET operations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

High-dimensional nuclear spins offer a hardware-efficient route to quantum error correction (QEC), with the spin cat code providing intrinsic robustness against phase errors -- the dominant noise channel in donor-in-silicon architectures. However, realizing the full potential of this encoding requires gate operations that preserve its error-correcting properties. In this work, we construct a universal logical gate set that is error-transparent (ET) to phase errors, and discuss its practical implementations and challenges. The ET gates ensure that phase errors occurring stochastically during gate operations are propagated in a systematically traceable manner and remain correctable in a subsequent QEC step. Among the universal gate set constructed, we identify the logical $X$ gate as the primary challenge and discuss potential realization schemes. In addition, to fully leverage the spin cat code's advantage over an unencoded qubit, multi-tone microwave driving of the logical $CZ$ gate is essential. Our simulations show that ET gates significantly outperform non-ET gates and may be necessary to surpass the break-even point. We further show how logical measurement and recovery can be constructed from ET operations, and explain why state-preparation cannot be made ET. In particular, ET measurement in the computational basis is realizable via spin parity measurement, and that error correction circuits constructed from ET operations achieve optimal error correction capacity. Our work charts a concrete path toward full fault-tolerant quantum computation with high-dimensional nuclear spin systems.

</details>

**Authors:** Hexiang Zhang, Mauro Antezza, Yi Zheng  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** disordered systems and neural networks · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.06203v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `non-equilibrium dynamics` **2/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5**

**Summary.** This paper proposes using near-field radiative heat exchange to build functional neural networks without any electrical circuits. By employing phase-change materials in diodes and transistors, they create a Thermal CNN (T-CNN) for pattern recognition and a T-RNN for memory. This establishes a new, energy-conserving computing paradigm where temperature gradients act as the carriers of information.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a novel platform where thermal dynamics, phase transitions, and non-equilibrium heat transfer govern information processing, offering insights into energy-efficient computation far removed from traditional electronic models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a non-electronic, contactless computing paradigm for artificial neural networks by replacing electrical charge carriers with heat transfer.

**Main result.** The authors successfully establish the first radiative realization of thermal deep learning, demonstrating that temperature fields can carry intelligence to perform complex computations.

**Method.** They model the network using near-field radiative coupling between components like diodes and transistors made from phase-change materials (PCMs).

**Model / system.** The system is a programmable Near-Field Radiative Thermal Network (NRTNN) comprising T-CNN for spatial processing and T-RNN for temporal memory. It relies on near-field photon tunneling between nanoscale surfaces, utilizing the temperature-dependent emissivity of PCMs like VO2 and GST.

**Key observables.** Heat flux ($\mathbf{h}$), radiative coupling coefficients (synaptic weights), and logic states $\{+1, 0, -1\}$ (Absorbing, Neutral, Releasing).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate temperature distributions ($T_G$), PCM relaxation time ($	au_s$), and the amplification factor ($\alpha$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The computation is modeled entirely through near-field radiative heat exchange, assuming that thermal behavior can be accurately mapped onto a neural network structure.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the basic units: radiative thermal diodes (rectification) and transistors (gating). Later figures detail the T-CNN/T-RNN structures incorporating temporal recurrence via hysteretic emissivity.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically builds from fundamental components (diodes, transistors) to complex architectures. It first details the spatial processing (T-CNN), then extends this to include time dependence and memory (T-RNN), culminating in a detailed derivation of the total loss function $\mathcal{J}$ for training.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose and analyze a programmable near-field radiative thermal network that emulates convolutional and recurrent operations through heat exchange. By integrating radiative thermal diodes and transistors composed of phase-change materials such as VO2 and GST, we construct two fundamental architectures: the Thermal Convolutional Neural Network (T-CNN) and the Thermal Recurrent Neural Network (T-RNN). The T-CNN executes spatial pattern recognition via temperature-dependent emissivity modulation, where each gate acts as a tunable radiative filter that amplifies or suppresses localized heat flux. The system is externally programmable via gate temperature distributions in VO2/GST, enabling weighted summation and nonlinear activation; hysteretic transitions provide state dependent responses that can be harnessed for memory. The T-RNN extends this functionality temporally, embedding radiative feedback and hysteretic phase transitions to realize memory and sequential inference. Together, these systems exhibit tri-state logic behavior, radiative gain, and non-volatile thermal storage, key attributes of physical learning. Each logical node operates without charge carriers or electrical circuits, relying solely on near-field photon tunneling between nanoscale surfaces. The resulting framework enables direct implementation of convolution, memory retention, and feedback learning within a contactless, energy-conserving platform. This study establishes the first radiative realization of thermal deep learning, revealing a new computing paradigm where temperature fields function as carriers of intelligence, uniting heat transfer, memory, and adaptive inference in a single non-electronic system.

</details>

**Summary.** The researchers observed unusually large anomalous shifts in specific Feshbach resonances of potassium-39 atoms trapped by an optical dipole trap. They determined these shifts arise from a substantial differential ac Stark shift, pointing to molecular polarizabilities four to seven times larger than expected. This finding highlights the sensitivity of resonance spectroscopy to near-resonant laser coupling with underlying molecular transitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly sensitive probe into the dynamic polarizabilities of weakly bound molecules, revealing strong coupling mechanisms that deviate significantly from simple predictions based on atomic polarizabilities. This is crucial for high-precision quantum control using optical traps.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study reports observing large anomalous shifts of specific Feshbach resonances in potassium-39 atoms confined within an optical dipole trap.

**Main result.** These large shifts are attributed to unexpectedly large dynamic polarizabilities of the weakly bound Feshbach molecules, which are significantly larger than previously expected for such systems.

**Method.** The shift is measured by tracking the resonance position as a function of the optical trap depth and temperature, relating this shift to differential ac Stark effects.

**Model / system.** The system involves ultracold potassium-39 atoms interacting via Feshbach resonances in an optical dipole trap (ODT). Theoretical models use coupled-channel scattering approaches involving singlet and triplet molecular potentials.

**Key observables.** Anomalous resonance shifts (up to +7.5 G), the dependence of these shifts on ODT depth, and the extracted dynamic polarizabilities ($\alpha_{mol}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Potassium-39 ($^{39}	ext{K}$), Feshbach resonances at 33.6 G and 39.9 G, trapping laser wavelength (1063.9 nm), temperatures up to $\sim 35 \mu	ext{K}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a spherically symmetric 3D-Gaussian trapping potential and uses thermal averaging over the trap depth.

**Figures summary.** Figures show calculated resonance positions, Zeeman energy level curves for molecular states, and plots detailing how the measured shift depends on the ODT trap depth and temperature.

**Paper structure.** The paper first reports the observation of large shifts, then analyzes the dependence on trapping parameters to isolate the source (differential ac Stark shift), and finally attributes this effect to a near-coincidence between the laser frequency and a molecular transition.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report the observation of large anomalous shifts, up to +7.5 G, of the positions of the 33.6 G and 39.9 G Feshbach resonances in potassium-39 atoms confined in a 1063.9 nm optical dipole trap (ODT) at temperatures up to around 35 μK and trap depths up to about 136 μK. When the atom cloud is cooled to lower temperatures, by reducing the trap depth of the ODT, the shifts decrease proportionally with trap depth and approach zero at zero depth. We show that the large observed shifts originate from a large differential ac Stark shift between the incoming pair of potassium-39 atoms and the weakly bound Feshbach molecule, which in turn originates from an unexpectedly large dynamic polarizability of the Feshbach molecule. The polarizabilities of the Feshbach molecules extracted from the measured shifts of the 33.6 G and 39.9 G resonances are about four to seven times the sum of the polarizabilities of the two incoming potassium-39 atoms, that is, about four to seven times larger than the usual polarizability of weakly bound Feshbach molecules. The large polarizabilities of the Feshbach molecules are attributed to a near-coincidence between the frequency of the 1063.9 nm ODT laser and the frequency of a molecular transition from the last vibrational level of the lowest triplet a3Σ+u potential to a vibrational level of the excited b3Σ+g potential. Other potassium-39 Feshbach resonances we have studied exhibit a zero or very small shift, corresponding to molecular polarizabilities close to the sum of the polarizabilities of the two incoming potassium-39 atoms.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.05294v1>

**Summary.** This paper constructs and analyzes a lattice model for the $S_3$ quantum double gauge theory, generalizing known self-dual $\mathbb{Z}_2$ systems. By employing three independent theoretical methods, the authors map out the phase diagram, predicting critical edge states described by the Ising CFT and identifying multiple distinct gapped phases in the bulk.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical framework for understanding how topological order breaks down in non-Abelian gauge theories, connecting lattice models directly to continuum CFT descriptions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to generalize the concept of electric-magnetic self-duality from $\mathbb{Z}_2$ gauge theories to non-Abelian systems, specifically analyzing phase transitions in $S_3$ quantum double models.

**Main result.** The model exhibits a gapless critical edge state described by the tetracritical Ising CFT, and the bulk undergoes three independent gapped phase transitions driven by bosonic perturbations.

**Method.** The authors employ multiple complementary theoretical approaches: category-theoretic anyon condensation, microscopic lattice Hamiltonians analysis, and Chern-Simons-Higgs (CSH) theory to characterize the phase diagram.

**Model / system.** The system is a lattice model for the $S_3$ quantum double $\mathcal{D}(S_3)$ on a tensor product Hilbert space. It generalizes the self-dual $\mathbb{Z}_2$ gauge theory and is constructed to be sign-problem-free.

**Key observables.** The existence of gapless critical edge states, the nature of the three distinct gapped phases in the bulk, and the realization of the $\mathbb{Z}_2$ anyon-permutation symmetry.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling constants ($\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \lambda_3$) governing bosonic perturbations; lattice size for numerical checks.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proposal introduces a 'minimal-condensation principle,' assuming that proliferating a bosonic anyon generically drives condensation of a minimal condensable algebra.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows the phase diagram for $D(S_3)$ and its dependence on three perturbation parameters $(\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \lambda_3)$. Table I details critical boundaries and neighboring gapped phases.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a unified picture by analyzing transitions using three independent methods (anyon condensation, lattice Hamiltonians, CSH theory). It establishes the minimal-condensation principle and extends the model to infinite families of dihedral quantum doubles $\mathcal{D}(D_{2n})$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electric-magnetic self-duality of the $\mathbb{Z}_2$ gauge theory, realized microscopically as a half-lattice-translation exchanging electric charge and magnetic flux, has been an influential example of a duality symmetry with an exact lattice realization. We construct the first non-Abelian generalization of this construction: a lattice model of the $S_3$ quantum double $\mathcal{D}(S_3)$ on a tensor product Hilbert space in which the $\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{em}}_2$ anyon-permutation symmetry, exchanging the non-Abelian chargeon $C$ and fluxon $F$, is realized via lattice translation. Consequently we find that the zigzag boundary termination of the model realizes, without fine-tuning, a gapless critical edge state described by the tetracritical Ising CFT. The bulk admits three independent $\mathbb{Z}_2^{\mathrm{em}}$-preserving bosonic perturbations, driving $\mathcal{D}(S_3)$ into distinct gapped phases. We analyze these transitions by three independent methods: category-theoretic anyon condensation, microscopic lattice Hamiltonians, and Chern-Simons-Higgs theory, which all agree, yielding a unified picture. These examples motivate a minimal-condensation principle: proliferating a bosonic anyon generically drives condensation of a minimal condensable algebra containing it, with symmetry-related condensates appearing as degenerate vacua that spontaneously break the anyon-permutation symmetry. Our model construction extends to an infinite family of self-dual dihedral quantum doubles $\mathcal{D}(D_{2n})$. Notably, each model is sign-problem-free, opening the door to large-scale numerical exploration of the phases of non-Abelian Chern-Simons-Higgs theories.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper treats the popularity distribution of given names in the USA and France as a physical system analogous to wealth inequality. By applying tools from statistical mechanics—specifically Rayleigh-Jeans thermalization theory—the authors demonstrate that name frequencies exhibit remarkable long-term stability. This suggests that naming preferences follow universal, quantifiable thermodynamic laws.

**Why it may be interesting.** Although applied to sociology, the reliance on established concepts like RJ condensation and entropy conservation provides a novel, non-standard application of statistical physics principles to complex empirical time series.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study analyzes the long-term statistical distributions of given names in the USA and France, treating name popularity as an analogue to wealth distribution.

**Main result.** The name frequency distributions exhibit remarkable stability over more than a century, suggesting they are governed by universal physical principles like Rayleigh-Jeans (RJ) thermalization and condensation.

**Method.** The authors apply concepts from statistical mechanics used in analyzing wealth inequality, such as the Lorenz curve, Gini coefficient, and Pareto distribution, to historical name data.

**Model / system.** The system is an analogy: given name frequency ($f_m$) is mapped onto wealth ($w_m$), and the underlying dynamics are modeled using concepts derived from energy conservation in physical systems (RJ thermalization).

**Key observables.** Lorenz curves, Gini coefficient (stable range of 0.85-0.95), Pareto distribution parameters, and Pearson correlator coefficients ($ho$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time scale exceeding 100 years; the RJ model parameters ($T, \mu$ or $a, \epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that name frequency distributions can be accurately modeled using thermodynamic analogies derived from wealth inequality analysis.

**Figures summary.** Figures show Lorenz and Pareto curves for US/FR names across various years, demonstrating high Gini coefficients and stability. Correlator plots (Fig. 9) track year-to-year correlations.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the analogy between name frequency and wealth distribution, applies statistical tools (Lorenz/Gini), models the dynamics using RJ thermalization theory, and validates this model by fitting parameters to historical data from both countries.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Using official government data sets of USA and France we analyze the occurrence/frequency/popularity distributions of given names on a time scale of more than 100 years. These distributions are characterized through the Lorenz and Pareto curves broadly used in the analysis of wealth inequality in the world. These curves remain stable during the considered time period with the Gini coefficient remaining in the narrow range 0.85-0.95. As for the case of wealth inequality, we show that the distributions of names are well described by the Rayleigh-Jeans (RJ) thermalization and condensation phenomenon well studied in various physical systems. The RJ thermalization results from two integrals of motion being analogous to energy and probability norm conservation in physical systems with energy states corresponding to popularity levels of names. Time correlations between names are also determined showing their stability until the middle of the twentieth century and a significant change after that.

</details>

### [Time-Dependent Hamiltonian Simulation with Optimal Query Complexity](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06094v1)

**Authors:** Boyang Chen, Minbo Gao, Xinzhao Wang, Shuo Zhou  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.06094v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **2/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2608.06094_figures/2608.06094_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: State flow for finite reuse. In panel (a), the same scaled catalyst is reused and the public outputs recombine into UC|ψ0⟩. Panel (b) starts at |z0⟩= 0; |zℓ⟩and |pℓ⟩are the private input and public output of call ℓ. After F † N, the K = 0 public component is PN|ψ0⟩. Gray and blue lines denote public and private components, respectively.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a query-optimal algorithm for simulating general time-dependent quantum Hamiltonians using the HAM-T model. By utilizing advanced transducer techniques and error analysis involving weighted circuit combinations, they achieve a complexity that matches theoretical lower bounds. This is significant because it proves that modeling time dependence does not incur an asymptotic overhead in terms of required queries.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides state-of-the-art results on quantum simulation complexity, directly impacting how efficiently we can model time evolution in complex physical systems using limited query access models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Determining the optimal query complexity for simulating a general time-dependent Hamiltonian $H(t)$ over a finite time interval $[0, T]$.

**Main result.** The authors achieve an algorithm with query complexity matching the known lower bound, demonstrating that general time dependence does not introduce asymptotic overhead compared to time-independent cases.

**Method.** They employ a novel one-query transducer framework combined with a weighted combination of reuse circuits to ensure factorial decay in simulation error.

**Model / system.** The system is governed by quantum dynamics dictated by $H(t)$, and the target operation is the time-ordered propagator $U_H(T)$. The analysis is conducted within the time-indexed block-encoding-access model (HAM-T).

**Key observables.** Query complexity, simulation error ($\epsilon$), and the evolution time scale ($\alpha T$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Lipschitz constant ($eta$) of $H(t)$, total time $T$, and desired precision $\epsilon$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The Hamiltonian $H(t)$ is assumed to be Lipschitz continuous, and the analysis operates in the worst-case black-box query complexity setting.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates state flow for finite reuse, showing recombination with a scaled catalyst ($\Gamma|\psi_0angle/\sqrt{N}$) versus zero private input, demonstrating error control.

**Paper structure.** The work progresses by first establishing the optimal query complexity using a transducer framework, then refining the error analysis through weighted combinations of reuse circuits to achieve factorial precision decay. Later sections explore continuous-time analogues and subspace constructions for theoretical completeness.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We give a query-optimal algorithm for simulating a general $n$-qubit time-dependent Hamiltonian $H(t)$ on $[0,T]$, assuming that $H$ is Lipschitz continuous and $\|H(t)\|\leqα$. In the standard $\mathrm{HAM\mbox{-}T}$ access model, the algorithm approximates the time-ordered propagator $U_H(T)$ to error $\varepsilon$ using $$ O\left(   αT+\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)}   {\log(e+\log(1/\varepsilon)/(αT))}   \right) $$ $\mathrm{HAM\mbox{-}T}$ queries. This matches the known query lower bound for time-independent Hamiltonians, showing that time dependence incurs no asymptotic query overhead.   Our method first constructs a one-query transducer that, given an auxiliary state, implements an approximation to $U_H(T)$ and returns the state unchanged. A weighted combination of circuits that apply the transducer different numbers of times makes the error caused by omitting this state decay factorially, yielding the stated optimal precision dependence. For time-independent Hamiltonians, the same method also gives a query-optimal alternative to qubitization.

</details>

### [Quantum Amplitude Estimation for Travel Time Estimation in Stochastic Vehicle Routing Problems](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06145v1)

**Authors:** Xingyue Wang, Monika Filipovska  
**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.06145v1>

**Summary.** This paper applies Quantum Amplitude Estimation (QAE) to estimate expected travel times in stochastic vehicle routing problems, offering a theoretical quadratic speed-up over classical Monte Carlo methods. By encoding the entire probability space into a quantum superposition, it provides a powerful tool for hybrid quantum-classical optimization frameworks in intelligent transportation systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on optimization/transportation, the core technique—using quantum amplitude estimation for expectation value calculation—is a direct application of advanced quantum algorithms relevant to simulating complex physical systems or calculating expectation values in open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is accurately estimating expected path travel times within Stochastic Vehicle Routing Problems (VRP) in complex transportation networks.

**Main result.** Quantum Amplitude Estimation (QAE) provides a theoretical quadratic speed-up over classical Monte Carlo simulation for this estimation task, with the Iterative AE variant showing stable performance.

**Method.** The study proposes using QAE to encode all feasible travel time realizations into a quantum superposition state, avoiding sampling and prior distribution assumptions.

**Model / system.** The problem is modeled on Stochastic Transportation Networks (STNs) represented as directed graphs with discrete random link travel times. The implementation uses IBM's Qiskit framework.

**Key observables.** Estimation accuracy, Mean Absolute Error (MAE), and the performance comparison across four QAE variants (CAE, IAE, MLAE, FAE).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The choice of rotation-angle range ($	ext{[0, 2\pi]}$ vs $	ext{[0, \pi]}$) and scaling method significantly impacts estimation accuracy.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work focuses only on the expectation-estimation subproblem; it does not solve the global combinatorial VRP. The results are currently limited by NISQ device noise.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate circuit structures (e.g., 4-qubit toy CAE circuit) and heatmaps showing mean absolute error across various scaling ranges and rotation-angle ranges for different distributions.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the computational bottleneck of classical VRP solving in STNs, then details the QAE methodology to estimate expected travel times, compares four specific QAE variants, and finally analyzes parameter sensitivity (scaling/rotation angle) using experimental results on a small-scale network.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Solving the Vehicle Routing Problem (VRP) in Stochastic Transportation Networks (STNs), a core task in Intelligent Transportation Systems (ITS), introduces estimation challenges for stochastic path travel times and the resulting VRP objective function. These challenges have typically been addressed through computationally expensive sampling-based techniques such as Monte Carlo simulation, whose performance depends on sample size, the sampling strategy, and the underlying travel time distributions. To address these issues, this study proposes and validates a quantum computing technique, Quantum Amplitude Estimation (QAE) for path-level travel time estimation in STNs. Without relying on sampling or prior assumptions of the travel time distribution, the proposed framework encodes all feasible travel time realizations into a quantum superposition, enabling a theoretical quadratic speed-up over Monte Carlo simulation. Four QAE variants are implemented in IBM's Qiskit framework, namely Canonical AE (CAE), Iterative AE (IAE), Maximum Likelihood AE (MLAE), and Faster AE (FAE), together with four rotation-angle scaling strategies for handling different discrete travel time distributions. Experiments on a small-scale STN show that the choice of scaling method and rotation-angle range significantly affects estimation accuracy, while the four QAE variants produce comparable estimates across all tested conditions, with IAE exhibiting the most stable overall performance. The results provide practical guidance on parameter selection for future hybrid quantum-classical optimization frameworks in ITS applications.

</details>

### [Quantum One-Way Functions and Related Cryptographic Primitives](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05754v1)

**Authors:** Georgios M. Nikolopoulos  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.05754v1>

**Topic relevance:** `entanglement & information structure` **2/5** · `quantum measurements` **2/5**

**Summary.** This paper provides a detailed review of quantum cryptographic primitives beyond simple key distribution, aiming to establish quantum analogues for classical one-way functions. It compares different theoretical constructs—such as QOWFs and pseudorandom states—by analyzing their security under various computational and information-theoretic models. The work is crucial for advancing the field toward practical, physically secure quantum cryptography.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly theoretical, the discussion of state distinguishability (as seen in Chunk 3's analysis of phase averaging) touches upon fundamental concepts of quantum measurement and state preparation that are central to understanding noise resilience in any physical implementation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper reviews quantum cryptographic primitives beyond Quantum Key Distribution, focusing on finding quantum analogues to classical one-way functions.

**Main result.** It compares various primitives like QOWFs, OWSGs, and PRSGs, clarifying their conceptual relationships while emphasizing the need for physical realizability in security proofs.

**Method.** The approach is a comprehensive review comparing computational vs. information-theoretic notions of quantum one-wayness using established principles from quantum information theory.

**Model / system.** The discussion centers on abstract quantum states and their manipulation (e.g., encoding classical bits into quantum states) rather than a specific physical Hamiltonian or experimental setup.

**Key observables.** Security assumptions, physical realizability, robustness to noise, and the distinguishability between different ensembles of quantum states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension of the Hilbert space ($d$), error probability in measurements, and the amount of extractable classical information ($	ext{I}_{	ext{acc}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** It distinguishes its focus on primitives relying fundamentally on quantum state properties from post-quantum cryptography based on classical hardness assumptions.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention that relationships between various cryptographic primitives are summarized in Table 1, though the table content is not provided.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews QOWFs and related primitives (OWSGs, PRSGs) by comparing them to classical OWFs, discussing both computational and information-theoretic security models, and concluding with open problems.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum cryptographic primitives beyond key distribution remain a less well understood area of research. In classical cryptography, one-way functions underpin nearly all standard cryptographic protocols, motivating the search for meaningful quantum analogues and for a clear understanding of the physical and computational mechanisms that could enforce one-wayness. In this article, we review quantum one-way functions and a range of closely related quantum-state primitives, including one-way state generators, pseudorandom quantum states, and efficiently indistinguishable pairs of states. We discuss both computational and information-theoretic notions of quantum one-wayness, emphasizing the different adversarial models and security assumptions that underlie these constructions. We compare and contrast the various proposed primitives, and clarify their conceptual relationships. Particular emphasis is placed on questions of physical realizability, experimental feasibility, and robustness to noise. Finally, we outline open problems and future directions toward the development of practical quantum cryptographic primitives beyond key distribution, and the emergence of a broader quantum-cryptographic ecosystem.

</details>

**Authors:** Miguel Hoyuelos  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.06143v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper develops and validates a purely thermodynamic theory for calculating transport coefficients in hard-sphere fluids. By relating these dynamic quantities to static, measurable thermodynamic properties, the authors show that their predictions for conductivity, viscosity, and self-diffusion significantly outperform traditional kinetic theories like Enskog's model. This work demonstrates the feasibility of describing complex fluid dynamics using only equilibrium statistical mechanics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical fluids, the methodology—linking dynamic transport properties directly to equilibrium thermodynamics via generalized fluctuation relations—is a powerful theoretical tool applicable to many-body quantum systems where such connections are sought.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a purely thermodynamic framework capable of predicting transport coefficients for hard-sphere fluids, thereby comparing its accuracy against established kinetic theories.

**Main result.** The theory successfully predicts compact expressions for thermal conductivity, viscosity, and self-diffusion that quantitatively reproduce simulation data, significantly improving upon Enskog kinetic theory predictions for self-diffusion.

**Method.** The approach generalizes the Onsager matrix formalism to relate transport coefficients ($\sigma$) to equilibrium thermodynamic properties via scaling relations derived from density variables.

**Model / system.** The system studied is a hard-sphere fluid, analyzed using concepts from statistical mechanics and fluctuation-dissipation theorems. The analysis involves deriving relationships between real and ideal systems through generalized density variables.

**Key observables.** Transport coefficients: thermal conductivity ($\lambda$), viscosity ($\eta$), and self-diffusion coefficient ($D$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Density ($ho$), temperature ($T$), and the thermodynamic factor $\Gamma = rac{ho}{T} (rac{\partial\mu}{\partialho})_T$. The comparison is made across various density regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include applying the Onsager matrix formalism, assuming local thermal equilibrium, and making approximations regarding constant-pressure conditions for certain derivations.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare $\lambda$, $\eta$, and $D$ vs. density ($ho$) between thermodynamic predictions and Enskog kinetic theory against numerical data points; a second figure shows the relative error of these coefficients versus density.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the framework by first introducing the general Onsager matrix, then deriving specific ratios for $\lambda$, $\eta$, and $D$ using scaling arguments based on thermodynamic variables. It concludes with quantitative comparisons against simulation data across different densities.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A thermodynamic theory for transport coefficients in hard-sphere fluids is developed from a general expression for the Onsager matrix. The theory predicts the ratio $σ/σ_{\rm id}$, where $σ$ is a transport coefficient and $σ_{\rm id}$ denotes its dilute-gas value. This ratio depends exclusively on equilibrium thermodynamic properties and can therefore be computed directly from the equation of state. Compact expressions are obtained for the thermal conductivity, viscosity, and self-diffusion coefficient. These expressions quantitatively reproduce simulation data over almost the entire fluid range and, in the case of self-diffusion, significantly improve upon the predictions of Enskog kinetic theory. The results demonstrate that transport coefficients can be accurately described within a purely thermodynamic framework.

</details>
