### [Bell nonlocality from twisted statistics](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06359v1)

**Authors:** Ivana Đorđ ević, Jovan Potrebić, Aleksandra Gočanin, Dragoljub Gočanin  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.06359v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `entanglement & information structure` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2608.06359_figures/2608.06359_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. A schematic representation of the relativistic Bell protocol. Alice and Bob reside in their space-like separated laboratories OA and OB, respectively. The source coupled to the dressed quantum field generates excitations in time- ordered preparation regions O(B) S and O(B) S belonging to the common causal past of OA and OB. The excitations are ar- ranged to propagate towards OB and OA, respectively. Each signal is a single-particle excitation in a state of quantum su- perposition of two orthogonal wave-packets.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work explores how noncommutative spacetime geometry, modeled by 'twisted statistics' in a free scalar field, can generate genuine Bell nonlocality. By analyzing the resulting two-particle state correlations, the authors show that measuring violations of the CHSH inequality serves as an operational signature for the underlying NC structure. This bridges quantum gravity phenomenology with fundamental tests of quantum foundations.

**Why it may be interesting.** It connects fundamental concepts of spacetime geometry (noncommutativity) directly to quantum information resources (Bell nonlocality), offering a novel theoretical tool for probing physics beyond standard QFT assumptions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Investigating whether Bell nonlocality can emerge from a quantum field theory structure modified by noncommutative (NC) spacetime geometry through 'twisted statistics.'

**Main result.** The violation of the CHSH Bell inequality ($S > 2$) is predicted, providing an operational probe for the underlying NC structure encoded in the multiparticle sector.

**Method.** The analysis uses a relativistic Bell protocol setup involving two spacelike-separated laboratories and calculates the CHSH parameter based on state preparation governed by twisted statistics.

**Model / system.** A free real quantum scalar field propagating on the noncommutative Moyal plane. The deformation is introduced via twisted multiparticle statistics, which modifies the exchange symmetry of creation/annihilation operators.

**Key observables.** Violation of the CHSH Bell inequality ($S_	heta$), and the non-local phase $\chi_	heta$ governing the entanglement.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The noncommutative parameter $	heta$, which controls the magnitude of the violation; the momentum separations ($\Delta p, \Delta q$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The state preparation is assumed to be local and causal, involving sequential pulses from a source in the common causal past.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the relativistic Bell protocol setup used for testing nonlocality.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first introducing the NC deformation via twisted statistics (modifying the coproduct/exchange symmetry), then applying this to a two-particle state preparation in a spacelike separated Bell test, and finally calculating the resulting CHSH violation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate Bell correlations for a free real quantum scalar field on the noncommutative Moyal plane. Although the free field dynamics and the one-particle sector remain unchanged, the deformation enters through twisted multiparticle statistics and its Fock-space dressing representation. A classical external source coupled locally to the twist-dressed quantum field prepares coherent superpositions of momentum-pair configurations propagating toward two spacelike-separated laboratories. The momentum-dependent twist phases are generally nonfactorizable and generate entanglement between the corresponding wave-packet modes. We show that suitable local mode measurements lead to a violation of the CHSH Bell inequality. The resulting correlations provide an operational probe of the noncommutative structure encoded in the multiparticle sector of the quantum field.

</details>

### [Robust logarithmic lower bound on shared-resource cost for $f$-routing](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05775v1)

**Authors:** Kevin Bogner  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.05775v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `entanglement & information structure` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2608.05775_figures/2608.05775_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1. One round of f-routing. Time runs downward. Alice receives x and the qubit Q, Bob receives y, and they share the state ρLR prepared before the inputs arrive. Double lines carry the classical inputs. Alice applies Ax to QL, keeps A0, and sends the message CA→B. Bob applies By to R, keeps B0, and sends CB→A. The messages cross simultaneously. If f(x, y) = 0, Alice applies Dx,y A to MA = A0CB→A and outputs the qubit. If f(x, y) = 1, Bob applies Dx,y B to MB = B0CA→B.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes a strong logarithmic lower bound on the shared quantum resource cost required for $f$-routing in one round. By analyzing the protocol's success using advanced tools like state differences and matrix rank bounds, it proves that the required resource dimension scales as $\Omega(n/\log n)$. The result is significant because it quantifies fundamental limitations in distributed quantum computation protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous quantum information-theoretic lower bound on communication complexity, which is fundamental to understanding the limits of distributed quantum computation and secure multi-party tasks.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines a robust logarithmic lower bound on the shared-resource cost required for performing $f$-routing in a one-round quantum communication protocol.

**Main result.** For the inner product modulo 2 function, the shared resource dimension must satisfy $	ext{Edim}(ho_{LR}) \ge \log_2 n - \log_2 \log_2 n - O(1)$.

**Method.** The proof involves converting protocol correctness into rank bounds for matrices derived from quantum state differences (e.g., using the SLD quadratic form) and applying techniques like Fourier expansion and sign-rank analysis.

**Model / system.** The model is a one-round $f$-routing task where Alice and Bob share an arbitrary mixed state $ho_{LR}$ and exchange messages based on inputs ($x, y$) and an unknown qubit. The cost is measured by the logarithm of the smaller marginal support dimension.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the trace-norm difference ($\|ho_0^{x,y} - ho_1^{x,y}\|_1$) and the SLD quadratic form $\chi(x, y)$, which quantify how much the protocol's success depends on the shared state.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key parameters are $n$ (the length of the input strings) and $d = \min\{	ext{rank } ho_L, 	ext{rank } ho_R\}$, which dictates the resource cost $	ext{Edim}(ho_{LR})$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a worst-case error bound of at most 0.09 in both routing cases and relies on approximating complex rank bounds using techniques like Chebyshev polynomials.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the general flow of the one-round $f$-routing protocol, showing the initial shared state preparation followed by simultaneous message exchange.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops its argument by first establishing necessary conditions via information-disturbance tradeoffs (Chunk 2), then refining these bounds using matrix rank analysis and Fourier expansions (Chunk 3), culminating in the specific lower bound for $f$-routing with $	ext{IP}_n \pmod{2}$ (Chunk 1 & 3).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In one-round $f$-routing, Alice receives an $n$-bit string and an unknown qubit, while Bob receives another $n$-bit string. They exchange one simultaneous message each, and the value of $f$ determines which party must recover the qubit. Message lengths, local systems, and local operations are unrestricted. We charge only $E_{\dim}(ρ_{LR})=\log_2\min\{\operatorname{rank}ρ_L,\operatorname{rank}ρ_R\}$, the logarithm of the smaller marginal support dimension of the shared state prepared before the inputs arrive. The state may be arbitrary and mixed. For the inner product modulo $2$, we prove that every protocol with worst-case error at most $0.09$ in both routing cases, measured in the full, unhalved diamond norm, satisfies $d\log_2(2d)=Ω(n)$ for $d=\min\{\operatorname{rank}ρ_L,\operatorname{rank}ρ_R\}$. Thus $d=Ω(n/\log n)$ and $E_{\dim}(ρ_{LR})\ge\log_2 n-\log_2\log_2 n-O(1)$. The closest earlier growing Schmidt-rank lower bound for an explicit routing function assumes zero error in one routing case. Our proof converts correctness into a matrix whose entries have a constant gap between the two routing cases. It approximates this matrix by one whose rank depends on $d$, but not on the dimensions of messages or local systems. A sign-rank lower bound for the matrix of the inner product modulo $2$ completes the argument. A lower bound polynomial in $n$ on $E_{\dim}$ remains open.

</details>

### [Cluster-State Witnesses of Finite-Speed Hidden Influences](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05271v1)

**Authors:** Weikang Li, Mengyao Hu, Dong-Ling Deng, Valerio Scarani  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.05271v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `entanglement & information structure` **2/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2608.05271_figures/2608.05271_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Finite-speed causality geometry. The blue solid lines de- note the ordinary light cone, whereas the orange dashed lines de- note the wider hidden v-cone associated with a finite influence speed v &gt; c in the preferred frame. The set E denotes the early side, whose measurement event(s) may influence both late parties B and C through the hidden influence. The late events are arranged out- side each other’s hidden v-cones, denoted by B ∼C, so no hidden influence can propagate between them during a run. Consequently, conditioned on every non-null input–output event on E, the BC dis- tribution must be Bell-local.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

(b) LC5 appends E, giving E = ADE with the same blind pair. The BC link in each panel denotes a graph-state edge, not a causal connection; no witness term contains both B and C. (c) Ideal white-noise visibility, given by the ratio of the HIC bound to the quantum value SQ, with lower values indicating greater noise tolerance. (d) LC4 projection π4(P) := (R4(P), L4(P)). The fully Bell-local set L, hidden-influence set HICAD, and no-signaling set NS project to the inner square, intermediate octagon, and outer rectangle, respectively....</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops novel witnesses based on linear cluster states to test theories involving hidden influences that travel at a finite speed. By formulating this constraint as a polytope separation problem, the authors derive specific four- and five-qubit inequalities that violate these hypothetical models if measured values exceed theoretical bounds. This establishes cluster graph states as powerful resources for testing causality.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete, experimentally friendly quantum resources (linear cluster states) for testing fundamental assumptions about causality in physics beyond standard Bell tests, which is highly relevant to foundational quantum information theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To formulate a testable constraint for hidden influences that propagate at a finite speed, extending beyond standard Bell tests.

**Main result.** The authors derive new four- and five-partite witnesses using linear cluster states that certify bounds on these finite-speed hidden influence models.

**Method.** The problem is formulated as a projected-polytope separation problem for cluster-state correlations, utilizing linear programming to find facet-defining inequalities.

**Model / system.** The model considers hidden influences propagating at a finite speed (v) in a preferred frame, leading to constraints on multipartite spacetime arrangements. The quantum resource used is the linear cluster state ($|	ext{LC4}angle$ and $|	ext{LC5}angle$).

**Key observables.** Witness values $S_4$ and $S_5$, HIC bounds ($\le 6$ and $\le 10$), and ideal white-noise visibilities ($
u^*$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Finite speed $v$, number of parties (four or five qubits), and the specific structure of the cluster graph state.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes measurement independence, no postselection, and that the exposed faces are facets of the projected hidden-influence polytopes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the finite-speed causality geometry (v-cone vs. light cone) and show projections of the full polytope onto lower-dimensional spaces defined by the witnesses.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical constraint (HIC) to formulating it as a polytope separation problem, deriving specific cluster-state witnesses ($S_4, S_5$), proving their facet status using optimization techniques, and calculating quantitative bounds and visibilities.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Bell experiments rule out local common-cause explanations of quantum correlations, yet they do not exclude hidden influences that travel faster than light while still having a finite speed in a preferred frame. Multipartite spacetime arrangements turn this possibility into a constraint: two late parties that are outside each other's hidden-influence cones must remain Bell-local once the earlier events are fixed. Here, we formulate this constraint as a projected-polytope separation problem for cluster-state correlations, using only marginal data containing at most one late party. From linear cluster states, we construct a four-qubit witness with the bound $S_4\le 6$ and quantum value $4+2\sqrt2$, and a five-qubit witness with $S_5\le 10$ and quantum value $6+4\sqrt2$. We certify that the exposed faces are facets of the corresponding projected hidden-influence polytopes. These results identify linear-cluster graph states as certifiable and experimentally friendly resources for finite-speed hidden-influence tests.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work investigates why quasiparticles break down in the Orbital-Selective Mott Phase (OSMP) using DMFT on a two-band Hubbard model. The key finding is that this breakdown is driven by Local Spin Excitations (LSEs), which are generated by specific components of Hund's coupling. Removing these LSE pathways restores coherent, well-defined quasiparticle behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of LSE-mediated dynamic coupling provides a concrete, microscopic mechanism for correlated electron phenomena like quasiparticle breakdown, which is highly relevant to understanding strongly interacting quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to uncover the microscopic mechanism responsible for quasiparticle breakdown observed in the Orbital-Selective Mott Phase (OSMP), moving beyond simple models of decoupled orbitals.

**Main result.** The authors identify that electronic coupling mediated by Local Spin Excitations (LSEs)—specifically those generated by the spin-flip and Ising components of Hund's coupling—is the fundamental mechanism driving this quasiparticle breakdown in the OSMP.

**Method.** Dynamical Mean-Field Theory (DMFT) is employed to analyze the two-band Hubbard model, calculating spectral functions and correlation metrics across different interaction regimes.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using a two-band Hubbard Hamiltonian on a Bethe lattice. The interactions include Coulomb repulsion ($U$) and Hund's coupling terms ($J_{sf}, J_z$), which are varied to isolate their physical roles.

**Key observables.** Local Spin Excitations (LSE) spectral function ($	ext{ALSE}(\omega)$), Local Two-Qubit Fidelity (LTQF), and the orbital Density of States ($ho_\alpha(\omega)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical parameters are the interaction strengths ($U, J_{sf}, J_z$) and the system's filling level, particularly focusing on the OSMP regime.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis utilizes DMFT applied to an effective single-impurity model; some derivations rely on Hartree-Fock approximations for analytical insight.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of the LSE spectrum across different interaction strengths, showing a sharp weight transfer to the Fermi level only within the OSMP regime. LTQF values are used to diagnose deviations from ideal metallic or insulating states.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem (OSMP breakdown), develops theoretical tools (DMFT, LSE definition), calculates observables across varying Hund's coupling components, and concludes by linking the observed spectral weight transfer directly to LSE mediation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The orbital-selective Mott phase (OSMP) is commonly described as a coexistence of localized and itinerant electrons within effectively decoupled orbitals, but emerging evidence for quasiparticle breakdown points to physics beyond this picture, whose microscopic origin remains unknown. Using dynamical mean-field theory for the two-band Hubbard model, we show that the spin-flip and Ising-type components of Hund's coupling generate local spin excitations (LSEs). These LSEs couple electrons between different orbitals, renormalize quasiparticle lifetimes and binding energies, and thereby destroy well-defined quasiparticles in the OSMP. Removing these two components of Hund's coupling restores coherent quasiparticle behavior and fully decouples the charge dynamics of the two bands. Our results therefore identify electronic coupling to LSEs as the fundamental mechanism driving quasiparticle breakdown within the OSMP.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces Self-Consistent Ultra-Coarse-Graining (SC-UCG) to improve molecular simulations by accurately handling complex internal states. It bypasses the need for manually defined collective variables by deriving state probabilities self-consistently using graph neural networks trained via relative entropy minimization. This allows for robust simulation of phase transitions in systems like biomolecular tetramers.

**Why it may be interesting.** The self-consistent treatment of discrete 'internal states' within a continuum framework is highly relevant for modeling quantum or near-quantum degrees of freedom in condensed matter systems where simple mean-field approximations fail.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is overcoming information loss during coarse-graining (CG) that prevents accurate sampling of complex, multistate phenomena in molecular dynamics simulations.

**Main result.** The proposed Self-Consistent UCG (SC-UCG) method successfully captures collective switching of internal states and accurately reproduces phase transitions even when trained on data from a single temperature.

**Method.** It introduces SC-UCG, which self-consistently infers internal state probabilities using graph message passing derived from minimizing relative entropy. The training utilizes the Multilayer Internal State Consistency (MISC) framework via graph backpropagation.

**Model / system.** The method is applied to a model tetramer system exhibiting a second-order symmetry-breaking phase transition between supercritical racemic and subcritical D/L-rich fluid phases.

**Key observables.** Internal state probabilities, collective switching events, and the order parameter characterizing the phase transition.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (T), coupling strengths derived from UCG interactions, and the structure of the internal states.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core approximation is that the system can be modeled as a mixed ensemble where internal state probabilities are determined self-consistently without relying on pre-defined collective variables (CVs).

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the application demonstrates capturing phase transitions and state switching.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from foundational bottom-up CG theory to introduce SC-UCG as an advancement over RLE approximations. It details the variational derivation leading to a message-passing form (ISC layer) for internal state inference, followed by training via MISC and application to phase transition simulations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Bottom-up coarse-graining expands the length and time scales accessible to molecular dynamics (MD) simulations, but information loss can hinder accurate representation of multistate phenomena in complex biomolecular dynamics. Ultra-Coarse-Graining (UCG) projects discrete "quantum-like" extended degrees of freedom, or "internal states," onto coarse-grained (CG) molecules, extending CG model expressiveness. The rapid-local-equilibrium (RLE) approximation in UCG depends on user-defined collective variables (CVs, e.g., local density) and neglects correlations between internal states within and between CG molecules. We present Self-Consistent UCG (SC-UCG), which uses the underlying UCG interactions directly to assign internal states without designing CVs in the CG ensemble. During simulation, internal state probabilities are determined self-consistently through graph message passing. We enhance the RLE Hamiltonian with the Bethe approximation and AI-based inference to represent explicit correlations between UCG beads. For force-field training, we develop Multilayer Internal State Consistency (MISC), a machine-learning method derived from relative entropy minimization that avoids iterative sampling of intermediate force fields. We apply SC-UCG to a tetramer exhibiting a second-order symmetry-breaking phase transition from a supercritical racemic fluid to subcritical D-rich and L-rich fluids. SC-UCG captures collective switching of internal states in the subcritical region and recapitulates the phase transition across temperatures, despite being trained on a single-temperature dataset.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a general, high-precision experimental platform for searching for nuclear symmetry violations using laser-cooled molecules containing spinful nuclei. By leveraging techniques like Zeeman tuning near state crossings, the authors project sensitivities significantly beyond current limits. This work provides both the theoretical framework and the practical roadmap for next-generation searches for physics beyond the Standard Model in molecular systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work represents a sophisticated integration of quantum optics (laser cooling/trapping) with fundamental physics searches. The detailed theoretical modeling of complex molecular Hamiltonians to predict optimal measurement points is highly relevant to advanced AMO research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is searching for new physics by measuring nuclear P- and CP-violating effects, such as NSD-PV, in molecular systems.

**Main result.** The authors propose a comprehensive experimental toolbox achieving projected sensitivities two orders of magnitude better than current molecular beam methods, applicable to various spinful molecules.

**Method.** The method involves using external magnetic fields (Zeeman tuning) to bring opposite-parity molecular states into near-degeneracy, enhancing the measurement sensitivity via an interrogation technique like Stark-interferometry.

**Model / system.** The system is laser-coolable diatomic molecules carrying spinful nuclei (e.g., ${}^{137}	ext{BaF}$). The theoretical framework involves detailed calculations of molecular Hamiltonians incorporating rotational, hyperfine, and Zeeman interactions in Hund's case ($\alphaeta S$).

**Key observables.** The key observable is the mixing element $ar{W}$ (or matrix element $	ilde{C}$) derived from parity-violating couplings, measured via an asymmetry $A$ proportional to $1/\Delta$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the magnetic field strength ($B$), the energy detuning ($\Delta$), and molecular constants like the permanent electric dipole moment (e.g., 3.17 Debye for BaF).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Major assumptions include neglecting contributions from weak electric fields in certain Hamiltonians and assuming that the dominant uncertainty source is the control over the energy detuning $\Delta$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows the entire experimental apparatus, from laser ablation to MOT capture. Figure 2 details the state structure of ${}^{137}	ext{BaF}$ in a magnetic field, illustrating relevant state crossings.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the general physical platform and required techniques (cooling, trapping). It then applies these methods to benchmark measurements like NSD-PV using specific molecular species (${}^{137}	ext{BaF}$), detailing Hamiltonian derivations and identifying precise magnetic field locations for maximal sensitivity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Cold heavy molecules are promising systems for exploring nuclear $\mathcal{P}$- and $\mathcal{CP}$-violating phenomena in search of new physics beyond the Standard Model. However, most proposed experimental strategies and their early realizations to date have been limited to proof-of-principle molecular species with effectively spin-zero nuclei that are not sensitive to nuclear symmetry-violating phenomena. Here, we introduce a comprehensive experimental toolbox that integrates cooling, trapping, coherent state manipulation, and a complete precision-measurement protocol that is applicable to molecules carrying relevant nuclear spins. Using ${}^{137}$Ba${}^{19}$F and nuclear-spin-dependent parity violation (NSD-PV) as representative species and benchmark application, respectively, our approach achieves a projected statistical sensitivity roughly two orders of magnitude beyond comparable molecular beams by combining techniques already demonstrated individually in current experiments. This level of precision could provide realistic experimental access not only to the enhanced NSD-PV signals arising from the heavy ${}^{137}$Ba nucleus within this molecule but also to the contributions from the lighter ${}^{19}$F nucleus, bringing direct benchmarks of nuclear \textit{ab initio} theory within reach. We further identify a candidate magic wavelength as a route to second-scale rotational coherence in future experiments. The techniques developed here can be transferred to measurements of nuclear Schiff and magnetic quadrupole moments in molecules containing deformed nuclei, establishing a general platform for laboratory searches for nuclear symmetry violations.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors present a compact Dual-Faraday-laser-pumped (DFP) Cesium beam clock designed to achieve superior frequency stability for portable timing systems. By integrating advanced laser stabilization techniques like Modulation Transfer Spectroscopy with Faraday filtering, they successfully suppressed noise sources inherent in previous designs. This results in record short-term stability of $7.7	imes 10^{-13}/\sqrt{	au}$, advancing the state-of-the-art for field-deployable atomic clocks.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work represents a significant engineering achievement in precision measurement using atomic physics principles. The successful suppression of laser noise to reach $10^{-13}/\sqrt{	au}$ stability is highly relevant for quantum sensing and metrology applications that require robust field operation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is improving the short-term frequency stability of compact Cesium (Cs) beam clocks for use in field-deployable timing systems, overcoming limitations imposed by laser noise.

**Main result.** The developed Dual-Faraday-laser-pumped (DFP) Cs clock achieved a fractional Allan deviation of $7.7	imes 10^{-13}/\sqrt{	au}$, pushing stability into the highly desirable $10^{-13}/\sqrt{	au}$ regime.

**Method.** The technique involves using two lasers stabilized via Modulation Transfer Spectroscopy (MTS) and employing an intracavity Faraday anomalous dispersion optical filter (FADOF) for inherent resonance alignment.

**Model / system.** The system is a compact Cs beam clock utilizing two distinct laser pumping transitions ($	ext{F}=4 	o 	ext{F}'=5$ and $	ext{F}=3 	o 	ext{F}'=2$). It relies on low-frequency-noise, atom-referenced lasers stabilized through complex optical feedback loops.

**Key observables.** Clock SNR (46,365 in 1 Hz), fractional Allan deviation ($7.7	imes 10^{-13}/\sqrt{	au}$), and Hadamard deviation ($7.7	imes 10^{-15}$ at 10,000 s).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Lorentzian linewidth of 2.12 kHz; stability improvement from $1.1 	imes 10^{-12}/\sqrt{	au}$ (SFP) to $7.7 	imes 10^{-13}/\sqrt{	au}$ (DFP).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation addressed is the conversion of laser frequency noise into fluorescence noise in standard two-laser pumping schemes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy level diagrams for single vs. dual Faraday pumping, the experimental schematic of the DFP clock setup, and plots detailing stability measurements (ADEV/HDEV) versus averaging time ($	au$).

**Paper structure.** The paper details the physical system components, describes the advanced stabilization techniques (MTS, FADOF), presents quantitative performance metrics comparing different pumping schemes, and concludes with future optimization paths.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Compact cesium beam clocks are major frequency references for deployable timing systems. However, further improvement of their short-term frequency stability is limited by the clock signal-to-noise ratio (SNR). Although two-laser optical pumping can increase the effective atomic utilization, the achievable clock SNR has long been limited by laser-induced frequency-to-amplitude noise conversion. Here, we demonstrate a compact dual-Faraday-laser-pumped (DFP) Cs beam clock enabled by a low-frequency-noise atom-referenced laser architecture. The intracavity Faraday anomalous dispersion optical filter provides inherent alignment to the Cs D$_2$ resonances, while modulation transfer spectroscopy offers suppressed frequency noise and drift. The resulting laser system supports robust turnkey operation with a Lorentzian linewidth of 2.12 kHz. The DFP Cs clock achieves a clock SNR of 46,365 in a 1-Hz bandwidth and a fractional Allan deviation of $7.7\times 10^{-13}/\sqrtτ$ , with Hadamard deviation reaching $7.7\times 10^{-15}$ at 10,000 s. This work pushes the fractional frequency stability of a compact Cs beam clock into the $10^{-13}/\sqrtτ$ regime, providing a pathway toward high-performance Cs frequency references for field-deployable precision timing, navigation, and synchronization.

</details>

**Authors:** Shohei Kiryu, Kazufumi Tanji, Yoshihiro Ueda, Kosuke Fukui, Masahiro Takeoka  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.06239v1>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the fundamental limits of phase estimation using heralded non-Gaussian states generated from Gaussian resources in optical systems. By defining an Effective Quantum Fisher Information (EQFI) that weights by success probability, the authors prove that this resource cannot surpass the QFI limit set by the original deterministic Gaussian input state. This highlights the critical importance of accounting for generation efficiency in quantum metrology.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous resource-counting benchmark for quantum sensing, showing that mere non-Gaussianity is insufficient for advantage if generation probabilities are ignored in linear optics settings.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether using heralded, probabilistic non-Gaussian quantum states offers a genuine metrological advantage over deterministic Gaussian states for single-parameter phase estimation.

**Main result.** It is proven that the Effective Quantum Fisher Information (EQFI) of the heralded non-Gaussian states cannot exceed the Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) achievable with the original Gaussian input state, when resource cost (success probability) is accounted for.

**Method.** The authors define and calculate the EQFI as a success-probability-weighted average of the conditional QFI over all possible detection outcomes from photon number resolving detectors.

**Model / system.** The analysis uses passive linear optical circuits acting on Gaussian input states (like squeezed vacuum) to generate heralded non-Gaussian states, with phase estimation performed by applying a global phase rotation operator.

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information (QFI), Effective Quantum Fisher Information (EQFI), Success Probability ($P(n_B)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The comparison is between $	ext{EQFI}$ and the input $	ext{QFI}$, particularly in regimes involving photon number conservation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes linear phase estimation using passive linear optics, and crucially incorporates the success probability of the heralding process into the performance metric.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate quantum sensing schemes comparing non-Gaussian vs. Gaussian states, showing how the probabilistic nature (heralding) is mapped to an effective measurement strategy.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes a formal mapping between heralded state preparation and postselection after parameter encoding. It then derives the EQFI metric and proves the upper bound ($	ext{EQFI} \le 	ext{QFI}_{	ext{input}}$), using commutation relations to justify this result.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-Gaussian states can exhibit large quantum Fisher information (QFI) in quantum sensing. In optical systems, however, its generation is often probabilistic via the boson-sampling type conditional operation and thus its generation rate is limited. This probabilistic generation of non-Gaussian resource should be taken into account for evaluation of the sensing performance. Then a natural question arising is whether the use of heralded probabilistic non-Gaussian states is better than that of the original deterministic Gaussian states for quantum sensing. In this paper, we answer to this question for single-parameter phase-estimation. By using photon-number conservation in passive linear optical systems, we show that heralded state preparation before parameter encoding can be mapped to a postselection problem after parameter encoding for phase estimation. This mapping allows the success probability of heralding to be included naturally in the metrological performance. We introduce an effective quantum Fisher information (EQFI), defined as the success-probability-weighted QFI of the heralded outputs, and prove that it cannot exceed the QFI of the original Gaussian inputs. The result highlights the importance of resource counting in quantum sensing toward better understanding of the resource efficient advantage of optical quantum sensing.

</details>

**Summary.** This work calculates the phonon spectral function for low-density polaron metals using DMRG. The results demonstrate that the spectral weight shifts significantly across energy scales, contradicting predictions based on the Migdal limit's $2k_F$ kink. The study proposes a 'dressed RPA' as an effective approximation while cautioning about the pitfalls of inconsistent diagrammatic expansions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on lattice models, the study of spectral weight renormalization due to coupling (electron-phonon interaction) is a core concept in understanding emergent collective excitations relevant to strongly correlated systems and superconductivity.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to compute the phonon spectral function of low-density polaron metals, contrasting these results with predictions from established theoretical limits like the Migdal limit.

**Main result.** The calculated spectra show significant phonon weight transfer both below and above the bare phonon energy, crucially lacking the $2k_F$ kink signature expected in standard Kohn anomaly phenomenology.

**Method.** The primary calculation uses Density Matrix Renormalization Group (DMRG), supplemented by theoretical comparisons using the Random Phase Approximation (RPA) and a 'dressed RPA' scheme.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a one-dimensional spinless Holstein model doped with a low but finite carrier concentration ($x \le 0.15$), describing polaron metals interacting via electron-phonon coupling ($\lambda$).

**Key observables.** Phonon spectral function, $B(q, \omega) = -rac{1}{\pi} 	ext{Im} D_R(q, \omega)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low carrier concentration ($x \le 0.15$), electron-phonon coupling ($\lambda$), and the bare phonon energy ($\Omega$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The 'dressed RPA' is proposed as an efficient approximation, but including lowest-order vertex corrections leads to unphysical negative spectral weight, signaling necessary consistent non-perturbative treatment of dressings.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare DMRG results (for $q<0$) against polarization calculations using RPA and dressed RPA variants (for $q>0$), illustrating the transfer of phonon weight across different energy regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem by comparing low-density polaron behavior to known limits. It presents DMRG results, contrasts them with lowest-order RPA predictions, develops an improved 'dressed RPA' scheme, and concludes by discussing the limitations imposed by vertex corrections.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We use the density matrix renormalization group (DMRG) to compute the phonon spectral function of a one-dimensional spinless Holstein model doped with a low but finite carrier concentration, $x \leq 0.15$, as a function of the electron-phonon coupling $λ$. To the best of our knowledge, these are the first such results in this regime, complementing extensive prior work at the single-polaron level ($x\to 0$). We find that significant phonon spectral weight is transferred both below, all the way down to $ω=0$, and above the bare phonon energy $Ω$, in stark contrast with the Kohn-anomaly phenomenology expected in the Migdal limit, where weight remains centered near $Ω$ with a kink at $q=2k_F$. No signature of this $2k_F$ kink appears in our results. This behavior is captured qualitatively by the Random Phase Approximation (RPA), and semi-quantitatively, at negligible extra computational cost, by a ``dressed RPA'' scheme in which the electron addition propagator is renormalized using the Momentum Average (MA) approximation for the low-density electron-polaron. By contrast, adding the lowest-order vertex correction to this dressed scheme produces unphysical negative spectral weight, signaling that vertex and propagator dressings must be treated consistently once the propagators are dressed nonperturbatively. Our results provide an efficient approximation for the phonon spectral functions of low-density polaron metals, a regime relevant to weakly doped insulators.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper models the liquid-superfluid phase transition in helium using an abstract toy model based on dense random graphs. By employing the mathematical formalism of graphons in the infinite system limit, the authors demonstrate how a difference in symmetry can naturally define an order parameter distinguishing the two distinct phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the use of advanced tools like graphons and infinite system limits to define symmetry-breaking order parameters is mathematically sophisticated and relevant to understanding emergent order in complex many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is modeling the phase transition between normal liquid ($	ext{He I}$) and superfluid ($	ext{He II}$) helium-4, focusing on developing a theoretical framework to identify an order parameter.

**Main result.** The authors propose using graph theory in the infinite system limit to show how symmetry differences can define an order parameter distinguishing the two distinct phases.

**Method.** The analysis relies heavily on constructing and analyzing 'toy models' based on dense random graphs, utilizing the mathematical formalism of graphons derived from Lovász et al. [42].

**Model / system.** The system is superfluid helium-4 ($	ext{He}$), modeled abstractly using an edge/triangle graph model defined on $n$ vertices. The analysis maps this to constraints on edge density ($\epsilon$) and triangle density ($	au$).

**Key observables.** Order parameter, entropy (in the infinite system limit), thermal conductivity, and viscosity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Edge density ($\epsilon$), triangle density ($	au$), and the transition point between $	ext{He I}$ and $	ext{He II}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model is a 'toy model' whose formalism (infinite system limits) is not guaranteed to apply to all physical transitions, especially the fluid/solid one.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate simplified P-T phase diagrams for ${}^4	ext{He}$ and $	ext{CO}_2$, and later show the structure of possible phases in the edge/triangle model (e.g., regions A(2,0), B(1,1), F(1,1)) represented by graphons.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical problem ($	ext{He I}/	ext{He II}$ transition) to introducing a mathematical analogy using random graphs and their infinite limits (graphons). It then uses this framework to derive structural differences that serve as order parameters for the superfluid phase.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We are interested in modelling superfluid helium-4, the common isotope of helium, of atomic number 4. We present a mathematically solvable toy model of the phase transition between the normal liquid and superfluid phases and use it to show how an order parameter might be obtained for the superfluid.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper critically reviews Sample-based Quantum Diagonalization (SQD) for electronic structure, arguing that current implementations do not demonstrate a clear quantum advantage over state-of-the-art classical selected configuration interaction methods. It establishes rigorous benchmarking standards and suggests that true quantum utility may only be found in specific regimes, such as robustness to noise or learning from quantum experiments.

**Why it may be interesting.** It directly addresses the computational bottleneck in strongly correlated electron physics by evaluating the practical utility and theoretical limits of hybrid quantum-classical algorithms like SQD against established methods (like DMRG or advanced CI).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is accurately determining the ground-state energy of a many-electron Hamiltonian, which is computationally intractable due to the exponential growth of the full configuration interaction (FCI) space.

**Main result.** The authors report that strong classical selected CI methods match or beat quantum-sampled subspaces in published comparisons, and they establish a regime map defining where a true quantum advantage might exist.

**Method.** The paper critically reviews Sample-based Quantum Diagonalization (SQD) and proposes rigorous benchmarking standards to test the limits of quantum sampling versus established classical correlated electron techniques.

**Model / system.** The focus is on many-electron systems governed by the electronic Hamiltonian, typically studied in chemistry using active spaces defined by orbital occupations. The methods compare QSCI/SQD against classical sCI approaches.

**Key observables.** Ground-state energy ($E_0$), determinant importance distribution, and configuration count required for a given accuracy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The multireference character of the electronic state is highlighted as a potential coordinate distinguishing quantum advantage regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption being tested is whether the sampling process inherent in SQD can overcome the combinatorial explosion better than classical selection heuristics, which is shown to be false under current comparisons.

**Figures summary.** Not specified (The notes describe the workflow and results rather than specific figure contents).

**Paper structure.** The paper critically reviews the existing ecosystem of ML-enhanced quantum samplers, confronts the central question of quantum vs. classical performance using negative results, proposes a benchmarking standard/regime map, and tests this map with FCI-exact experiments.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Sample-based quantum diagonalization (SQD), equivalently quantum-selected configuration interaction (QSCI), has in two years become a pragmatic centre of gravity of pre-fault-tolerant quantum chemistry: a quantum processor samples electronic configurations, and the many-electron Hamiltonian is diagonalized classically in the resulting determinant subspace. Its accuracy is set entirely by which configurations enter that subspace, a selection problem for machine learning made acute by a coupon-collector bottleneck. We critically review the ecosystem of generative and learned selectors, organizing it by the object each method generates and the importance signal it exploits, and expose one conspicuous gap: a reward-proportional generative-flow-network proposer built for tail discovery. We then confront the field's central question -- whether the quantum sampler beats classical selected configuration interaction -- and report a carefully scoped negative: across published same-active-space comparisons, strong classical selected CI matches or beats the quantum-sampled subspace, and the flagship single-layer circuits now admit polynomial-time classical energy estimation. We distil a benchmarking standard and turn the negative into a regime map, then test it with FCI-exact experiments that confirm one prediction and refute another: the cheap prior's rank correlation with the exact weights declines with multireference character (a usable coordinate), but a controlled single-molecule noise sweep shows the one generative advantage we find, robustness to valid-shot starvation, to be generic rather than the multireference-specific effect a confounded contrast first suggested. Finally, we flag learning from quantum experiments, whose classical sample-complexity lower bound is an unconditional theorem, as the one adjacent frontier where a quantum advantage is provable but not yet bridged to chemistry.

</details>

**Authors:** Hongfei Shu, Jingjing Yang

**Summary.** This paper develops a novel theoretical framework to calculate the partition function and spectral zeta function for anharmonic oscillators, including both standard Hermitian and PT-symmetric cases. It achieves this by establishing a direct link between these quantum spectral functions and solutions derived from integrable models via contour integration techniques. This provides powerful analytical tools for studying thermal properties in complex quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous connection established between spectral properties (thermodynamics) and the mathematical structure of integrable systems provides powerful new tools for calculating quantum observables in complex potentials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing a direct mathematical bridge connecting quantum spectral functions (like partition functions and spectral zeta functions) to solutions derived from integrable models.

**Main result.** The authors successfully express the thermal partition function and spectral zeta function for both Hermitian and PT-symmetric oscillators using contour integrals involving the counting function derived from the integrable model's equations.

**Method.** The methodology relies on the ODE/IM correspondence, utilizing the Destri–de Vega equation to find the quantization condition $a(E)$, which then parameterizes the required contour integrals for thermodynamic quantities.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes homogeneous anharmonic oscillators, specifically Hermitian ($V(x) = x^{2M}$) and PT-symmetric potentials. The analysis extends these techniques to calculate thermal properties across different symmetry classes.

**Key observables.** Partition function $Z(eta)$, Spectral zeta function $\zeta_H(s)$ (and $\zeta_{PT}^H(s)$), Ground state energy ($E_0$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inverse temperature ($eta$), Oscillator exponent ($M > 1$), and the spectral parameter $s$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the applicability of the ODE/IM correspondence and relies on asymptotic expansions (WKB) for high-temperature regimes.

**Figures summary.** Tables compare calculated values of the ground state energy ($E_0$) and spectral zeta function ($\zeta(s)$) against established literature results, demonstrating consistency across different methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the formulation for Hermitian oscillators first, deriving contour integral representations for $Z(eta)$. It then extends this framework to PT-symmetric systems, requiring careful selection of Stokes sectors and yielding analogous integral forms for $Z_{PT}(eta)$ and $\zeta_{PT}^H(s)$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop an ODE/IM-based formulation for the thermal partition function and the spectral zeta function of the homogeneous Hermitian and PT-symmetric oscillators. For both classes of systems, the quantization condition can be expressed using the counting function $a(E)$, which can be solved via the Destri-de Vega equation of the integrable model. We then express the partition function and spectral zeta function as contour integrals involving the counting function, thereby providing a direct bridge between quantum spectral functions and integrable models.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work establishes fundamental limits on how much data (sample complexity) is needed to characterize an unknown quantum channel. By analyzing qudit and bosonic systems, the authors show that accessing both the channel $\mathcal{E}$ and its complex conjugate $\mathcal{E}^*$ simultaneously provides a significant advantage over simply having multiple copies of $\mathcal{E}$. This work defines a resource hierarchy for quantum process learning.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous resource quantification for quantum process tomography, showing that simply having multiple copies of a channel is insufficient without complementary information (like conjugate access) to achieve efficient learning scaling.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper derives sample-complexity lower bounds for estimating entries of transfer matrices or functions that characterize unknown quantum channels under limited parallel access resources.

**Main result.** Efficient learning requires specific resource access, such as simultaneous access to the channel and its complex conjugate ($\mathcal{E} \otimes \mathcal{E}^*$), establishing a hierarchy of required quantum information resources for characterization.

**Method.** The analysis uses techniques from quantum estimation theory, deriving lower bounds by comparing the distinguishability of states/channels under different resource constraints (e.g., $c$-copy access vs. conjugate access).

**Model / system.** The study considers quantum channels acting on qudit systems and multi-mode bosonic systems, characterizing them via Heisenberg–Weyl transfer matrices or characteristic-function transfer functions derived from the Choi state.

**Key observables.** Transfer matrix/function entries, sample complexity bounds ($\epsilon^{-k}$ scaling), and distinguishability measures like the diamond distance.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Accuracy $\epsilon$, success probability $1-\delta$, number of copies $c$, and local dimension $d$ (for qudits).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the learner has control over input states and ancillary assistance, allowing for strong lower bounds on required resources.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates a general learning protocol using c-copies of an unknown channel E. Table I summarizes lower bounds across different resource models (c copies vs. conjugate access).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the mathematical framework for characterizing channels on qudits and bosons, then deriving complexity bounds based on limited parallel access ($c$ copies), and finally comparing these bounds against optimal protocols using complex-conjugate access.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum channels can characterized by their action on an orthogonal operator basis, where these operators are related to observable properties of the quantum system. For qudit and multimode bosonic systems, this is encoded respectively in the Heisenberg--Weyl transfer matrix estimated from the Choi state, and in the characteristic-function transfer function estimated from the Choi state generated by probing with a two-mode squeezed vacuum state. We derive sample-complexity bounds for estimating entries of these transfer matrix/function to additive accuracy $ε$ with success probability $\geq1-δ$, under different resources: access to the complex-conjugate channel $\mathcal{E}^*$ and/or simultaneous access to $c$ copies of the channel. In all settings, the learner uses parallel channel calls with adaptively chosen, ancilla-assisted input states and measurements. Absolute values of transfer-matrix entries can be learned efficiently with simultaneous access to $\mathcal{E}$ and $\mathcal{E}^*$, with tight scaling $ε^{-4}$. Without conjugate access, any $c<d$ copies are insufficient for efficient learning, requiring sample complexity exponential in the number of ($d$-level) qudits $n$ (for prime $d$). Efficiency is recovered at $c=d$, with tight scaling $ε^{-2d}$. For bosonic systems, exponential sample complexity persists for all $c=O(1/ε)$. Although the task is learning a particular state, these bounds carry stronger implications than standard state-learning bounds since the learner controls the inputs and ancillary assistance. This establishes a hierarchy of channel-learning resources: self-complex-conjugate channels require two-copy ancilla-assisted access for efficient learning, while for every square-free $d$, some channels require $d$-copy access. As a corollary, we bounds tighter lower bounds for state learning with limited multi-copy access.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.05845v1>

**Summary.** This work compares two methods for calculating liquid shear viscosity from MD simulations, confirming strong agreement between them. The authors demonstrate that the results are robust against finite size effects and propose practical guidelines for scaling simulation parameters ($\tau_{\text{block}}$) to achieve massive computational savings without losing numerical accuracy.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical liquids, the rigorous statistical analysis of error propagation and the development of scaling rules (like adjusting $\tau_{\text{block}}$ by $1/8$ or $1/4$) are highly relevant to optimizing resource-intensive simulations in quantum many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the accurate computation of shear viscosities ($\eta$) for molecular liquids using Molecular Dynamics (MD) simulations, comparing two established theoretical approaches.

**Main result.** Both the OrthoBoXY and Green-Kubo methods yield highly consistent results across a wide range of liquid viscosities. Furthermore, the study provides significant computational recommendations to reduce simulation time without sacrificing accuracy.

**Method.** The viscosity is calculated using MD simulations in the NVT ensemble, comparing the stress tensor autocorrelation function (Green-Kubo) against an approach derived from self-diffusion coefficients (OrthoBoXY).

**Model / system.** The study investigates 15 neat molecular liquids modeled using the OPLS force field. The analysis covers systems ranging across three orders of magnitude in viscosity.

**Key observables.** Shear viscosity ($\eta$), self-diffusion coefficients ($D_0$, $D_{PBC,ii}$), and their associated standard errors.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size (down to 250 molecules), simulation block length ($	au_{	ext{block}}$), and temperature/density conditions for the liquids.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The OrthoBoXY approach relies on a specific geometric relationship involving box lengths, and statistical robustness is achieved by using Variant C averaging method.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the agreement between different viscosity calculation methods (OrthoBoXY vs. Green-Kubo) and show how error estimates are independent of system size due to compensation effects.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem and compares the two main computational methods. It then analyzes the dependence on system size, confirming robustness down to small systems. Finally, it proposes detailed, quantitative refinements for setting the simulation block length ($	au_{	ext{block}}$) based on liquid viscosity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We calculated shear viscosities of 15 neat molecular liquids from equilibrium molecular dynamics (MD) simulations using the OrthoBoXY approach and compare them to viscosities calculated via the Green-Kubo method. Data from both methods agree very well. Here, we show how to avoid pitfalls while computing the OrthoBoXY-data to obtain optimal results. From simulations of multiple system sizes, we could verify that the viscosity of molecular liquids is not influenced by finite size effects down to systems as small as 250 molecules. Moreover, we demonstrate that also the standard error of the viscosity is nearly independent of the system size. This is shown to be a consequence of a compensation effect of an increasing accuracy of the self-diffusion coefficients with increasing systems-size and the system-size dependent weighting according to the OrthoBoXY-equation. As a consequence, we suggest that it is preferable to run simulations of smaller systems with longer simulation times rather than larger systems with shorter simulation runs. In addition, we discuss a refinement of the recently introduced "recipe" for OrthoBoXY simulations block-lengths $τ_\mathrm{block}$. Based on data from simulations with varying run-lengths, we suggest the following modification: for highly viscous systems, the value of $τ_\mathrm{block}$ might safely be scaled by a factor of $1/8$, significantly reducing the computational resources needed. For less viscous systems, the value of $τ_\mathrm{block}$ might safely be scaled by a factor of $1/4$. For systems with high fluidity, the value of $τ_\mathrm{block}$ should not be scaled down in order to achieve reliable results. When using a smaller system size of 250 molecules, these refinements are leading up to a 24-fold reduction in computational cost compared to the previous recommended set-up without sacrificing numerical accuracy.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an Interferometric Quantum Polynomial Chaos Expansion (QPCE) as a novel generative model for simulating particle calorimeter showers. By encoding the simulation into a quantum circuit, it leverages the depth of the circuit rather than classical coefficients to capture complex statistical dependencies. This approach allows the model to reproduce subtle structural features of real detector data that are inaccessible to standard classical generative models.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on HEP, the core methodology—using quantum circuits to encode complex statistical dependencies (chaos expansion) for generative modeling—is highly relevant to simulating non-equilibrium dynamics or complex many-body states where classical sampling fails.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing a fast, accurate generative model for simulating high-energy physics calorimeter showers, which are computationally intensive using traditional methods.

**Main result.** The proposed Interferometric Quantum Polynomial Chaos Expansion (QPCE) successfully models the complex dependence structure of shower data, reproducing non-trivial structural properties like tail asymmetry that classical models cannot capture.

**Method.** It employs a quantum circuit as a generative model, where randomness enters via rotation angles. The training involves minimizing an energy distance between generated and target distributions using adjoint gradients.

**Model / system.** The system is the simulation of particle showers in calorimeters. The model is the QPCE, which uses a single quantum circuit whose depth dictates the order of the chaos expansion for latent variables.

**Key observables.** Tail asymmetry ($\lambda_L - \lambda_U$), mean absolute correlation $|ho_S|$, and structural properties like rank statistics ($D$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit depth ($L$) acts as the truncation order; training uses L-BFGS optimization.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the circuit structure itself can encode complex, non-classical correlations necessary for accurate simulation.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare structural correlation measures (Spearman rank) and tail behavior ($\Lambda$) between Geant4 data, QPCE simulations, and actual hardware measurements.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the QPCE framework by contrasting it with classical PCE. It details the circuit ansatz, describes the training/generation/verification pipeline, presents results showing structural fidelity on both simulated and physical hardware, and concludes by discussing the model's inherent advantages in attribution.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present the quantum polynomial chaos expansion, a generative algorithm in which a single circuit is the entire model, and we use it to learn calorimeter images. In a classical chaos expansion the randomness is the input and the coefficients are fitted. Here the randomness is still the only input, entering the circuit as rotation angles and re-uploaded at every block, so that each measured observable is a chaos expansion of the latent variables whose order equals the circuit depth, and what is fitted are the gate angles themselves. Expressivity therefore grows with depth rather than with classical coefficients, correlations between outputs arise only from entangling gates, and a single latent wire read by all qubits carries the collective mode of the data. Nothing fitted stands between the circuit and the sample, so switching the entanglers off is a setting of the model itself and provably yields independent outputs, and attribution of the learned correlations to individual gates becomes a measurement. Choosing between two measurement bases shot by shot sharpens attribution into certification, and the trained model violates the Bell bound obeyed by every classical generative model with local response, whatever its size. We train the model on Geant4 shower data, execute the identical circuit on a superconducting processor with its accuracy loss predicted in advance, prove a no-go theorem for the tail dependence of every smooth generator read out through expectation values, and identify the circuit primitive that removes this limit.

</details>

**Authors:** Bao G. Bach, Ilya Safro, Filip B. Maciejewski  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** disordered systems and neural networks · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.06212v1>

**Summary.** This work proposes using local correlators from the QAOA algorithm to 'warm-start' the Burer-Monteiro relaxation for solving the MaxCut problem. The results show this quantum-informed initialization provides a significant speedup, quickly reaching high-quality solutions compared to random starts. However, it highlights an exploitation/exploration tradeoff, suggesting that while quantum assistance is useful for rapid initial progress, exhaustive classical search might still yield marginally better final results.

**Why it may be interesting.** It demonstrates a concrete pathway for near-term quantum utility by showing how limited quantum circuit outputs can provide valuable structural guidance to classical optimization solvers tackling hard combinatorial problems like MaxCut.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to improve classical optimization heuristics for the MaxCut problem by incorporating structural information derived from quantum computation.

**Main result.** A warm-start initialization using QAOA local correlators provides a significant early advantage in finding high-quality solutions quickly, though random multi-start methods can eventually surpass it with sufficient computational budget.

**Method.** The authors propose a hybrid strategy where low-depth QAOA outputs (local correlators $\langle Z_i Z_j angle$) are used to initialize the Burer-Monteiro rank-two relaxation solver for MaxCut, which is then compared against random initialization strategies.

**Model / system.** The problem studied is the weighted MAXCUT problem on a graph $G(V, E)$, formulated as an Ising model ($\mathcal{H}_C$). Benchmarks include random Erdős–Rényi (ER-10) graphs and Sherrington-Kirkpatrick (SK) spin glass models.

**Key observables.** Best found energy, paired difference in best energy vs. baseline, WinRate (probability of matching or exceeding baseline performance).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Graph size ($n=500$ and $n=1000$), QAOA depth parameter ($p=1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The comparison is limited to the BM refinement stage, not an end-to-end quantum advantage; the warm-start relies on mapping low-depth correlators to initial parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 shows performance vs. iteration budget for ER-10 graphs ($n=500, 1000$), illustrating WS's early lead and MultiStart's eventual crossover. Figure 3 presents similar results for the SK model.

**Paper structure.** The paper details deriving QAOA correlators to initialize BM relaxation, compares this warm-start approach against random multi-starts on ER and SK ensembles across varying iteration budgets, and analyzes the resulting exploitation/exploration tradeoff.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum optimization has attracted growing interest as quantum hardware continues to improve, yet state-of-the-art classical solvers remain a formidable benchmark for practical utility. Rather than seeking a fully quantum replacement for classical optimization, we propose a hybrid strategy that uses quantum information to enhance leading classical heuristics. Specifically, we introduce a warm-start method based on local correlators obtained from the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA), and use this information to initialize the Burer-Monteiro (BM) rank-two relaxation. We demonstrate numerically that, compared to a random, multi-start initialization baseline (a standard strategy used for BM), this quantum-informed initialization offers a significant head start, i.e., high-quality solutions with very small number of iterations, for two problem classes -- random Erdős Rényi graphs with edge density of $10\%$ (ER-10) and fully-connected Sherrington Kirkpatrick (SK) spin glass models, at $n=500$ and $n=1000$ qubits. At the same time, given enough iterations, the random baseline often eventually catches up and slightly outperforms the warm-start strategy on average, an effect visibly stronger for $n=500$ than for $n=1000$. The results demonstrate an exploitation/exploration tradeoff of using WS to quickly arrive at very good solutions vs exploring slightly better solutions with a larger iterations budget via a standard strategy. Our results highlight how low-depth quantum circuits can provide useful structural information for classical optimization and suggest a promising route toward near-term quantum utility through quantum-assisted initialization.

</details>

### [A Scale-Invariant Theory of the Universe](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05929v1)

**Authors:** Julian Barbour, Maria I. R. Lourenço  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.05929v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2608.05929_figures/2608.05929_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: Two planar central configurations of 5,000 equal-mass particles. The configuration on the left lies at, or very close to, the absolute minimum of the variety, with V=0.585701. The configuration on the right corresponds to a higher critical point, with V=0.595574.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a radical ontological shift by eliminating absolute concepts like time and space using Leibniz's principle of sufficient reason. It develops the 'variety' ($V$) as a fundamental, scale-invariant measure of structure within gravitational N-body systems. The core argument suggests that physical evolution is better described by changes in this internal structural complexity rather than external temporal passage.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about quantum information or AMO systems, its focus on relational ontology and deriving physical laws from intrinsic symmetries (like scale invariance) echoes foundational questions in quantum gravity and emergent spacetime theories relevant to advanced theoretical physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central goal is to eliminate absolute structures (like absolute time, position, and scale) from physics by adopting a relational ontology guided by Leibniz's principle of sufficient reason.

**Main result.** This leads to a formulation based on scale-invariant ratios, using the 'variety' ($V$) as a central measure of structure that unifies previously distinct solutions in Newtonian gravity.

**Method.** The authors apply relational principles (like PSR) to reduce physical descriptions to dimensionless ratios, focusing on critical points of a defined structural variety $V$.

**Model / system.** The analysis is initially applied to the gravitational N-body problem under Newtonian gravity but proposes general principles applicable to General Relativity and quantum mechanics.

**Key observables.** The 'variety' ($V$), which measures structure via ratios of characteristic lengths ($\ell_{rms} / \ell_{mhl}$), and the derived quantity representing structural 'age', $a(s)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dimensionless ratios, total energy ($E=0$), zero angular momentum ($L=0$), and the number of particles ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The universe is treated as a closed system necessitating scale-invariant descriptions; the program assumes that removing absolute structures enriches physical theories.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate planar central configurations for N-body systems, showing points near the minimum variety value ($a=0$) and those at higher critical points representing different structural states.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the need to eliminate absolute structures (Newtonian context), then developing the mathematical tool of the scale-invariant 'variety' $V$ using N-body dynamics, and finally arguing for the interpretation of time/evolution based on increasing structure ($a(s)$) rather than external time.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Modern physics has achieved extraordinary empirical success while retaining much of the absolute, unobservable structure introduced by Newton, largely without questioning its necessity. We investigate how far this structure can be eliminated by adopting a relational ontology guided by Leibniz's principle of sufficient reason. Removing absolute position, orientation, time and, finally, scale leads naturally to a formulation of the gravitational $N$-body problem where only dimensionless ratios are physically meaningful. Within this framework, the scale-invariant variety $V$ becomes a central quantity, providing a measure of structure, a natural ordering of shapes, and an emergent gravitational arrow of time. We argue that the resulting formulation unifies classes of Newtonian solutions previously regarded as distinct, uncovering a possible new symmetry, suggests a notion of explanation based on timeless spatial correlations rather than temporal evolution, and points towards a more economical ontology. Although developed in the context of Newtonian gravity, the principles proposed here may also offer a new perspective on general relativity and quantum mechanics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses how mapping symmetric quantum circuits onto fixed hardware topologies causes symmetry-breaking due to uneven routing costs. The authors prove that if the physical patch geometry allows it, this imbalance can be eliminated cost-free by making specific compiler assignments. This suggests that optimizing for balanced resource distribution is a crucial, often overlooked, objective in quantum compilation.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides concrete, actionable guidelines for quantum compiler design by shifting focus from minimizing total depth to ensuring equitable resource distribution across symmetric components of the circuit.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Mapping quantum circuits with inherent symmetries onto physical hardware restricts connectivity introduces 'routing overhead' that breaks theoretical symmetry due to unequal error accumulation across identical logical roles.

**Main result.** When patch geometry permits, achieving a perfectly balanced distribution of routing costs is possible without increasing circuit depth, suggesting this imbalance is primarily a compiler optimization choice rather than an unavoidable hardware limitation.

**Method.** The authors combine theoretical analysis (exhaustive search on lattice patches) with quantitative simulation using realistic noise models to measure symmetry-breaking metrics.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes quantum circuits requiring cyclic symmetries (e.g., four-part or six-part rings) mapped onto a 57-qubit 'heavy-hex' lattice structure.

**Key observables.** Routing Imbalance (measured as variance/spread of per-role measurements), Decoherence-corrected symmetry-breaking reduction ($\hat{E}$ and $\mathcal{E}_{	ext{rel}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The size and shape of the physical qubit patch (e.g., path vs. star-shaped) relative to the required circuit symmetry.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that compiler optimization should prioritize even cost distribution over simply minimizing average depth, especially when geometry allows for balance.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the difference between balanced (zero spread) and concentrated assignments on paths, and show simulated error distributions demonstrating significant reduction in symmetry-breaking via balancing.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem of symmetry breaking due to routing overhead, develops a two-level model distinguishing shape constraints from assignment choices, proves topological constraints using exhaustive search for specific ring structures, and validates findings through noise simulations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Mapping quantum programs to restricted physical chips requires SWAP operations, incurring depth and error penalties. In symmetric programs, this routing overhead breaks theoretical symmetry because identical logical roles experience unequal shuffling. While often attributed to hardware topology alone, we show this is a two-level phenomenon. A qubit patch's shape dictates if it can host a balanced assignment. When balance is possible, the actual imbalance is set by the logical-to-physical assignment, meaning a balanced assignment can distribute routing costs perfectly evenly at no extra depth. Through exhaustive search on a 57-qubit "heavy-hex" lattice, we prove these topological constraints. For a four-part ring, 108 of 124 connected patches admit a cost-free balanced assignment, with the 16 exceptions being star-shaped. For a six-part ring, cost-free balance is impossible on compact patches. For a fully connected four-part symmetry, balance is structurally impossible at any depth. Simulations using realistic error rates show that, relative to the worst-case concentrated assignment, balanced assignments reduce symmetry-breaking by 92.7% (95% CI [+89.8%, +95.3%]) for the raw metric and 87.0% (95% CI [+79.6%, +94.1%]) for the decoherence-corrected measure (p = 2.45 x 10^-32). Substrate error heterogeneity accounts for at most 10.8% of this effect. Notably, switching to the compiler's highest generic optimization level did not yield a statistically significant change in routing imbalance, highlighting the need for targeted symmetry-aware passes. When patch geometry permits, routing imbalance is a compiler choice rather than a hardware limitation. Thus, symmetry-aware assignment should be a primary objective for compiler optimization and chip design.

</details>
