**Summary.** This paper rigorously characterizes the algebraic structure of Clifford circuits that can preserve the encoding properties of CSS quantum error-correcting codes. By decomposing these circuits into fundamental diagonal and CNOT layers, the authors determine which specific codes allow their logical operations to be generated by relatively simple 'two-fold transversal' gates. This is crucial for designing efficient, fault-tolerant quantum computation protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep structural insights into the gate sets required for fault-tolerant quantum computation, which underpins many theoretical models of quantum information processing relevant to AMO physics platforms like trapped ions or superconducting qubits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper characterizes the structure of Clifford circuits necessary for implementing fault-tolerant logical operations within Calderbank–Shor–Steane (CSS) quantum error-correcting codes.

**Main result.** It establishes that code-preserving circuits decompose into products of diagonal gates, and it identifies 78 CSS codes whose full logical Clifford group is generated by two-fold transversal circuits.

**Method.** The authors use algebraic methods involving symplectic geometry to analyze the constraints imposed on circuit blocks (Z-diagonal, X-diagonal, CNOT) required for code preservation.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on CSS quantum error-correcting codes, which are analyzed using a symplectic formalism where Pauli operators are represented by labels $(x|z)$. The circuits implement logical gates acting on these encoded qubits.

**Key observables.** The size and structure of the logical Clifford group generated by specific circuit families (e.g., two-fold transversal group).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code parameters such as distance (3, 4, 5, etc.) and rate ($2/5$, $3/4$, etc.).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the relevance of Clifford circuits for fault tolerance and relies on decomposing general code-preserving maps into specific product structures.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates four key Clifford gate families (Z-diagonal, X-diagonal, CNOT, partial duality) for a CSS code. Figure 2 schematically details the generating sets for different gate groups ($\mathcal{N}_{\emptyset}$, $\mathcal{N}_M$, $\mathcal{N}_{2	ext{fold}}$, $\mathcal{N}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by characterizing four distinct families of circuits (code-preserving, transversal, etc.), deriving their normal forms using algebraic constraints, and finally applying these results to enumerate specific CSS codes whose logical group structure is determined.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We characterize four groups of Clifford circuits for Calderbank--Shor--Steane (CSS) codes that are relevant to fault-tolerant logical operations. First, we show that every code-preserving Clifford circuit is a product of Z-diagonal circuits, composed of S and CZ gates, and their X-basis analogues. Second, we define the two-fold transversal group, generated by depth-one two-local code-preserving circuits, and show that each of its elements can be expressed as a product of layers consisting of either Z-diagonal, X-diagonal, or CNOT gates. As a corollary, every transversal gate is a product of three transversal diagonal circuits; for connected non-self-dual codes, two such circuits suffice.   We further show that every code-preserving automorphism circuit, consisting of single-qubit Clifford gates and permutations, has a normal form comprising a Hadamard layer, a permutation, and two diagonal circuits. We also define a two-fold automorphism group, in which a depth-one two-local circuit may be code-preserving up to a permutation, and show that its logical image can be larger than that of the two-fold transversal group.   For 136 CSS codes, we provide explicit generators and determine the logical image of the two-fold transversal group. We find 78 codes whose full logical Clifford group is generated by two-fold-transversal circuits, including codes of distances 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, and 12, with respective rates $2/5$, $3/4$, $1/9$, $1/5$, $2/5$, and $3/56$. We construct three families of CSS codes from bipartite grids, cut-complements, and quadrics, many of which realize the full logical Clifford group in this way. More generally, the induced logical group can be large even when it is not full logical Clifford group: it has order at least $460\,800$ for the gross code and roughly $10^{26}$ for a clustered-cyclic code.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes error correction in Time-Entanglement Quantum Key Distribution (TE-QKD) to determine conditions for high reliability. It establishes that by using specific error-correcting codes and soft decoding techniques, the system can achieve 'infinite diversity' despite having a physically finite channel diversity. This result is significant because it demonstrates a coding advantage unmatched in classical communication theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a novel theoretical result showing how coding theory can fundamentally alter the asymptotic performance limits of quantum communication channels, achieving an infinite diversity gain unattainable in classical fading models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is establishing the conditions under which an error-correcting code can achieve infinite diversity order in Time-Entanglement Quantum Key Distribution (TE-QKD) reconciliation.

**Main result.** The paper proves that for soft-decision decoding, infinite diversity is achieved if and only if the underlying code possesses the Maximal Finite Diversity (MFD) deficient property. This behavior is unique compared to classical fading channels.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving necessary and sufficient conditions using asymptotic limits of error probabilities ($P_e$) with respect to the Signal-to-Noise Ratio ($\gamma$), comparing algebraic vs. soft decoding approaches.

**Model / system.** The system is TE-QKD, where raw key bits are encoded across time bins affected by Gaussian noise modeling detector timing jitter. The analysis uses coded error-correcting codes (e.g., Golay, BCH) to process this noisy quantum channel output.

**Key observables.** Diversity Order ($d$), Reconciliation Error Probability ($P_e(\gamma)$), and the Maximal Finite Diversity (MFD) property.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Signal-to-Noise Ratio ($\gamma$), number of photons per codeword, and the decoding radius.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes independent photon-pair realizations and memoryless detector jitters across channel uses. The analysis focuses on high SNR asymptotic behavior.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the frame structure in TE-QKD, showing the physical positions of photons before and after being corrupted by detection jitter noise.

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines the diversity order based on error probability decay. It then analyzes two decoding regimes: bounded-distance algebraic decoding (deriving necessary/sufficient conditions) and soft-decision decoding (introducing and utilizing the MFD property).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We establish conditions and give proofs on how an error-correcting code can attain infinite diversity in a time-entanglement quantum key distribution (TE-QKD) reconciliation. The shocking result, never encountered in the literature on coding and communication theory, is that a decoder exhibits an infinite diversity order while the channel has finite diversity and the code has a relatively short finite length.   This paper studies the diversity order of coded TE-QKD reconciliation, defined by the asymptotic slope of the error probability at high signal-to-noise ratio. For bounded-distance algebraic decoding, we derive a necessary and sufficient condition in terms of the number of photons per codeword and the decoding radius.   For soft-decision decoding, we introduce the maximal finite diversity (MFD) property and prove that infinite diversity is achieved if and only if the code is MFD deficient. The infinite diversity in TE-QKD has no counterpart in classical fading channels, where decoding can only multiply a finite diversity order by a finite factor.   Examples of short codes based on Golay, Reed-Solomon, Bose-Chaudhuri-Hocquenghem (BCH), and Reed-Muller codes validate the analysis and illustrate how the TE-QKD system parameters and the relatively short code parameters affect the achievable diversity for both hard and soft information reconciliation.

</details>

**Authors:** Dahye Kim, Monika Filipovska  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.05394v1>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the challenge of applying quantum computation to highly constrained routing problems like TSP and VRP. It introduces a novel QAOA+ framework that enforces route feasibility directly into the quantum mixer operator, moving beyond relying on penalty terms. The results demonstrate that structurally enforcing constraints during search leads to significantly better sampling behavior than traditional methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete example of how structural constraints from classical combinatorial optimization can be mapped into quantum circuit design principles (the mixer operator) to guide the search space, which is relevant for designing algorithms for complex physical simulations or constrained state preparation in quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a quantum search method for constrained transportation routing problems (like TSP/VRP) that inherently preserves feasibility during the quantum search process.

**Main result.** The proposed constraint-aware QAOA+ framework, which embeds feasibility logic into the mixer operator, shows superior convergence and probability concentration on feasible routes compared to standard penalty-based QUBO approaches.

**Method.** The authors compare three architectures: penalty-based QUBO-QAOA, a penalty-free QAOA+ using a custom feasibility-preserving mixer (quantum neighborhood analogue), and a hybrid approach.

**Model / system.** The model is the constrained transportation routing problem (TSP/VRP). The quantum implementation uses the Quantum Alternating Operator Ansatz ($	ext{QAOA+}$) structure, where constraints are enforced via structural modifications to the mixing operator rather than just penalty terms in the cost Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Feasibility preservation, sampling efficiency, convergence behavior, and probability concentration over low-cost feasible routes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The choice of constraint-handling architecture (penalty vs. mixer enforcement) is the key variable tested across small TSP/VRP instances.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Computational experiments are limited to controlled small-instance studies on a classical quantum simulator, and results should not be extrapolated to large-scale quantum advantage claims.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews the limitations of standard QUBO encodings for routing problems, proposes the QAOA+ framework with a custom mixer, compares three constraint architectures (penalty-based, penalty-free mixer, hybrid), and analyzes results on small TSP/VRP instances.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Transportation routing problems such as the Traveling Salesperson Problem (TSP) and the Vehicle Routing Problem (VRP) are characterized by strict feasibility requirements involving customer assignment and visit rules, route sequencing, and depot-return logic alongside cost minimization. Most quantum routing formulations adopt Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) encodings, where feasibility is incorporated indirectly via penalty terms in the cost Hamiltonian. While convenient for standard implementations of the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA), QUBO encodings allow the quantum search dynamics to allocate substantial probability to infeasible route configurations. This study develops a transportation-grounded constraint-aware Quantum Alternating Operator Ansatz (QAOA+) framework that embeds feasibility-preserving logic directly into the search operator. We introduce a custom mixer that functions as a quantum analogue of feasibility-preserving routing neighborhoods, using column-wise swap moves, it restricts evolution to feasible configurations while enabling structured exploration of valid routes. We compare three constraint-handling architectures: penalty-based QUBO QAOA, penalty free QAOA+ with the feasibility-preserving mixer, and a Hybrid QAOA+ combining mixer based feasibility with and penalty guidance. Results on small TSP and VRP instances show that constraint-handling architecture strongly influences feasible-route sampling, convergence behavior, and probability concentration over low-cost feasible routes. These findings position constraint-aware quantum search as a methodological extension of transportation routing search approaches, where feasibility is enforced through admissible quantum transitions rather than post-hoc penalties.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces QSCI-CMP, an advanced hybrid quantum-classical algorithm designed to calculate molecular ground states more efficiently than existing methods. It achieves this by using chemical knowledge—specifically excitation level and seniority number—to preselect basis states classically. This significantly reduces the required quantum resources (query count and gate count) for achieving chemical accuracy.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses computational bottlenecks in quantum chemistry simulations, providing a concrete algorithmic improvement for simulating strongly correlated electron systems that are central to materials science.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing an efficient quantum algorithm for molecular ground-state calculations by improving upon existing methods like Quantum-Selected Configuration Interaction (QSCI).

**Main result.** The proposed QSCI-CMP method reduces the query count and gate count required compared to SQD-AA by up to 68% and 72% respectively for 24-qubit systems.

**Method.** It hybridizes quantum sampling with classical preselection by fixing 'chemically trivial' basis states based on structural properties (excitation level, seniority number) before running amplitude amplification.

**Model / system.** The system is molecular electronic structure calculation, involving the ground-state energy and eigenvectors of an electronic Hamiltonian ($\hat{H}$), mapped onto qubits using a Slater determinant representation relative to a Hartree–Fock reference state.

**Key observables.** Ground-state energy, query count, gate count (T-gate count), excitation level ($e(x)$), and seniority number ($\Omega(x)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The size of the chemically trivial subspace is a tunable parameter that can shift work from quantum sampling to classical diagonalization.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Previous methods suffer from measurement bottlenecks (QSCI) or gate counts growing with measured states (SQD-AA); QSCI-CMP assumes chemical knowledge allows preselection of important basis states.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail block-level quantum circuits for the oracle $\hat{O}_T$, showing components like reference shifts, population count, and phase flips used to enforce constraints on excitation energy and seniority number.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces QSCI-CMP as an improvement over SQD-AA by incorporating chemical knowledge. It details how this knowledge is encoded into the oracle via structural quantities (e.g., $h(x)$) and provides numerical demonstrations showing significant resource reductions compared to baseline methods.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present QSCI-CMP, a quantum-classical hybrid algorithm for molecular ground-state calculations that reduces both the query count and the gate count of sample-based quantum diagonalization with amplitude amplification (SQD-AA). SQD-AA mitigates the measurement bottleneck of quantum-selected configuration interaction (QSCI) by amplifying the basis states that have not yet been measured. Its oracle, however, specifies the measured states by listing them one by one, so its gate count grows with the number of collected states. Moreover, the quantum resources are spent even on states whose importance is evident from chemical knowledge, such as low-order excitations from the Hartree-Fock reference, which could be collected classically at the outset. We therefore propose to fix such chemically trivial states in advance, include them in the diagonalization subspace from the start, and exclude them from the amplification target, using a low-cost oracle that recognizes them through the excitation level and the seniority number of each basis state. We numerically demonstrate that QSCI-CMP reduces the query count and the gate count required to reach chemical accuracy by up to approximately 68% and 72% relative to SQD-AA for 24-qubit systems. The chemically trivial subspace is freely tunable within the classical computational budget. A larger subspace shifts more work onto the classical solver and increases the reduction in quantum cost, and when it captures the ground state sufficiently well, no quantum sampling is needed at all. We also point out that a query-optimal iteration count known from the analysis of quantum search further reduces the query count of both methods by approximately 12%.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel, globally-controlled architecture for quantum computing that solves the qubit overhead problem in Quantum Error Correction (QEC). By using global driving fields on a ring of qubits, it enables syndrome extraction circuits entirely with global gates. This approach yields significantly higher theoretical QEC thresholds compared to previous local control schemes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a theoretical blueprint for scaling quantum computation by fundamentally changing how error correction is implemented—by making all qubits computational and globally addressable—which has implications for superconducting circuit design.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving fault tolerance in quantum computation by overcoming hardware scaling bottlenecks, specifically addressing the 'wiring problem' associated with conventional local control architectures.

**Main result.** The authors propose a globally-controlled architecture that implements Quantum Error Correction (QEC) with zero auxiliary qubit overhead and achieves QEC thresholds significantly higher than previous estimates.

**Method.** They utilize global driving fields to implement programmable pairwise exchange operations, enabling syndrome extraction circuits using only global gates for cyclic stabilizer codes.

**Model / system.** The model is a homogeneous ring of N computational qubits coupled via nearest-neighbor interactions. Control is achieved through time-dependent Hamiltonian engineering that allows information routing and gate operations globally.

**Key observables.** QEC Threshold ($p_{th}$), Logical Error Rate, Qubit Overhead (aiming for zero).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code distance ($d$), Number of qubits ($N$), Global coupling strengths ($h(t), g(t)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes the ability to selectively activate desired interactions using global detuning, and that syndrome measurement can be sequentially performed despite the global nature of the gates.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the ring topology with global couplings, connectivity graphs showing universal computation paths, and performance plots comparing achievable QEC thresholds against physical error rates.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the architecture by first establishing global control to bypass wiring limits. It then details the implementation of cyclic stabilizer codes for syndrome extraction using only global gates, culminating in threshold calculations and discussions on improving performance via local measurement sites.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Reaching fault tolerance means scaling qubit counts by orders of magnitude, a jump that conventional superconducting architectures cannot sustain without solving the so-called `wiring problem'. Global control sidesteps this bottleneck, but implementing quantum error correction (QEC) on previously proposed global architectures incurs extremely steep overhead costs, due to the need for separate correction procedures for the computational and auxiliary qubits that comprise the global device. We resolve this by introducing the first globally-controlled architecture with zero qubit overhead. Every physical qubit is a computational qubit, and thus, every qubit is protected under a single error correcting scheme. We identify a class of cyclic stabilizer codes realizable through global iSWAP and single-qubit gates, yielding QEC thresholds nearly seven orders of magnitude larger than previous estimates for globally-controlled arrays. We further show these thresholds improve systematically as the global architecture is augmented with a limited amount of local measurement sites, demonstrating a trade-off between wiring simplicity and fault-tolerant performance.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents a unified framework to solve quantum circuit cutting by jointly optimizing gate and wire cuts. It models the problem using an augmented graph structure and applies advanced graph partitioning heuristics to minimize exponentially growing sampling overheads. This allows for the efficient decomposition of large circuits into smaller, executable pieces on limited quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum computation theory, the underlying methodology—graph partitioning to optimize resource constraints (qubits/overhead)—is highly relevant for designing complex quantum simulations or mapping algorithms onto realistic hardware architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is minimizing the exponentially growing sampling overhead associated with circuit cutting, which is necessary to execute large quantum circuits on hardware limited by qubit count.

**Main result.** The proposed unified framework significantly reduces total sampling overhead compared to optimizing gate or wire cuts in isolation by jointly considering gate groups alongside both types of cuts.

**Method.** It formulates the problem as a scalable graph partitioning task, utilizing an augmented Circuit Topology Graph and applying modified Karmarkar-Karp (KL) heuristics.

**Model / system.** The model is based on quantum circuits ($C$) composed of qubits ($Q$) and two-qubit gates. The system is constrained by hardware limitations ($	ext{qmax}$), requiring partitioning into independent subcircuits.

**Key observables.** Sampling Overhead ($\mathcal{S}$), which must be minimized; also diagnostic feedback on circuit suitability for cutting.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits, the maximum allowed qubits per subcircuit ($	ext{qmax}$), and various sampling overhead constants ($\kappa$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Existing approaches often treat gate and wire cuts independently, missing opportunities for optimal joint decomposition. The problem is NP-hard.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the workflow of circuit cutting (Input $ightarrow$ Cut Placement $ightarrow$ Subcircuit Execution); they compare sampling overheads for gate-only vs. wire-only vs. combined optimal cuts; and show examples of how joint cutting reduces overhead significantly.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces a unified framework that combines gate and wire cutting into a single partitioning strategy, leveraging joint cutting via novel gate grouping techniques, and solves this by formulating the problem as a scalable graph partitioning task.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum circuit cutting enables the execution of large circuits on devices with a limited number of qubits by partitioning circuits into independent subcircuits. However, this introduces a sampling overhead, which grows exponentially with the number of cuts, rendering the choice of cut placements critical for practical circuit cutting. Determining optimal cut placements remains computationally challenging, particularly as circuits grow in size. Additionally, existing circuit cutting approaches typically treat gate and wire cuts independently. Those combining both cutting approaches, however, do not take advantage of joint cutting, i.e., identifying common gate groups and cutting them jointly for a reduced overhead. This work presents a unified framework that combines gate and wire cutting within a single partitioning strategy, enabling more efficient circuit decompositions. Moreover, our approach incorporates joint cutting via a novel gate grouping technique, further reducing sampling overhead. By formulating the cut placement problem as a scalable graph partitioning task, our method efficiently identifies near-optimal cut placements for large circuits, also providing diagnostic feedback on whether circuits are suitable for cutting.

</details>

**Authors:** Alessia Ciacco, Luigi Di Puglia Pugliese, Francesca Guerriero

**Summary.** This paper proposes an advanced mathematical framework using Integer Linear Programming to solve the complex problem of reorganizing public school networks. It applies this model to real Italian data, comparing classical computational results against those obtained from hybrid quantum optimization environments. The work establishes a robust decision-support tool for sustainable and equitable resource allocation in educational infrastructure.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core problem is civil engineering/operations research, the explicit reformulation into a constrained quadratic model and subsequent testing on Quantum Annealing environments makes it relevant for demonstrating near-term applications of quantum computation to complex combinatorial optimization problems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the strategic planning problem of school network reorganization, aiming to efficiently allocate public resources while balancing demographic trends, accessibility, and educational requirements.

**Main result.** The proposed optimization framework effectively identifies optimal aggregation plans for real-world regions like Calabria, demonstrating its potential as a robust decision-support tool for equitable infrastructure planning.

**Method.** It develops an Integer Linear Programming (ILP) formulation that is also reformulated as a constrained quadratic model, allowing it to be implemented and tested within a hybrid quantum optimization environment alongside classical solvers.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is the public school network of a region (e.g., Calabria), which must be partitioned into optimal clusters based on complex constraints including travel time, capacity, and administrative compatibility.

**Key observables.** Optimal aggregation plans, objective function values ($z_C$ vs $z_Q$), structural indicators ($	ext{CZ}, 	ext{KR}, 	ext{RC}$, etc.), and solution times for various policy scenarios.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Policy configurations (64 tested), maximum execution time limits ($T_{IC}^{max} = 1200 	ext{ s}$), and integration of multiple constraints like territorial criticality and accessibility.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The methodology assumes that the problem can be accurately modeled by integrating geographical, administrative, and educational criteria into a single optimization framework; quantum performance is noted as potentially limited by current hardware capabilities.

**Figures summary.** The notes reference tables comparing classical vs. quantum objective values and solution times across policy configurations for the Calabria case study.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically reviews related literature on facility planning, details its novel ILP/quadratic formulation integrating multiple constraints, applies this framework to a real-world Italian case study (Calabria), and compares classical optimization results with preliminary quantum annealing results.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

School network reorganization is a strategic planning problem that requires balancing demographic trends, territorial accessibility, educational requirements, and institutional constraints while ensuring an efficient allocation of public resources. This paper proposes an optimization framework for school dimensioning decisions based on a novel Integer Linear Programming formulation integrating geographical, administrative, and educational criteria. A synthetic benchmark generator is introduced to evaluate the scalability and computational performance of the model on artificial instances, while a real-world case study involving the complete public school network of the Calabria region (Italy) is conducted using actual institutional, territorial, and demographic data. The proposed approach effectively identifies optimal aggregation plans under different policy scenarios while preserving the structural characteristics of the educational system. Furthermore, the model is reformulated as a constrained quadratic model and implemented within a hybrid quantum optimization environment, demonstrating its compatibility with emerging quantum technologies. The results highlight the robustness of the proposed methodology and its potential as a decision-support tool for sustainable and equitable school network planning.

</details>

### [The Hidden Subgroup Problem in Semidirect Products and Quasi-Hamiltonian Groups](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05321v1)

**Authors:** Mauro E. S. Morales  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.05321v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2608.05321_figures/2608.05321_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: Schematic inclusion relations among several classes of groups. The class Nil≤2 includes the groups of nilpotency class ≤2. For these groups, the algorithm of [15] solves the HSP efficiently. The nilpotency class of quasi-Hamiltonian groups is not bounded by a constant and therefore these two classes are incomparable (see Appendix A). Both classes contain the finite Dedekind groups, in which every subgroup is normal. Note that the dihedral group DN is nilpotent if and only if N is a power of two.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops polynomial-time quantum algorithms for solving the Hidden Subgroup Problem (HSP) in specific classes of non-Abelian finite groups, including semidirect products and quasi-Hamiltonian groups. It achieves this by exploiting deep structural properties of these groups and refining quantum measurement techniques like the block measurement protocol. The research advances the theoretical understanding of what computational problems can be efficiently solved on a quantum computer using group structure knowledge.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work pushes the boundaries of quantum complexity theory by extending known quantum algorithms beyond simple Abelian structures to complex, structured non-Abelian groups relevant in abstract algebra.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the Hidden Subgroup Problem (HSP) for non-Abelian finite groups, which is significantly harder than the Abelian case.

**Main result.** Polynomial-time quantum algorithms are provided for two structured families of non-Abelian groups: semidirect products $A times_{\varphi} \mathbb{Z}_{p^k}$ and quasi-Hamiltonian groups.

**Method.** The methods involve adapting techniques from Abelian HSP solvers, utilizing structural properties like modular subgroup lattices (for quasi-Hamiltonian groups), and employing advanced quantum measurement protocols like the block measurement technique.

**Model / system.** The theoretical model is abstract group theory applied to quantum computation; no specific physical Hamiltonian or lattice system is described. The focus is on solving a computational problem defined over finite groups $G$.

**Key observables.** Success probability of the quantum algorithm, which must be shown to be high enough (e.g., $\geq rac{\phi(N)}{N} 2^{mt} p$) for polynomial time complexity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Group structure parameters such as $p$ (prime), $k$, and the exponent of the Abelian factor, $	ext{Exp}(A)$. Efficiency depends on conditions like $	ext{Exp}(A)/p = 	ext{polylog}(|G|)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The algorithms rely on specific structural assumptions about the groups (e.g., quasi-Hamiltonian structure, or $A$ having bounded generator rank) and assume black-box access to group operations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the inclusion relationship between various group classes: Nilpotent $\le 2$, Quasi-Hamiltonian, Dedekind, and Abelian groups.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the context of HSP difficulty in non-Abelian settings. It then details algorithms for semidirect products (Theorem A/B), followed by a dedicated treatment for quasi-Hamiltonian groups (Theorem C). The latter sections involve deep analysis of quantum measurement protocols (PGM) and structural comparisons with other group families.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Several early quantum algorithms, including Simon's algorithm and Shor's period-finding are instances of the hidden subgroup problem (HSP) over finite abelian groups. No polynomial-time quantum algorithm is known for the HSP over arbitrary non-abelian finite groups. The non-Abelian case is of particular interest because some instances, such as the dihedral and symmetric group HSPs, are connected to lattice problems and graph isomorphism, respectively.   In this work, we give polynomial-time quantum algorithms for two further families containing non-Abelian groups. First, we consider groups of the form $G=A\rtimes_{\varphi} \mathbb{Z}_{p^k}$, with $A$ finite Abelian, $p$ prime, $k\in \mathbb{N}$ and the action of $\mathbb Z_{p^k}$ is generated by the scalar automorphism $a\mapstoμa$, for some $μ\in\mathbb Z_{\operatorname{Exp}(A)}^\times$, where $\mathrm{Exp}(A)$ is the exponent of $A$. Our algorithm is efficient when $A$ has bounded generator rank and $\mathrm{Exp}(A)/p=\mathrm{polylog}(|G|)$. This includes the case $A=\mathbb{Z}_N$ for $N\in\mathbb{N}$ and $k=1$, studied by Bacon, Childs and van Dam (FOCS 2005), and $A=\mathbb{Z}_{q^r}$ with $q$ prime and $r\in \mathbb{N}$ studied by van Dam and Dey (TQC 2014). Second, we give a polynomial-time quantum algorithm for finite quasi-Hamiltonian groups under a mild assumption on the input structure. Quasi-Hamiltonian groups are finite nilpotent groups with modular subgroup lattice, or equivalently the finite groups in which every subgroup is permutable. As far as we know, this is the first quantum algorithm to exploit the modularity of the subgroup lattice for solving the HSP. This extends, under the aforementioned structured input assumption, the quantum algorithm for Dedekind groups given by Hallgren, Russell, and Ta-Shma (SIAM J. Comput. 32, 2003).

</details>

**Authors:** Eric Kubischta, Ian Teixeira

**Summary.** This work applies time-reversal symmetry to the theory of quantum error correction, showing that this symmetry imposes strong selection rules on the underlying error algebra. These rules automatically satisfy many necessary conditions for fault tolerance, simplifying the requirements for reliable quantum computation. The framework unifies concepts from spectral protection (Kramers pairs) and algebraic constraints (Knill-Laflamme conditions).

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep structural constraint on quantum codes derived from fundamental symmetries, which is relevant for designing robust quantum memories or computation protocols in physical systems exhibiting time-reversal symmetry.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how applying time-reversal symmetry ($\Theta$) imposes structural selection rules on quantum error correction (QEC) codes.

**Main result.** Time-reversal invariance forces all even-weight Pauli operators to act as scalars, automatically satisfying the even-weight Knill–Laflamme conditions and simplifying error detection requirements.

**Method.** The authors use the antiunitary time-reversal operator $\Theta$ to derive parity selection rules for Pauli strings and reinterpret Rains' shadow enumerator through this symmetry.

**Model / system.** The analysis is performed on quantum codes defined over spin-1/2 particles (qubits) and generalizes to multi-spin systems. The focus is on the time-reversal invariance of the code subspace itself.

**Key observables.** Kramers degeneracy, Pauli weight parity, Knill–Laflamme conditions satisfaction, and coefficients of the Rains shadow enumerator ($S_w$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The degree of error correction distance ($d$) and the number of spins ($n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes working with spin-1/2 particles and relies on the antiunitary nature of time reversal ($\Theta$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the link between spectral protection (Kramers degeneracy) and algebraic error protection by applying $\Theta$ to QEC codes, deriving selection rules, and reinterpreting shadow invariants.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We apply time-reversal symmetry to quantum codes and show that it imposes parity selection rules on the physical error algebra. A time-reversal-invariant logical qubit on an odd number of spins is a Kramers doublet, forcing every even-weight Pauli to act as a scalar. Consequently, all even-weight Knill--Laflamme conditions hold automatically, so single-qubit error detection implies correction. We then reinterpret the Rains shadow enumerator through time reversal: each coefficient is a sum of error-resolved overlaps between a code and its time-reversed image.

</details>

### [Kinetic Lifshitz invariants and dynamics of nonreciprocal fluctuations in superconductors](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05306v1)

**Authors:** Tony Liu, Joaquim Telles de Miranda, Daniel Shaffer, Alex Levchenko  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.05306v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **4/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **3/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **3/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **3/5** · `spintronics-quantum-optics interface` **2/5** · `Kondo & dissipative impurity` **1/5** · `exotic spin models in light-matter` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2608.05306_figures/2608.05306_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 2: Disorder crossover of the helical modulation vector, Eq. (35). For weak Dyakonov-Perel re- laxation (g ≪1) the helix is suppressed, q0 ∝(αRpF )2τ αRh/Tv2 F ; for g ≫1 it saturates at the universal, disorder-independent value q0 = 4αRh/v2 F . The same function (and its derivatives) controls the crossover of every entry of Table 1.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a generalized time-dependent Ginzburg-Landau theory for disordered, noncentrosymmetric superconductors using the Keldysh formalism. It determines that nonreciprocal Lifshitz invariants appear in both the free energy and the dynamic fluctuation terms. The results unify conflicting theoretical predictions by introducing a single crossover function controlling the system's response to disorder.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of fluctuation-dissipation theorems and symmetry principles (Onsager reciprocity) to derive non-equilibrium dynamics in a superconducting context provides advanced tools for understanding dissipation beyond simple mean-field treatments.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study derives a generalized time-dependent Ginzburg-Landau theory for disordered noncentrosymmetric superconductors, focusing on the appearance of Lifshitz-type nonreciprocal couplings in the quantum (Keldysh) sector.

**Main result.** The kinetic Lifshitz invariants are governed by a single crossover kernel dependent on the ratio of the spin-relaxation rate to temperature, reconciling previous conflicting results for magnetoelectric couplings.

**Method.** The theory is derived from the Keldysh nonlinear sigma model and utilizes principles like the Fluctuation-Dissipation Theorem (FDT) and Onsager reciprocity to determine dynamics.

**Model / system.** The minimal model is a disordered noncentrosymmetric superconductor realized via a two-dimensional electron gas with Rashba spin-orbit coupling and an in-plane Zeeman field. The analysis uses the Keldysh formalism to construct the effective action.

**Key observables.** Superconducting diode efficiency, fluctuation-induced magnetochiral anisotropy, relaxation rate of fluctuations ($\mathbf{q}$ dependence), and Langevin noise power (which acquire $\mathbf{q}$-odd and $B$-field odd terms).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of the Dyakonov-Perel spin-relaxation rate ($\Gamma_t$) to temperature ($T$), which dictates the crossover between weak-relaxation and relaxation-dominated regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is performed in the diffusive regime and relies on gauge covariance arguments within the Keldysh formalism.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the general context of Lifshitz invariants, applies this to superconductors using the Keldysh NLSM, derives the full dynamical theory (TDGL equation), and computes specific nonreciprocal couplings in various limits defined by $\Gamma_t/T$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We derive the generalized time-dependent Ginzburg-Landau theory of a disordered noncentrosymmetric superconductor from the Keldysh nonlinear sigma model, using a two-dimensional electron gas with Rashba spin-orbit coupling and an in-plane Zeeman field as a minimal model. On the thermodynamic side we construct the Lifshitz invariants of the free energy, the linear and cubic gradient terms and the momentum-odd part of the quartic vertex, and trace their dependence on disorder. These couplings are governed by a single closed-form kernel controlled by the ratio of the Dyakonov-Perel spin-relaxation rate to temperature, interpolating between the weak-relaxation regime, where the invariants are suppressed, and the relaxation-dominated regime, where the helical modulation of the order parameter saturates at a universal, disorder-independent value. This crossover reconciles conflicting results for the magnetoelectric couplings of dirty Rashba superconductors. Because the theory is formulated on the Keldysh contour, it also determines the dissipative dynamics: the relaxation rate of a fluctuation with pair momentum $\mathbf{q}$, and hence, by the fluctuation-dissipation theorem, the Langevin noise power, acquires a term odd in $\mathbf{q}$ and odd in the magnetic field. The structure of this kinetic Lifshitz invariant is dictated by Onsager reciprocity: friction and noise renormalize in lockstep, so equal-time fluctuations remain Gibbsian while the dynamics are nonreciprocal. As applications we compute the superconducting diode efficiency near $T_c$, where the cubic invariant competes with the quartic vertex and with even higher-gradient terms rendered odd by the helical shift, reversing the sign of the diode coefficient, and the fluctuation-induced magnetochiral anisotropy above $T_c$, where the current-resolved nonreciprocal resistance forms a plateau across the Gaussian regime.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically analyzes the Electric Hall Effect in magnetic Weyl Semimetals, revealing unique scaling behaviors dictated by topology. The key findings include universal $E_F^{-1}$ scaling at zero temperature and a logarithmically corrected scaling at finite temperatures. This suggests a robust, all-electric mechanism for sensing weak electric fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter topology, the concept of field-induced topological transitions and universal scaling laws are highly relevant in understanding quantum transport phenomena that can be modeled using effective Hamiltonians common in AMO physics (e.g., superconducting qubits or cavity QED systems exhibiting topological phases).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the unconventional scaling laws governing the Electric Hall Effect (EHE) in 2D magnetic Weyl Semimetals (WSMs). The goal is to understand how topology dictates this response, especially under weak electric fields.

**Main result.** The EHE exhibits universal scaling at zero temperature ($\chi_{xy} \propto E_F^{-1}$) and an unconventional logarithmically corrected scaling at finite temperature ($\sigma_{xy} \propto E_z \ln(1/|E_z|)$).

**Method.** Theoretical derivation using the Kubo formula, Berry curvature calculations, and detailed scaling analysis involving momentum-to-parameter space mappings.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as 2D magnetic Weyl semimetals hosting doubly degenerate nodal points. The physics is captured by a two-band Hamiltonian subjected to an out-of-plane electric field ($E_z$) which acts as a mass term, inducing topological phase transitions.

**Key observables.** Electric Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{xy}$) and the intrinsic EHE coefficient ($\chi_{xy}$), both dependent on $E_F$ and $E_z$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fermi energy ($E_F$), out-of-plane electric field strength ($E_z$), and the global topological charge ($C$) of the Weyl points.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a two-band model approximation near the nodal points, and specific mathematical mappings between momentum space and parameter space are utilized for integration.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the $E_z$-driven topological phase transition (gap closing) and compare numerical/analytical results showing the characteristic logarithmic scaling of $\sigma_{xy}(T)$ versus field strength.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically derives the EHE coefficient ($\chi_{xy}$) at zero temperature using Berry curvature methods, then extends this analysis to finite temperatures by incorporating thermal effects into the conductivity calculation, revealing the unconventional scaling laws.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electric Hall Effect (EHE), a unique phenomenon in two-dimensional (2D) magnetic systems, refers to the generation of Hall current by an out-of-plane electric field $\Ez$. Here, we demonstrate that for 2D magnetic Weyl semimetals that host doubly degenerate nodal points, the EHE features multiple unconventional scaling laws. At zero temperature, the EHE exhibits a topological $E_F^{-1}$ Fermi-energy scaling. Remarkably, the prefactor of the scaling is determined by the global topological charge of the point without any dependence on the local parameters of the system, leading to a universal and significant enhancement of Hall response in any species of Weyl points as the Fermi energy approaches the Weyl point. This significant response enables a weak electric field to be directly converted into a measurable Hall signal. Surprisingly, this enhanced Hall response is not diminished by temperature, but evolves into an unconventional logarithmically corrected scaling at finite temperature $σ_{xy}\propto\Ez\ln(1/|\Ez|)$ for weak $\Ez$, still yielding a divergent electric-field susceptibility. Thus, our work not only unveils intriguing scaling laws resulting from the interaction between magnetism and topology, but also suggests a novel scaling-enhanced and temperature-robust mechanism that may enable weak electric-field sensing through a practical and all-electric route.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper models nonlinear waves in a 2D mechanical topological insulator using coupled nonlinear Schrödinger equations. It predicts the existence and properties of various vector edge solitons and domain walls, which are topologically protected. The findings suggest that these systems can be engineered for advanced functionalities like collision-based computing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges nonlinear wave physics, topological materials, and mechanical systems. The emergence of robust, controllable soliton dynamics in a physical, non-electronic platform like pendulums is highly relevant for developing novel quantum or classical information processing devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates nonlinear edge waves and domain walls within a two-dimensional mechanical topological insulator (MTI). The goal is to characterize the complex soliton dynamics arising from this system.

**Main result.** The study predicts the existence of various vector edge solitons, including bright-bright and dark-dark types, which are topologically protected when carrier frequencies lie within a band gap. These solitons exhibit potential for energy transfer during collisions, suggesting applications in information processing.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving a one-dimensional two-component coupled nonlinear Schrödinger (CNLS) equation from the underlying mechanical lattice dynamics. Soliton solutions are found by analyzing this continuum model and exploring continuation techniques.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a 2D MTI built using pendulums with on-site cubic nonlinearity, connected by linear springs to realize the quantum spin Hall effect. The dynamics are modeled via coupled nonlinear equations of motion that reduce to the CNLS equation for edge modes.

**Key observables.** Bright-bright (BB) and dark-dark edge solitons, domain walls, energy transfer during soliton collisions, and topological protection confirmed by passing through defects.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system depends on parameters defining the cubic nonlinearity ($\sigma$), coupling strengths ($f$), and the quasi-momentum space structure that allows for Equal Group Velocity (EGV) points.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on deriving a continuum approximation (CNLS equation) from the discrete lattice model, assuming near degeneracy in the linear spectrum to facilitate this reduction.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes; however, the text implies figures illustrate the schematic connections of pendula, the dispersion relation showing EGV points, and visualizations of the resulting solitons/domain walls.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured logically: reviewing the MTI design (Section II), deriving and analyzing the CNLS equation solutions (Section III), designing the platform and studying propagation (Sections IV & V), exploring BB solitons via defects and collisions (Sections VI & VII), and concluding with future work (Section VIII).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report nonlinear edge waves in a 2D mechanical topological insulator. A bulk lattice consists of pendulums with on-site cubic nonlinearity connected by linear springs realizing quantum spin Hall effect. We show that the nonlinear interaction between two edge modes with equal group velocities (EGV) is described by a 1D two-component coupled nonlinear Schrödinger (CNLS) equation. On the interface separating two bulk lattices with opposite spin Chern numbers, we construct linear springs such that the dispersion relation exhibits EGV points with favorable CNLS coefficients. Thus, we realize nonlinear edge waves propagating along the interface, including bright-bright (BB) edge solitons for focusing CNLS coefficients, and dark-dark edge solitons, edge domain walls, and dark-bright edge solitons for defocusing CNLS coefficients. In terms of the site amplitudes, these solutions resemble bright and dark breathers. These solutions should be topologically protected when both carrier frequencies lie within a band gap, which we explicitly show by passing BB edge solitons through compact defects on the interface. We also show energy transfer in BB edge soliton collisions with potential application to collision-based computing. Generally, vector edge solitons exhibit a large parameter space for soliton collisions, which endows mechanical devices with greater potential for information processing and other functionalities.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports on the generation and characterization of 'abruptly autofocusing waves,' which concentrate optical energy suddenly at a specific point rather than gradually like Gaussian beams. By experimentally realizing spherical Airy wavepackets in the full space-time domain, the authors demonstrate a method for achieving highly localized light delivery. This breakthrough opens new avenues for ultrafast structured light applications in photonics and material science.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems as it deals with engineered light fields capable of extreme spatial/temporal confinement. The underlying physics involves nonlinear wave propagation and structured light control, which has parallels in cavity QED or advanced photonic implementations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research focuses on understanding and utilizing 'abruptly autofocusing waves' due to their ability to deliver optical energy with high precision at a prescribed focus, contrasting them with conventional Gaussian focusing.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated the experimental realization of spherical Airy wavepackets in the full space-time domain, achieving a tightly focused beam with significantly higher contrast than an equivalent Gaussian beam.

**Method.** The experiment involved generating a 2D space-time Airy precursor using a spatiotemporal hologram and extending this field into spherical geometry via log-polar optical transformation combined with spectral phase control.

**Model / system.** The system involves light propagation governed by the wave equation, specifically utilizing Airy profiles in both two-dimensional (circular) and full three-dimensional space-time geometries. These waves are compared against standard Gaussian focusing profiles.

**Key observables.** High-contrast intensity surge at a prescribed focus; comparison of peak intensity to Gaussian beams; ability to achieve tight energy localization.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The geometry (2D vs 3D space-time); the contrast ratio relative to Gaussian beams; spectral bandwidth and aperture limitations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Current experimental implementations are limited by pixelated spatial light modulators, finite apertures, and approximations introduced by log-polar mapping requiring careful compensation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the historical progression of these waves: from theoretical prediction (2010) to circular wave observation (2011), culminating in the recent experimental realization of the full space-time spherical counterpart [13].

**Paper structure.** The paper appears structured around establishing the physical phenomenon, reviewing the evolution of its study (from 2D to 3D), detailing the complex experimental methodology required for 3D generation, and concluding with applications and future directions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Whereas conventional Gaussian focusing gradually concentrates optical energy around the focal plane, abruptly autofocusing waves maintain a low peak intensity over most of their evolution before undergoing a sudden, high-contrast intensity surge at a prescribed focus. Since their introduction in 2010, their two-dimensional spatial realizations have enabled applications ranging from particle manipulation and material processing to terahertz generation and nonlinear optics. The recent work of Cao et al. marks the transition from (2+1)-dimensional spatial autofocusing to the full space-time domain through the experimental synthesis of spherical Airy wavepackets. This advance opens new opportunities for ultrafast structured light, tightly localized energy delivery, and nonlinear photonics.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses STM to probe the electronic structure of a skyrmion magnet across multiple magnetic phases. It establishes that the observed charge modulations (LDOS) are not arbitrary but are microscopically dictated by the local alignment of neighboring spins. This provides crucial evidence for the intimate, intertwined relationship between spin and orbital degrees-of-freedom in itinerant magnets.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the detailed mapping of charge (LDOS) modulation driven by magnetic order touches upon concepts relevant to emergent quantum phenomena in correlated electron systems, which share mathematical frameworks with many-body physics studied in AMO or strongly correlated contexts.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to elucidate the microscopic mechanisms stabilizing non-collinear magnetism and establish the direct relationship between multi-$Q$ charge modulations (LDOS) and spin textures in itinerant skyrmion magnets.

**Main result.** The analysis demonstrates a tight correlation where the alignment of nearest-neighbor Gd spins dictates the local density-of-states (LDOS) patterns observed in the Ru 4d orbitals, providing a microscopic view of charge-spin intertwining.

**Method.** The research combines Scanning Tunneling Microscopy (STM) measurements to map LDOS modulations with theoretical modeling that relates these electronic textures to underlying spin structures via bond-centered dot products.

**Model / system.** The system is the compound GdRu2Ge2, which exhibits multiple magnetic phases including skyrmion crystal states. The physics involves itinerant electrons mediating interactions between localized Gd 4f spins and delocalized Ru 4d orbitals.

**Key observables.** Periodic LDOS patterns observed via STM (analyzed by FFT), wavevectors ($Q_A, Q_B$), scalar chirality ($	ext{nsc}(\mathbf{r})$), and the topological index ($N_{sk}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature/Magnetic Field sweeps across five magnetic phases; energy bias voltage range for LDOS mapping.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the electronic modulations (LDOS) are directly governed by local spin-spin interactions ($\langle \mathbf{S}_{i,j} \cdot \mathbf{S}_{i+1,j} angle$) via a Non-Collinear Magneto-Resistance effect.

**Figures summary.** Figures show STM images and their Fourier transforms confirming superstructures related to magnetic wavevectors in different phases; energy dependence plots detail how the LDOS pattern changes with bias voltage, revealing phase inversions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first characterizing multiple magnetic phases using STM/FFT analysis (identifying multi-$Q$ patterns), then developing a theoretical model linking these patterns to spin-spin dot products ($\mathbf{W}(\mathbf{r})$), and finally confirming the correlation between local spin alignment and measured electronic modulations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The mechanisms stabilizing non-collinear magnetism in centrosymmetric crystals remain unclear, but likely involve spin-spin interactions mediated by itinerant electrons, such as the RKKY interaction. Finite-$Q$ magnetic order may then be accompanied by electronic modulations that are observable using a scanning tunneling microscope. In five successive magnetic phases of GdRu$_{2}$Ge$_{2}$, including two nano-scale skyrmion crystal phases, we show that multi-$Q$ magnetism among Gd 4$f$ spins entails a corresponding multi-$Q$ texture among the Ru 4$d$ orbitals that contribute itinerant electron bands. With atomically-resolved images of each electronic texture's motif, and a simple numerical modeling scheme drawing on the underlying spin structures, we infer their key relationship: The alignment between nearest-neighbor Gd spins tightly correlates with the local density-of-states of the Ru 4$d$ orbitals on the two bond-centered sublattices of the Gd square net. These analyses offer a microscopic view of the atomic-scale intertwining of charge and spin degrees-of-freedom in non-collinear itinerant magnets.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a geometrically closed thermodynamic theory to model how external magnetic fields control nanoparticle size during synthesis. By reformulating classical nucleation theory using a sphere-packing approach, the authors create a unified formalism applicable across different magnetic material types. The resulting framework accurately predicts both the reduction of mean particle size and the narrowing of size distributions with increasing field strength.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical nucleation, the rigorous thermodynamic formulation and the development of an analytical framework to handle complex field dependencies provide valuable methodological tools applicable to non-equilibrium phase transitions in quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is developing a unified, computationally efficient theory to control nanoparticle size during synthesis using external magnetic fields.

**Main result.** The authors establish a geometrically closed thermodynamic framework that successfully unifies the description of nucleation across superparamagnetic, paramagnetic, and diamagnetic systems.

**Method.** They reformulate classical nucleation theory by introducing a sphere-packing representation to link discrete atomic structure to continuum free-energy contributions, yielding a field-driven evolution equation for the critical nucleus size.

**Model / system.** The model describes nanoparticle formation where the total free energy ($\Delta F$) is decomposed into surface, bulk driving, induced magnetization, and permanent moment terms. The system spans various magnetic classes (superparamagnetic to diamagnetic).

**Key observables.** Mean particle size ($r$) and the narrowing of the size distribution as a function of applied magnetic field ($B$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic susceptibility ($\chi_m$), chemical potential difference ($\Delta\mu$), surface free energy ($\gamma$), and the applied magnetic field strength ($B$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory assumes a specific structure (core + defective shell) for calculating atomic counts, and it treats the permanent moment by assigning it to one effective collective NP moment.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how an applied magnetic field lowers the free-energy barrier for nucleation and show model-generated size distributions confirming predicted size reduction with increasing field.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the geometrically closed thermodynamic framework, derives the field-driven evolution equation for the critical nucleus size, solves this equation for different magnetic limits (including a susceptibility-only limit), and validates the results by comparing predictions against experimental data for various magnetic materials.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Controlling nanoparticle size during synthesis remains a central challenge in nanoscience, particularly in systems where external magnetic fields are used as continuous control parameters. Existing descriptions of magnetic-field-assisted nucleation are typically material-specific or rely on computationally intensive atomistic methods. Here, we reformulate classical nucleation theory as a geometrically closed thermodynamic framework by introducing a sphere-packing representation of atomic assembly. This construction establishes a direct link between discrete atomic structure and continuum free-energy contributions under applied magnetic fields, yielding a field-driven evolution equation for the critical nucleus size. The resulting theory provides a unified description of nanoparticle nucleation across superparamagnetic, paramagnetic, and diamagnetic systems within a single formalism. It quantitatively reproduces previously unresolved experimental observations for magnetite and nickel nanoparticles, namely the systematic reduction of mean particle size and narrowing of size distributions with increasing magnetic field. In the diamagnetic limit, the framework recovers our earlier analytical susceptibility-based description of silver nanoparticles, in which the field-dependent critical radius is governed by the induced-magnetization contribution to the nucleation free energy. Beyond modeling the reduction of mean particle size with increasing magnetic field, the framework reveals that the narrowing of size distributions emerges naturally from the curvature of the field-modified free-energy landscape. These results establish magnetic-field-assisted nucleation as a geometrically constrained thermodynamic process, providing a computationally efficient route for controlling nanoparticle size across distinct magnetic material classes.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work calculates how electron-electron interactions modify the density of states in highly anisotropic 2D conductors subjected to spin-orbit coupling. The key finding is that SOC dramatically alters the energy dependence of this correlation correction, leading to a sign reversal at critical strengths. This provides a distinct spectroscopic tool for characterizing SOC effects beyond standard Altshuler-Aronov physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on electron correlation, the strong anisotropy, low dimensionality, and explicit inclusion of SOC make it relevant to understanding quantum transport in mesoscopic systems where spin-orbit coupling dictates orbital/spin texture effects.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates Altshuler-Aronov type interaction corrections to the single-particle Density of States (DOS) in a strongly anisotropic 2D conductor.

**Main result.** The DOS anomaly exhibits distinct dimensional crossovers (2D log dip vs. quasi-1D $\sqrt{\cdot}$ singularity), and SOC can induce a sign reversal of this correction, providing a unique spectroscopic signature.

**Method.** Calculations are performed in Matsubara space using diagrammatic techniques to compute the exchange contribution in the diffusion channel, incorporating dynamically screened Coulomb interactions.

**Model / system.** The system is a strongly anisotropic 2D electron gas with an open Fermi surface and weak disorder. It includes coexisting Rashba and Dresselhaus spin-orbit couplings (SOCs) constrained to the longitudinal direction.

**Key observables.** The DOS anomaly ($\deltaho(\epsilon, T)$), which shows logarithmic dips near $\epsilon_F$ and square-root singularities further away.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Transverse coupling scale ($\varepsilon_c$), SOC strength, disorder scattering rates, and the energy relative to the Fermi level ($|\epsilon - \epsilon_F|$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a weak-disorder diffusive regime ($k_F l \gg 1$) and utilizes approximations like the Random Phase Approximation (RPA) for screening.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the four warped lines forming the Fermi surface. Figure 7 shows the SOC dependence of the DOS anomaly, and Figure 9 details the complex frequency integration contours used in the calculation.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first defining the physical model (anisotropic conductors with SOC), then detailing the theoretical framework using Matsubara sums and diagrammatic techniques to calculate the self-energy corrections, culminating in an analysis of the resulting DOS anomaly's energy dependence and critical behavior.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study Altshuler-Aronov-type interaction corrections to the single-particle density of states (DOS) in a strongly anisotropic 2D conductor with an open Fermi surface (FS) and weak disorder, in the presence of coexisting Rashba and Dresselhaus spin-orbit couplings (SOCs) constrained to the longitudinal direction. The low-energy band consists of two warped sheets weakly tunnel-coupled transversely; SOC splits the sheets into helicity branches with a fixed spin axis. Working in a Matsubara space, we compute the exchange contribution in the diffusion channel with dynamically screened Coulomb interaction and an impurity ladder. The resulting DOS anomaly exhibits a dimensional crossover governed by the transverse coupling scale $\varepsilon_c$. Close to the Fermi level ($|\varepsilon-\varepsilon_F|<\varepsilon_c$), the system behaves two-dimensionally, featuring a logarithmic DOS dip whose magnitude is enhanced by intrinsic SOCs. Further from the Fermi level ($|\varepsilon-\varepsilon_F|\!>\!\varepsilon_c$), the system behaves quasi-one-dimensionally, featuring a sharper square-root singularity whose amplitude is remarkably enhanced by the SOCs. Notably, we identify a critical SOC strength at which these spin-orbit effects exactly cancel the electron-correlation correction, perfectly restoring the unperturbed density of states. Furthermore, increasing the SOC beyond this critical point inverts the sign of the anomaly entirely, yielding a positive DOS correction. This sign reversal fundamentally alters the energy dependence, such that at energies beyond $\varepsilon_F + \varepsilon_c$, the positive correction decays to smaller values as energy increases, opposite to the standard negative correction. This contrasting trend provides a distinct spectroscopic signature of SOC-modulated correlation effects.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically predicts the existence of an annular Majorana mode within superconducting vortices hosted on topological insulators. The stability of this unique, ring-shaped zero-energy state is linked to specific crystalline symmetries and a total surface state winding number of three. It provides concrete predictions for candidate materials like $\text{ZrTi}_2$ where such exotic topological superconductivity can be realized.

**Why it may be interesting.** The prediction of a robust, geometrically defined zero-energy state (the ring shape) offers a clear, measurable signature that could be sought in advanced STM experiments on superconducting topological materials, connecting topology to observable quantum phenomena.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper predicts a novel, stable Majorana zero-energy mode characterized by a ring shape (annular) within superconducting vortices in topological insulators.

**Main result.** This annular Majorana mode is stabilized by the rotational symmetry and winding number 3 of the surface states, existing either from a nonlinear Dirac cone at $\bar{\Gamma}$ or three linear cones at $\bar{M}$.

**Method.** The analysis involves solving the Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) Hamiltonian for superconducting vortices and performing symmetry analysis on the resulting bound states.

**Model / system.** The system is a topological insulator surface state made superconducting, specifically requiring 3-fold or 6-fold rotational symmetry. The model considers two cases: a single nonlinear Dirac cone at $\bar{\Gamma}$ or three linear Dirac cones at $\bar{M}$.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the real-space wave function profile, which is predicted to be ring-shaped (annular), contrasting with exponentially decaying modes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rotational symmetry ($3$-fold or $6$-fold); winding number of 3; superconducting gap $\Delta$; and momentum scattering terms ($	ilde{A}, 	ilde{B}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial analysis assumes the adiabatically reachable condition where a specific coupling term ($	ilde{B}$) is zero, although stability is shown even when finite.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the two physical conditions supporting the annular MZM and show the resulting ring-like wave function profile in real space for the nonlinear Dirac cone case.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework by identifying the necessary symmetry conditions (winding number 3) leading to the annular mode. It then analyzes the two distinct physical scenarios ($\bar{\Gamma}$ vs $\bar{M}$) using effective Hamiltonians and concludes with predictions for candidate materials.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

When the surface states of a topological insulator becomes superconducting, topological superconductivity can be obtained, and each vortex on the surface can host one single Majorana zero-energy mode which is usually a wave packet decaying exponentially off the vortex core. Here, we predict stable Majorana zero-energy mode whose wave function is ring-shape, dubbed as annular Majorana mode, in the superconducting vortex in topological insulators respecting $3$-fold or $6$-fold rotational symmetry. Such topological insulators are featured with a single nonlinear Dirac cone located at $\barΓ$ or three linear Dirac cones at $\bar{\text{M}}$ in the surface Brillouin zone. The annular Majorana mode originates from the effective chiral $f$-wave superconductivity on the nonlinear Dirac cone in the former case and the interference of the effective chiral $p$-wave superconductivity on the three linear Dirac cones in the latter. In both cases, the annular Majorana mode is stabilized by the rotational symmetry and the winding number $3$ carried by the surface states. Candidate materials supporting the annular Majorana mode are predicted. Our work provides new insights into the topological superconductivity in superconducting topological insulators.

</details>

**Summary.** The research analyzes the generation of pseudovorticity—a measure of local rotational flow—in self-focusing beams subjected to thermal amplitude and phase noise. By using a cubic-quintic nonlinear model, the authors demonstrate that quintic saturation prevents collapse, trapping noise into persistent, oscillating multipoles. This suggests a novel mechanism for controlling optical torque through careful 'noise engineering'.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges nonlinear wave physics with stochastic processes, showing how engineered noise can stabilize or control complex topological features like pseudovorticity in optical systems, relevant for advanced quantum optics applications.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how amplitude and phase noise affect pseudovorticity generation in self-focusing Gaussian beams.

**Main result.** Quintic saturation arrests collapse, allowing persistent, oscillating multipolar pseudovorticity patterns to form, suggesting noise can control local optical torque.

**Method.** Numerical simulations using the split-step Fourier method on a 2D grid are employed to solve the cubic-quintic nonlinear Schrödinger equation.

**Model / system.** The system models self-focusing beam dynamics governed by the (2+1)-dimensional cubic-quintic Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation. Noise is introduced via Gaussian white noise added to both amplitude and phase components of the field.

**Key observables.** Pseudovorticity ($\omega$), asymmetric intensity residue, angular modal coefficients ($m=1$ dipole, $m=2$ quadrupole) for intensity, phase, and pseudovorticity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise correlation length ($\sigma$), nonlinearity saturation parameter ($\epsilon$), and propagation distance ($z$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the paraxial scalar approximation; the pure cubic case's late-time collapse behavior was limited by spatial resolution.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare intensity, pseudovorticity evolution for noisy vs. clean beams (Fig. 2); Fig. 3 shows spatial snapshots of asymmetric fields and $\omega$; Figs. 4-7 quantify the mode competition dynamics between dipole and quadrupole components under varying noise statistics.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the model, defines pseudovorticity, simulates the evolution under noise, analyzes the persistence and oscillation of multipoles using modal decomposition (dipole/quadrupole), and concludes by linking input noise statistics to controllable optical torque.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate pseudovorticity generation in Gaussian beams undergoing self-focusing under amplitude and phase noise, using the cubic-quintic nonlinear Schrödinger equation. Pseudovorticity, defined as the curl of the optical momentum flux, characterizes local rotational flow in the absence of phase singularities. Our numerical simulations show that thermal amplitude and phase noise induce a multipolar pseudovorticity pattern. Unlike the pure cubic case, where noise asymmetries are radiated away during collapse, the quintic saturation arrests collapse and traps the noise in the resulting soliton. Hence, pseudovorticity multipoles persist, oscillating at the focusing-refocusing period. These results suggest a potential pathway for controlling local optical torque through noise engineering.

</details>
