**Summary.** This work investigates how nanocrystal size and stoichiometry affect charge transport in $	ext{CsPbBr}_3$ perovskite semiconductors. By decoupling electronic and ionic contributions using transient measurements, the authors show that smaller NCs exhibit superior charge transport properties. This demonstrates that controlled synthesis can optimize device performance by managing both quantum confinement effects and defect-related ion migration.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state optoelectronics, the detailed separation of fast electronic hopping from slower, thermally activated ionic drift provides a model for understanding coupled charge carrier dynamics in complex interfaces, relevant to open quantum systems modeling.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to comprehensively understand size-dependent charge transport by decoupling ionic and electronic transport dynamics in mixed-semiconductor nanocrystals.

**Main result.** Contrary to popular belief, the smallest $	ext{CsPbBr}_3$ NCs exhibit superior charge transport characteristics, evidenced by lower hole activation energy and higher ion migration barriers.

**Method.** The research utilized transient current measurements and space charge limited current (SCLC) analysis on thin films of varying sizes to probe coupled electronic and ionic dynamics.

**Model / system.** The physical system is all-inorganic cesium lead bromide ($	ext{CsPbBr}_3$) nanocrystals synthesized via controlled precursor chemistry, allowing systematic tuning of size and stoichiometry across different phases (p-, t-, d-).

**Key observables.** Time-of-flight mobility ($\mu_{	ext{TOF}}$), hole activation energy ($E_{a	ext{hole}}$), ion migration activation energy ($E_{a	ext{ion}}$), and characteristic time constants ($	au_c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** NC size (5.6 nm to 11.3 nm), precursor chemistry, quantum confinement regime, and temperature dependence.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on fitting transport data to Arrhenius laws for temperature dependence and using SCLC formalism for high-field electronic measurements.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the measurement setup for transient current pulses, show temperature-dependent transient currents across different NC phases, and present $J$-$V$ characteristics used to determine activation energies for both holes and ions.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically investigates transport by first establishing the mixed-transport nature using transient measurements, then quantifying electronic mobility via SCLC, and finally characterizing ion dynamics through temperature-dependent analysis to correlate size/stoichiometry with optimal charge transport.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

All-inorganic cesium lead bromide (CsPbBr3) nanocrystals (NCs) have established themselves as an emerging semiconductor for next-generation optoelectronic technologies due to their unique combination of properties, such as near unity photoluminescence quantum yields, narrow color pure emission, and exceptional defect tolerance. Although size-dependent optical signatures of these NCs are well reported, the complexity of mixed ionic-electronic transport remains largely unexplored. In this study, we provide a comprehensive analysis of size-dependent charge transport by decoupling ionic and electronic transport dynamics through carefully designed transient current and space charge limited current measurements. By employing NCs of different sizes ranging from 5.6 nm to 11.3 nm in thin films, we provide a comprehensive understanding of quantum confinement effects and related synthetic chemistry. Contrary to popular beliefs of quantum confinement and band gap broadening, our results demonstrate that the smallest NCs exhibit the most efficient transport characteristics, evidenced by the lowest activation energy (hole activation energy = 78 meV) for hole transport and the highest barrier for vacancy-mediated ion migration (ion activation energy = 370 meV). This work paves a way forward for perovskite-based efficient quantum devices, by demonstrating that moving into a strong quantum confinement regime, a superior charge transport can be facilitated, when supported by carefully tailored stoichiometry.

</details>

The inset schematics indicate the perturbed surface regions, with P denoting per- turbations that break the vertical My mirror symmetries of the NW in the surface region.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically examines pentagonal topological crystalline insulator nanowires, predicting the existence of two spatially separated helical Dirac crossings—one localized in the core and one on the outer surface. These crossings appear only when the twin planes are cationic and the bulk band structure is inverted. The findings establish these specific nanowire geometries as a promising platform for realizing distinct boundary-bound topological states.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state topology, the concept of spatially separated boundary modes (core vs. surface) is analogous to engineered quantum systems where confinement dictates distinct physical behaviors at different spatial locations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the electronic band topology of pentagonal topological crystalline insulator (TCI) nanowires, aiming to identify spatially separated helical Dirac crossings.

**Main result.** When the twin planes are cationic and bulk band inversion occurs, two distinct helical Dirac crossings—one at the core and one on the outer surface—are predicted. This establishes these structures as an accessible platform for realizing such separated topological channels.

**Method.** The research employs tight-binding (TB) calculations, supplemented by low-energy k·p theory, to model the electronic band structure of the nanowires.

**Model / system.** The system is a pentagonal IV–VI semiconductor nanowire (e.g., SnTe-class), characterized by five radial {111} twin planes meeting at the axis. The analysis compares results based on whether these twin planes are cationic or anionic.

**Key observables.** Electronic band structure, presence/absence of Dirac crossings, and wave function localization (core vs. surface).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cationic vs. anionic composition of the twin planes; nanowire thickness ($\ge 50$ nm for predictions); bulk band inversion.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations neglect axial strain from transverse deformation and use idealized core structures, which may not reflect true relaxation states.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate band structures showing the emergence of core/surface Dirac crossings (Fig. 3) and demonstrate that these crossings can arise even when considering only a single cationic twin plane shell (Fig. 4).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the theoretical framework using TB models, then analyzing the spectral consequences based on the sublattice composition of the twin planes, and finally discussing structural stability via binding energy calculations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the electronic band topology of recently fabricated pentagonal IV-VI semiconductor nanowires, which contain five radial $\{111\}$ twin planes meeting at the nanowire axis. Tight-binding calculations show that, when the bulk band structure is inverted and the twin planes in the nanowire are cationic, the spectrum contains two spatially separated helical Dirac crossings near $\overlineΓ$: one localized at the core and the other at the outer surface. When the twin planes are anionic, the corresponding spectra remain gapped. The crossings originate from the hybridization of five helical channels associated with the twin-plane edges, whose odd number leaves one Kramers pair near the nanowire axis and the other at the outer boundary. Realistic multiorbital calculations for $\mathrm{Pb}_{0.4}\mathrm{Sn}_{0.6}\mathrm{Te}$ predict well-developed core and surface modes at nanowire thicknesses of approximately 50~nm and above. These results establish pentagonal SnTe-class nanowires as an experimentally accessible realization of spatially separated helical channels bound to the axial defect and the outer boundary.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that the internal structure of charge-transfer excitons can intrinsically generate topological band structures. By modeling these excitons on a Kagome lattice derived from off-site orbitals, the authors show that breaking time-reversal symmetry induces a robust topological flat band. This establishes a general theoretical pathway for engineering exotic correlated bosonic phases in condensed matter systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of emergent topology in composite bound states, like excitons, provides an alternative route to realizing topological phases compared to engineering topology directly into single-particle bands, which is highly relevant for designing novel quantum materials and light-matter interactions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a microscopic origin for exciton topology arising from the internal structure of bound states, specifically using charge-transfer (CT) excitons where electron and hole are laterally separated.

**Main result.** The emergent multi-orbital manifold supports a topological exciton flat band upon time-reversal symmetry breaking, even when the constituent electron and hole bands are topologically trivial.

**Method.** Solving the Bethe-Salpeter equation (BSE) and analyzing the system's response to various symmetries ($	ext{T}$, $	ext{PI}$).

**Model / system.** The study uses CT excitons in a honeycomb lattice, which form an intrinsic manifold of symmetry-related off-site composite orbitals that mimic a Kagome lattice structure.

**Key observables.** Exciton band structure, topological nature (Chern numbers) of the resulting flat band, and exciton quantum geometry (Berry curvature).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hopping amplitudes ($t_c, t_v$), potential strength ($\Delta$), mass asymmetry ($m_e^*/m_h^*$), and symmetry breaking parameters ($\eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the exciton structure arising from off-site orbitals rather than constituent bands; approximations include neglecting exchange interaction initially.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic electron-hole distributions, the resulting Kagome lattice of CT excitons, and the calculated band dispersions under various symmetry breaking conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical problem (CT exciton topology) to deriving an effective Hamiltonian on a Kagome manifold. It then uses continuum models and BSE calculations to analyze how $	ext{T}$ and $	ext{PI}$ symmetry breaking dictates the topological properties of the resulting flat band.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Excitons possess internal structure absent from single-particle Bloch particles, allowing their band topology to emerge from the bound-state structure rather than being inherited from their constituents. This raises the question of how the internal structure of a bound state can provide a microscopic origin of exciton topology. Here we show that the real-space embedding of charge-transfer excitons can generate an intrinsic manifold of symmetry-related off-site composite orbitals whose coupling supports topological exciton bands. Lateral electron-hole separation embeds the localized exciton on the bond connecting its constituent sites rather than on either site. We demonstrate this mechanism in a honeycomb lattice, where three bond-centered charge-transfer exciton orbitals form a Kagome lattice. By solving the Bethe-Salpeter equation, we show that this emergent multi-orbital manifold supports a topological exciton flat band upon time-reversal symmetry breaking, even when the electron and hole bands are topologically trivial. The resulting band exhibits nearly uniformly distributed quantum geometry, favorable for interaction-driven bosonic states. Our results establish a general route toward topological bands of localized composite bound states and unconventional strongly correlated bosonic phases.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that atomic preplating of MCM-41 nanopores with argon can systematically engineer the pore's internal surface chemistry. By combining extensive simulation techniques (GCMC, MD) with experimental data analysis, the authors derive a smooth, quantifiable confinement potential for helium atoms. This provides crucial microscopic input for theoretical studies of quantum fluids in porous media.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, atomistically derived microscopic potential for quantum fluids in confined geometries, directly validating continuum models used in advanced quantum simulations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand how modifying mesopore interfaces via preplating affects the microscopic confinement potential experienced by adsorbed quantum fluids like helium.

**Main result.** Preplating MCM-41 with argon modifies the pore interface, resulting in a smooth radial confinement potential that can be accurately described by continuum models for confined helium.

**Method.** The research combines grand-canonical Monte Carlo simulations of argon adsorption, low-temperature molecular dynamics, and helium test-particle insertion calculations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is MCM-41 mesoporous silica nanopores. Interactions are modeled using Lennard-Jones potentials, and the confinement potential for He is derived by simulating Ar adsorption first.

**Key observables.** Helium adsorption minimum location (annular region), radial confinement potential $U(r)$, argon adsorption isotherms, and structure factors $S(q)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Simulation temperatures ($90 	ext{ K}$, $4 	ext{ K}$), chemical potentials ($\mu$), pore radius ($	ext{R}_{	ext{pore}} = 20 \mathring{	ext{A}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the argon preplating screens enough roughness to allow the confinement potential to be approximated by a smooth, continuum cylindrical model.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare simulated and experimental adsorption isotherms for argon; other figures detail structural profiles (density/pair-correlation) and derivatives used to determine monolayer completion points.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a progression from characterizing the Ar adsorption process (GCMC, structure factor), determining interface modification details (occupancy curves, $\mu_{	ext{mono}}$), and finally applying these results to calculate and validate the He confinement potential using test-particle insertion and Gaussian Process regression.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Atomic-scale modification of mesopore interfaces provides a route to tune the confinement experienced by adsorbed fluids, but how a specific interface preparation translates into the resulting microscopic confinement potential remains unclear. Here, we show that preplating MCM-41 with an argon monolayer modifies the effective pore interface by occupying strongly attractive regions of the heterogeneous silica surface and screening its atomic-scale corrugation. Grand-canonical Monte Carlo simulations of argon adsorption, low-temperature molecular dynamics, and helium test-particle insertion are combined with adsorption isotherms and neutron-scattering measurements to characterize the preplated pore at the atomic scale. Helium test-particle insertion calculations show that the modified interface shifts the helium adsorption minimum to an annular region inside the pore and produces a confinement landscape dominated by a smooth radial component. The resulting radial confinement potential can be described by a continuum cylindrical model, providing microscopic support for the effective potential used in earlier quantum Monte Carlo studies. Residual corrugation persists over multiple spatial scales and is accurately captured by a Gaussian process surrogate. These results demonstrate how atomic preplating can tailor nanopore confinement and provide an experimentally constrained microscopic potential for predictive studies of confined quantum fluids.

</details>
